# Dossier 1: The perennial philosophy movement

Prepared 2026-10-07 for Alex's Part two ("Spiritual"). Built only from the research files listed in section 1. Nothing in the repository was touched.

The question behind every part of this dossier: how do these people look at the subject (the one and the many, the veil, lives, karma, return, stages, letting go), and how do they compare different religions to show that they speak of the same thing? That means which traditions they set side by side, which terms they equate, which texts they cite, which method they use, and how they explain the differences.

## Main findings

Details and tiers are in the sections named. Nothing below is confirmed against a page or a book (see 1.1).

1. The perennial philosophy movement is a family of schools and books, not one doctrine. The files count at least five strands, and the strands disagree with each other on rebirth, on method, on Theosophy and on whether history rises or falls (2.3, 12.3).
2. The name is older than the movement. Steuco used it in 1540 and Leibniz in 1714. Huxley's Foreword wrongly says Leibniz coined it (2.1, 5.1).
3. The strict core is the Traditionalist school: Guenon from 1921, Coomaraswamy, Schuon from 1948, then a second generation. Their rule is that outer forms differ and the inner teaching agrees. They also require one real tradition, a lineage and no mixing, so they would not accept Alex's method of collecting witnesses (3.6).
4. On lives the movement splits. Guenon and, as far as the files can say, Schuon reject reincarnation in the Western sense. Coomaraswamy says the one Self is the only transmigrant. Theosophy teaches repeated lives. The Traditionalists are therefore weak witnesses for "my soul comes back" and good witnesses for the one whole, the veil, why a whole would separate, letting go and an ethic that follows from identity (11.3).
5. Huxley is one anthologist in the middle of a long line. The facts that support Alex's view are listed in 5.7. "Best" is a judgement that no file can settle, so it should read as Alex's opinion with reasons (5.7).
6. Every historical comparer had a home team and a top. Alex has no team, and his top is his axiom (2.2, 12.3).
7. Scholars contest the strong forms of the claim (same experience, one source, all traditions say the same) and tolerate careful forms (a family of ideas that recur, with the respect of comparison stated). Alex's modest claim sits in the second group (13).
8. Best witness by job, from the files' leads (all unchecked): the one and why separation, Guenon (Universal Possibility), Schuon (Absolute, Infinite, Maya), Smith; who carries over, Coomaraswamy on the one transmigrant and Gaudapada; the veil, Shankara's superimposition and Vasubandhu's three natures; the fingers, Schopenhauer, Gita 13.28, Wang Yangming, Marcus Aurelius 8.34, Luria's souls as limbs of Adam; stages, Underhill, Teresa, Smith; letting go, Coomaraswamy's self-naughting, Schuon's three-part way, James (the detail is in sections 3, 9, 10 and 11).

## 1. How to read this dossier

### 1.1 Evidence status

In every verification pass behind these files the web search tool refused all calls ("this turn's web search budget is used up", limit 200 per turn, shared by every agent) and the fetch tool was blocked by the network egress proxy. Only the first passes of P01, P02 and P03 had working search, and they saw search summaries, not pages or books. So nothing in this dossier has been confirmed against a page or a book in any verification pass. Where two files agree from memory, that is the same kind of evidence twice, and no tier was raised for it. Every date, title, locator, attribution and wording must be opened at its source before anything goes public. Section 15 lists what to check first.

### 1.2 Tier tags

The tags are carried over from the files and none was raised.

- [V] appears only on items in P03 (Coomaraswamy). It means a first-pass researcher reported that a search result showed the wording or fact. No one re-saw it. In this dossier treat [V] as the same strength as [C].
- [C] carried: a first-pass researcher reported a search result (mostly P01, P02, P03). Not re-seen by anyone.
- [S] a search result supported the gist; wording or locator from memory.
- [M] memory, confident, not search-checked. [M-] memory, less than confident.
- [U] memory, unsure. Not for the essay until checked. Where a [U] item is useful to Alex's understanding it is kept and flagged. Many low-value [U] items were dropped.

Where files disagree, both readings are given and the lower tier stands.

### 1.3 Quotation policy

Exact sentences are given in quotation marks only for the few items tagged [V] in P03, and even those come from search summaries and must be checked in the book. Every other wording is paraphrased or described. Titles, technical terms and short formulas (for example tat tvam asi, philosophia perennis) appear as terms. Quotation marks elsewhere only name a popular wording so that it can be recognised or avoided (mostly in section 14) or mark a short title or formula. They never mean a cleared quotation. Section 15 names the lines Alex might want to quote and says where to find them.

### 1.4 Scope and Alex's correction

Alex's correction, relayed with the task: the perennial philosophy movement is much bigger than Huxley, plenty of other books belong to it, and Huxley is not even the best of them. This dossier treats Huxley as one anthologist among many. Per Alex, the movement itself ranks the Traditionalist school (Guenon, Coomaraswamy, Schuon and their circle) as foundational (P03). The files agree that Huxley is not the first, not the strictest and not the largest, and they agree that "best" is a judgement: no source in the files ranks the books, and the right book depends on the job. Section 5.7 sets out the facts that support Alex's view and marks what stays his opinion.

Theosophy is not this dossier's subject. It appears only where it touches the perennial movement (2.4, 11.3, 12).

### 1.5 File key

File ids appear in brackets at the end of paragraphs so the author can trace a claim. In the files, "the colleague" means the first-pass researcher who wrote the original slice, and "the verification pass" or "the checker" means the later reader who corrected it from memory.

P01 history of the idea. P02 Guenon. P03 Coomaraswamy. P04 Schuon on method. P05 Schuon on metaphysics. P06 second generation. P07 anthologies. P08 Huxley. P09 Huston Smith. P10 Wilber and the transpersonal line. P11 comparers of mysticism. P12 academic debate. P13 non-Western precedents. P14 the One. P15 veil and way. P16 lives and karma. P17 ethic of unity. P18 stage maps. G1-3 Traditionalist primary sentences. G1-4 perennial and experience classics. G1-7 Kashmir Shaivism and other Indian systems. G2-3 technical theories of illusion. S3 catalogue of comparison methods. S5 why the religions differ.

## 2. What the movement is, and its history

### 2.1 One name, three meanings, three claims

The phrase. Agostino Steuco published De perenni philosophia libri X in 1540 (Lyon, Gryphius [M]) [C]. Leibniz used the phrase in a letter to Nicolas Remond dated 26 August 1714 [C: several results agree on date and addressee]; some give 1715 [U]. Charles B. Schmitt showed in 1966 that the phrase is Steuco's, in "Perennial Philosophy: From Agostino Steuco to Leibniz", Journal of the History of Ideas 27, pp. 505 to 532 [C, M]. Amberger (Lexicon Philosophicum 7, 2019) argues that Leibniz took Steuco's term and changed it [C]. Huxley's Foreword credits Leibniz with coining the phrase. That is an error, and it is repeated on many web pages [M] (P01, P07, P08, G1-4).

Other things that share the name. In Catholic neo-Thomist usage, philosophia perennis names the philosophy of Aquinas (Leo XIII, Aeterni Patris, 1879; Maritain, Gilson) and is not the same movement [M]; P01 reports that neo-Thomists revived the phrase early in the twentieth century for their own tradition [C]. "Traditionalism" in the press often means Julius Evola or Alexander Dugin, whose politics are far right; the Guenon and Schuon line is a different current, and Sedgwick covers all of them [M] (P06).

Three claims that scholars keep apart (P12) [M]. The first is about experience: mystics in every tradition have had the same experience and describe it in different words (Stace; James in part; Forman in a narrow form). The second is about doctrine: the inner teachings of the great traditions say the same things about reality, the self and the end of life (Guenon, Coomaraswamy, Schuon, Nasr, Lings, Burckhardt, Smith; the anthologists Perry and Huxley). The third is about origin: it all descends from one primordial source (the prisca theologia, Guenon's primordial tradition, Blavatsky's Wisdom Religion). Most quarrels are about which claim is meant. The Traditionalist form of the second claim does not rest on mystical experience at all (see 13.1).

Alex's claim, as the files read it, is a modest form of the second: examples that fit, witnesses and not authorities, the same ideas in different words, held as a family resemblance (P12, S3).

### 2.2 From the ancient seeds to the Parliament of 1893

The files (mostly P01) describe four stages, with a fifth, the twentieth century, in 2.3.

The ancient seeds. Early Christian and Platonist writers already held that Greek wisdom borrowed from Moses or that the true religion existed before Christ.
- Numenius (second half of the second century [M]), quoted by Clement of Alexandria, Stromata I.22.150.4, said in effect that Plato is Moses speaking Attic Greek (English wording varies) [C]. A search result attached this line to Pico; it belongs to Numenius via Clement, and the Pico attachment is doubtful [U].
- Augustine, Retractationes (c. 427): the thing now called the Christian religion existed among the ancients and never lacked from the beginning of the human race [S]; locator Retr. I.13.3 from memory [S]. The sentence then puts Christ at the turning point, which is the home-team move [M].
- Suhrawardi (d. 1191) identified the wisdom of the Zoroastrian sages with Hermes, Pythagoras and Plato, and is called in modern writing a reviver of perennial wisdom [C].
- From memory only [M]: Plutarch, De Iside et Osiride 67 (about 120 CE), says the gods are not different for different peoples; Justin Martyr speaks of the seed of the Logos in the philosophers; Symmachus, Relatio 3 (384 CE), says so great a mystery cannot be reached by one road.

Stage one, the lineage claim: one wisdom handed down, all later religions echo or corrupt it. The proof is genealogy and the method is concordance.
- Plethon, Florence 1438 to 1439. He taught Plato and Zoroaster to the Florentines and described a golden chain from Zoroaster to Plotinus, Porphyry and Iamblichus. His Laws was condemned by Gennadios [C]. That he moved Cosimo de' Medici to found the Platonic Academy may be legend [M]. Whether Plethon taught rebirth is [U].
- Ficino, 1463 to 1492. Translation of the Corpus Hermeticum (the Pimander) in 1463, first printed at Treviso in 1471 [C]. The Argumentum makes Hermes the first author of theology and builds one ancient theology from six theologians: Mercurius, Orpheus, Aglaophemus, Pythagoras, Philolaus, Plato [C for the gist; the six names M]. He called his tradition pia philosophia and Christianity docta religio [C]. De christiana religione (written 1474, printed 1476 [M]) holds that belief is universal while salvation lies in Christ [C]. He rejected Averroes' single intellect (Theologia Platonica book 15; written 1469 to 1474, printed 1482 [M]) because he wanted every soul to be a lasting individual [C]. Contrast with Alex, who proposes one whole [M].
- Pico della Mirandola, 1486 to 1489. The Conclusiones (900 theses from Greek, Hebrew, Arabic and Latin writers) and the Oration later called "On the Dignity of Man" [C]. His friends called him Princeps Concordiae [C]. He thought Plato and Aristotle used different words for the same ideas, and that the Hebrew and Hermetic sources gave the same view of God as the Old Testament in other words [C]. A papal commission condemned thirteen theses in 1487; the Apologia is 1487 (not 1489); the pope condemned the whole book by a bull of August 1487; Pico was briefly arrested in France in 1488 [M]. One condemned point was that it is more reasonable to believe Origen saved than damned [M]. Correction: Origen was not "excommunicated for teaching reincarnation". He was condemned long after his death (543 and 553) for the pre-existence of souls and universal restoration, and he argued against transmigration into other bodies [M] (P01, P06, P16).
- Steuco, 1540. Vatican librarian from 1538, bishop of Kisamos on Crete, Counter-Reformation polemicist, c. 1497 to 1548 [C]. His work claims one principle of all things and one knowledge of it among all peoples, given early, kept in Hebrew scripture and echoed by Chaldean, Egyptian, Greek and Roman sages [C; English rendering from encyclopedia summaries, no Latin seen]. He found the Trinity, creation, immortality of the soul and the end and renewal of the world shared [C]. He worked from Ficino and Pico [C]. His meaning was a standing deposit of revealed truth guarded by the Church [C for the polemic, S for the guardian reading]. The idea is older than the phrase [M] (G1-4).

Stage two, the peace claim: sameness as a reason for tolerance.
- Nicholas of Cusa, De pace fidei, 1453, after the fall of Constantinople. A vision of a heavenly council in which wise men of many nations agree on one religion in a variety of rites; English by Jasper Hopkins, 1990 [C]. The Latin fragment una religio in rituum varietate is safe; the word order and verb around it are [U]. The Word (Logos) is the bridge, and a scholar reads him as saying the Gospels and the Qur'an both affirm Jesus as the Word [C]. His metaphysics is the One enfolded and unfolded (complicatio and explicatio; the world as the contracted maximum, De docta ignorantia book 2) [C]. Scholars read his "one religion" both as a rational universal religion and as Christianity in disguise; he also wrote the hard attack Cribratio Alkorani (1461) and took part in crusade planning [C]. The participant list of the council is [M]. P01 calls this the earliest Western text that does what Part two does, with the One unfolding into many as the axiom in fifteenth century Latin [M, the file's reading].
- Dara Shikoh, 1650s. A Mughal prince and Sufi who argued that Sufism and Upanishadic Vedanta are one knowledge in two vocabularies. Majma al-Bahrayn ("Mingling of the Two Oceans", c. 1655; the title comes from Qur'an 18:60, where Moses reaches the meeting of the two seas and finds one who has knowledge from God, whom the tradition identifies as Khidr [M]) has twenty or twenty-two short sections [C]. Sirr-i Akbar ("the Great Secret", 1657) put fifty-two Upanishads into Persian [C]. He held that apart from the manner of exposition there was no essential difference between Islam and Hinduism [C, paraphrase]. He sets terms side by side: the five elements against the Hindu five (panch bhut); Brahma, Vishnu and Mahesh against the angels Jibril, Mika'il and Israfil [C]; the Upanishads as the hidden book of the Qur'an (kitab al-maknun) [C]. His jamal and jalal against the three gunas is incomplete as summarised [U]. His Persian text reached Anquetil-Duperron in 1775, through Jean-Baptiste Gentil [C, M], and became the Latin Oupnek'hat of 1801 to 1802, printed at Strasbourg [C, M]. He was executed in 1659 [M]. English: Mahfuz-ul-Haq (1929) [M-]; study: Supriya Gandhi, The Emperor Who Never Was (2020), who reads his choices as pushing Advaita and Sufi compatibility [C]. P01 calls him the first systematic term-by-term comparer, and notes that his Vedanta came out in Sufi words (P01, P13, G1-4).

Stage three, the lineage collapses.
- Isaac Casaubon showed in 1614 that the Corpus Hermeticum was late antique and not Mosaic-age Egyptian [C]. Frances Yates (1964) called 1614 a watershed [C; her wording and page U]. D. P. Walker, The Ancient Theology (1972), traces the claim that pagan wisdom derived from Moses [C]. Ralph Cudworth (1678) kept the ancient theology alive among the Cambridge Platonists; Mosheim's Latin translation (1733) took it to Germany [C].
- Leibniz, 1714. The truth is spread across all sects and can be panned out. The letter says one could draw the gold from the dross (the French may say mud, [U]), the diamond from its mine, the light from the shadows, and that this would be a kind of perennial philosophy (perennis quaedam Philosophia) [C; English translator not shown; Rutherford's translation, RemondVII1714.pdf on his UCSD page, unread by anyone]. He praised the "Orientals" [C; place in the letter unknown]. For Amberger, Leibniz made it a conditional sifting task and not a deposit: primordial truths are "seeds of truth" that must grow, so the idea implies progress [C]. P01 judges Leibniz the best fit for Alex's stance: he does not say every school is right, he says there is gold in each [the file's reading].

Stage four, philology and experience.
- Sir William Jones (Asiatic Society, 1784) compared Hindu and Greek gods inside the biblical frame of Noah's descendants [C]. His sentence about a common source "which perhaps no longer exists" is about the languages Sanskrit, Greek and Latin (Third Anniversary Discourse, 1786), not about religions; the comparison of the gods is a separate essay, written 1784 and printed 1788 [M].
- Anquetil-Duperron's Oupnek'hat reached Schopenhauer [C]. Schopenhauer said it was the solace of his life and would be of his death (Parerga II, section 184) [C]; the longer English form circulating online has no named translator [U]. He met it through Friedrich Majer in 1814 in Dresden [M] (G1-4 says Weimar, 1813 to 1814 [M]; files disagree). He used maya, tat tvam asi and the principle of individuation, called Nirvana extinction, cited Eckhart by the 1859 edition, wrote that true Christianity agrees with Buddhism and Brahmanism in ultimate aim (locator unknown), and took up rebirth as palingenesis in 1844 [C]. His conclusion was denial of the will, which is not return [C, M].
- Lessing. The ring parable (Nathan the Wise, 1779, Act III, scene 7): three identical rings, the true one cannot be proved, so each son should act as if his were true and prove it by love and goodness [C]. The Education of the Human Race (1777 for the first 53 paragraphs, 1780 for all 100) ends on rebirth in paragraphs 93 to 100 [C]: why should an individual not have existed more than once, and why should I not come back as often as I can acquire fresh knowledge. Paragraph numbers are off by one or so in the sources [U]; open Robertson (1858) before giving a number. P01 calls Lessing the one thinker in this stage who ties the sameness of religions to lives as school, with an honest position that matches Alex's: no proof, only examples that fit [the file's reading].
- Emerson, The Over-Soul (Essays, First Series, 1841). We live in pieces, in division, in succession; within each person is the soul of the whole [C for the gist; wording and punctuation open]. The sentence that follows says the seer and the spectacle, subject and object, are one, and states clause one more directly [M] (G1-4). Emerson read the Gita and Vishnu Purana in earnest only from 1845, so the Over-Soul does not lean on them; his sources were Plato, Plutarch, Plotinus (via Thomas Taylor), Swedenborg and the Quaker inner light [C, M]. Compensation (same volume) puts karma without Sanskrit: cause and effect cannot be severed, the thief steals from himself [M]. Emerson is not a link in the chain Dara, Anquetil, Schopenhauer, Deussen; he is an independent witness who arrived by another road (G1-4) [M]. "Over-Soul" is Emerson's coinage and not Blavatsky's [M].
- Max Muller: Introduction to the Science of Religion (1873) and the Sacred Books of the East (1879 to 1910) [C]. His line that he who knows one religion knows none echoes Goethe on languages [M]. His Gifford Lectures of 1892, Theosophy or Psychological Religion (book 1893), treat the oneness of God and soul as the end of religion while attacking the Theosophical movement as unhistorical; his "theosophy" there is his own term for an inner religion [C, M]. Olcott met him in 1888 [C]. He never visited India [C].
- Ramakrishna and the Parliament of 1893. Ramakrishna: every path leads to the same goal; as many faiths, so many paths (yato mat tato path) [C]. His practice of Islam and Christianity comes from disciples' accounts [M] (c. 1866 and 1874, each a few days [U]). The Parliament opened 11 September 1893 at the Art Institute of Chicago, organised by the World's Congress Auxiliary under Charles C. Bonney with John Henry Barrows chairing the General Committee [M]. Its aims included making the golden rule the basis of union [C; the word "all" in "against all irreligion" is [U]]. Vivekananda, opening address: streams from different sources all mingle in the sea, and Gita 4.11; he said not only toleration but acceptance of all religions as true [C]. Closing address, 27 September: the Christian is not to become a Hindu, but each must take in the spirit of the others and keep his individuality and grow by his own law [C]. The identification of his hymn with the Shivamahimna stotra is the editors' [M-]. Dharmapala spoke on "The World's Debt to Buddhism" (he was a Theosophist) and Besant and Judge spoke for Theosophy [C]. Critics: Barrows' "universal" was a larger Christianity, and the Parliament had little room for African American and Native American voices [C].

How the comparers worked (P01 §4) [the file's synthesis]. Genealogy (Plethon, Ficino, Steuco, Jones): agreement is inheritance, differences are decay. Concordance by translating terms (Pico, Dara): the risk is that the comparer decides which word matches which. Council or dialogue (Cusa, Lessing, the Parliament): the agreement is in the script. Philology and history (Jones, Muller, Anquetil): sameness becomes a finding about language and diffusion. Inner experience (Ramakrishna, Vivekananda, Emerson).

How they explained differences (P01) [the file's synthesis]: the rites differ and the faith is one (Cusa); the words differ and the idea is one (Pico, Dara); the stage of education differs (Lessing, Leibniz); corruption, so older is purer (Plethon, Steuco); different paths up one mountain (Ramakrishna, Vivekananda); the truth is in the practice and cannot be shown in words (Lessing's ring). Each is a claim by the comparer and not by the tradition.

Three explanations of the agreement itself keep returning: common revelation, common transmission, common experience (P01). Alex proposes a fourth, common logic: the agreement follows from one axiom [M, the file's reading]. P01 notes that the first two have lost most of their historical support (Casaubon, Yates, Walker) and that Alex's version is testable, since a tradition that contradicts the axiom would count against him.

Home teams. Ficino, Cusa (on one reading) and Steuco put Christianity at the top; Dara read Vedanta in Sufi words; Vivekananda ranks Advaita highest [C, M] (P01, P13). Alex is not religious and has no team.

### 2.3 The twentieth-century movement and its strands

P06 section 1A counts at least five strands that the essay should not merge [M].
1. The Traditionalists (Guenon, Coomaraswamy, Schuon and the second generation): doctrine first, a living tradition required, hostile to Theosophy and to reincarnation in the Western sense.
2. The popular perennialism of Huxley's circle (the Vedanta Society of Southern California, Gerald Heard, Isherwood): anthology and commentary, Vedanta lens.
3. The academic common-core line (Underhill, Otto, Stace, later Wilber's stage map).
4. The Gurdjieff and transpersonal fringe (Needleman; Wilber).
5. The critics inside the study of religion (Zaehner, Katz, Prothero, J. Z. Smith, King, Sedgwick as historian).
Theosophy is a separate family: it can be counted in the same umbrella (its "Wisdom Religion" is a primordial-source claim), yet the Traditionalists fought it by name, so the fault line runs inside the umbrella (P12).

A timeline of the main books, with years as the files give them (all [M] unless marked; check each before printing):

| Year | Work |
|---|---|
| 1901 | Bucke, Cosmic Consciousness |
| 1902 | James, The Varieties of Religious Experience |
| 1911 | Underhill, Mysticism |
| 1921 | Guenon, Introduction generale a l'etude des doctrines hindoues (English 1945, Luzac [M]) |
| 1925 | Guenon, L'Homme et son devenir selon le Vedanta |
| 1926 | Otto, West-oestliche Mystik (English 1932) |
| 1927 | Guenon, La Crise du monde moderne |
| 1932 | Guenon, Les Etats multiples de l'etre |
| 1943 | Coomaraswamy, Hinduism and Buddhism |
| 1944 | Coomaraswamy, On the One and Only Transmigrant (JAOS Supplement 3) |
| 1945 | Huxley, The Perennial Philosophy; Guenon, Le Regne de la quantite |
| 1948 | Schuon, De l'unite transcendante des religions (English 1953; revised 1975) |
| 1950 | Watts, The Supreme Identity |
| 1957 | Zaehner, Mysticism Sacred and Profane |
| 1958 | Smith, The Religions of Man (revised 1991 as The World's Religions) |
| 1960 | Stace, Mysticism and Philosophy; The Teachings of the Mystics |
| 1971 | Perry, A Treasury of Traditional Wisdom |
| 1976 | Smith, Forgotten Truth |
| 1978 | Katz (ed.), Mysticism and Philosophical Analysis |
| 1980 | Wilber, The Atman Project |
| 1981 | Nasr, Knowledge and the Sacred |
| 1991 | Wilson (ed.), World Scripture |
| 2004 | Sedgwick, Against the Modern World |
| 2006 | Shah-Kazemi, Paths to Transcendence |

The movement's own phrases differ by house: "perennial philosophy" (Steuco, Leibniz, Huxley), "sophia perennis" and "religio perennis" (Schuon, who prefers wisdom to philosophy), "primordial tradition" (Guenon's term, used by Smith in the 1976 subtitle), "philosophia perennis et universalis" (Coomaraswamy). They name the same idea in different houses and should not be treated as three movements or as one author's word [M] (P04, P09).

### 2.4 The perennial movement and Theosophy: two movements that disliked each other

Alex leans on both as the two movements that compared religions systematically. The files insist the essay say they do not agree with each other and that he does not claim either as a team.

- Guenon wrote a book-length attack, Le Theosophisme, histoire d'une pseudo-religion (1921), and L'Erreur spirite (1923) [C, M]. He still shared Theosophy's central idea that one esoteric teaching underlies all religions and differed over which lineage is genuine (Smoley, Quest 2010) [C]. Whether Schuon named Theosophy or Blavatsky is unchecked [U]; attribute the attack to the school and to Guenon by name (P05, G1-3).
- Coomaraswamy joined the Theosophical Society in 1907 and left [C, V in P03]; his Traditionalist writing followed his correspondence with Guenon in the 1930s [V]. The Pasadena Theosophical Forum (March 1944; the Point Loma lineage, not Adyar) reviewed Hinduism and Buddhism as showing Hinduism to be one facet of the Philosophia Perennis [V, M]. Eliade's "Some Notes on Theosophia Perennis" (History of Religions 19, 1979) uses theosophia in the Corbin and Boehme sense of divine wisdom and is not a bridge to the Theosophical Society [M].
- Quest Books, the imprint of the Theosophical Publishing House, published Wilber's first books and reissued Schuon's Transcendent Unity [M, U for the Schuon edition]. Read it as a small irony, no more (P04, P10).
- Julie Chajes shows that Blavatsky taught two doctrines of reincarnation: no earthly return in Isis Unveiled (1877) and earthly rebirth of the individuality in The Secret Doctrine (1888) [C]. The visible change is usually placed around 1881 to 1882 [M]. This partly vindicates Guenon on Theosophy [the file's reading] and means the draft sentence naming Isis Unveiled as a main route for karma and reincarnation is too wide for Isis (P02, P12, S3).
- Muller attacked "esoteric Buddhism" and the Theosophical movement (2.2). Coleman (1895) traced much of the Secret Doctrine to printed books [M] (P07, S3).
- The Secret Doctrine, Proem, third fundamental proposition is the nearest nineteenth century statement of Alex's axiom, according to several files: the fundamental identity of all souls with the Universal Over-Soul, and the obligatory pilgrimage of every soul through the cycle of incarnation under cyclic and karmic law [M for the gist; wording recalled in several files, U, not for quotation; public domain, so open the Proem]. It belongs to another dossier (P13, P14, P16, P17, S3).

## 3. The Traditionalist school: Guenon, Coomaraswamy, Schuon

### 3.1 What the three share

Who counts. Rene Guenon (1886 to 1951) is the first. Ananda K. Coomaraswamy (1877 to 1947) was nine years older than Guenon, already an established scholar, and came to share his positions in the 1930s [M]. Frithjof Schuon (1907 to 1998) was the younger disciple, the most influential after Guenon, with the largest body of books and the largest circle [M]. "The most learned of the three" is an admirer's judgement and not a fact (P03). Mark Sedgwick, Against the Modern World: Traditionalism and the Secret Intellectual History of the Twentieth Century (Oxford University Press, 2004), is the standard history [M] (P02, P03, P04, P05).

Shared frame [M, with the details in 3.2 to 3.4]:
- One primordial Tradition behind all genuine religions. The metaphysical core is the same everywhere and the outer forms differ because they are adapted to peoples and ages.
- Each tradition has an outer face (exoterism: dogma, law, ritual, collective salvation) and an inner face (esoterism: metaphysical doctrine and a method of realization). Only the inner face carries the metaphysics.
- Metaphysics is not philosophy or theology. It is intellectual intuition, with the Intellect (nous, intellectus, buddhi, the eye of the heart) as the faculty that knows the Real directly.
- Advaita Vedanta, Sufism and Neoplatonic and scholastic metaphysics are the base, and Advaita is read as the clearest statement.
- A seeker needs one regular tradition, with a lineage, rites and a teacher. Mixing forms or building a private synthesis is dilettantism.
- They reject Theosophy, spiritism and reincarnation in the Western sense, they read history as decline (the Kali Yuga) and they reject modern evolutionism and progress.
- Schuon prefers sophia perennis ("perennial wisdom") to philosophia perennis, since modern philosophy is rational speculation; religio perennis is the primordial inner religion present in every orthodox form (P04). Coomaraswamy used philosophia perennis et universalis (P04).

### 3.2 Rene Guenon

Life [P02]. In 1905 he left the preparation for an elite school for health reasons and moved into Papus's circles (Martinist Order) [C]. He joined the Gnostic Church of Fabre des Essarts, ran the review La Gnose on Hindu, Taoist and Sufi themes, and entered the Thebah lodge of the Grande Loge de France [C]. Ivan Agueli brought him into the Shadhili Sufi order; results give 1910, 1911 and 1912 [C, conflicting; 1912 is usual, M]. He took the name Abd al-Wahid Yahya, probably at that initiation and not on arrival in Cairo [U]. He taught philosophy at Setif in 1917 [C], moved to Cairo in 1930, and died there on 7 January 1951 [C]. Sedgwick notes that he is not known ever to have recommended anyone to become a Hindu, whereas he introduced many to Islam [C, as reported in a result]. Sylvain Levi, an Indologist, rejected the 1921 Introduction as a thesis [C].

Books and dates [C unless marked]. Introduction generale a l'etude des doctrines hindoues (1921); Le Theosophisme, histoire d'une pseudo-religion (1921, from articles in a review whose name is U; English only in 2003); L'Erreur spirite (1923; English 2004 [M]); Orient et Occident (1924); L'Esoterisme de Dante (1925); L'Homme et son devenir selon le Vedanta (1925; one result says 1927, [C, conflicting]); La Crise du monde moderne (1927); Le Symbolisme de la Croix (1931); Les Etats multiples de l'etre (1932); Le Regne de la quantite et les signes des temps (1945); Apercus sur l'initiation (1946, from articles of about 1932 to 1938 [U for the range]). Studies in Hinduism and Insights into Islamic Esoterism and Taoism are posthumous collections of articles [M]. English translations came from Luzac in the 1940s [M]: Introduction (tr. Marco Pallis, 1945), Man and His Becoming (tr. Richard C. Nicholson, 1945), Crisis of the Modern World (tr. Pallis and Nicholson, 1942) [M]; Multiple States of the Being (tr. Joscelyn Godwin, Larson, 1984) [M]. Cite from the copy in hand (P02, G1-3).

Core doctrines.
- The primordial Tradition. All regular traditions descend from it, agree in principle and differ in form; Traditionalism posits a metaphysical unitary source called Tradition as the basis of the major religions in their orthodox forms [C]. Hindu doctrine comes first as the oldest and most complete [C]; then Sufi, Taoist, Christian, Kabbalistic, Masonic. This is the closest thing to Alex's sentence that the traditions describe the same principles in different words. Guenon adds two claims Alex should not take over: that the agreement comes from one historical source, and that only lineages with regular transmission count (P02).
- Exoterism and esoterism. A tradition has an outer, religious, theological side open to everyone, and an inner, initiatic, metaphysical side. Chapters of the Introduction include Tradition and Religion; Relations between Metaphysics and Theology; Metaphysical Thought versus Philosophical Thought; Esoterism and Exoterism; Metaphysical Realization [C, bookseller listing]. A line widely quoted from the Introduction, that metaphysics opens a limitless vista of possibilities and must not lose sight of the inexpressible, has an unsafe locator because quote sites misplace such lines [C; book U]. Cite the idea, not a sentence (G1-3). The distinction is applied to Hinduism, Islam (Sufism), China (Taoism inner, Confucianism outer), Judaism (Kabbalah) and Christianity [C]. This is the twentieth-century source of the public face and inner face idea in Part two, and the reason Guenon would say a religion's popular teaching may not carry the doctrine.
- Orthodoxy and initiation. Realization needs a regular initiatic line: someone qualified, a rite, a chain back to the origin, then personal work [C; the triad "qualification, transmission, work" M]. Mysticism without that chain is a different, lesser thing. Western survivals are Masonry and compagnonnage; Sufi orders and Hindu lineages are the live ones [S]. Wikipedia's statement, recorded in P02, is that Traditionalists differ from other perennialists such as Huxley in their anti-modernism and their insistence on esoteric initiation, in which Huxley had no interest [C].
- The Infinite as Universal Possibility (Les Etats multiples de l'etre, 1932, with The Symbolism of the Cross and Man and His Becoming as a trilogy [C]). The Infinite cannot be limited, since a limit on possibility would be an impossibility. Being (manifested existence) is the first determination and is not the Infinite. The human state is one of indefinitely many states of the total being. Early chapters include Infinity and Possibility; Possibles and Compossibles; Being and Non-Being; Relationships of Unity and Multiplicity [C, order U]. Brahma beyond Ishvara is matched to his pair Non-Being and Being [M]. The step "so manifestation must be included in the Infinite" is [M]. This answers the question the axiom invites, why a whole would experience separation at all: nothing real can be excluded from the Infinite, so manifestation is included. It gives a possibility and no purpose (P02, G1-3, P14).
- The Self and the individual. Atma is never individualised; the "I" of ordinary life is a modality of one state of a larger being. Liberation, even in this life (jivan-mukti), is the realization of an identity that was always there, with all separateness ceasing (Man and His Becoming; publisher text [C]). A study summarised in a result says he distinguishes Self (Soi) from Ego (Moi), and Personality from Individuality [S]. In his idiom nothing returns, because the Self never left; this is a careful form of Alex's third clause and sits well with the picture of one hand and its fingers (P02, G1-3).
- Individualism and the cycle. The modern West is an anomaly because it denies any principle higher than the individual (Crisis of the Modern World, 1927, chapter Individualism; the lead-in clause is U, so quote only from the printed book) [C, U]. The Kali Yuga makes things harden. Guenon's own figure, that the Kali Yuga has lasted more than six thousand years, is his own; the standard Puranic reckoning has it lasting 432,000 years from 3102 BCE [M], so do not write that the Hindu tradition says six thousand. Hesiod has five ages (gold, silver, bronze, heroes, iron); the four-age sequence is Ovid's [M]. This is a ready account of why separateness feels so real, but his cycles describe the decline of a whole age and run opposite to the personal ascent of Alex's arrow. His "time over space" and Alex's speed and space picture share only words; do not link them (P02).

Reincarnation, what he rejected [P02, G1-3].
- Claim [C]: reincarnation in the Western sense, the same individual returning to the earthly human state, is not a doctrine of any traditional teaching. The traditional doctrine is transmigration, which means a change of state, the passage of the being to other states of existence defined by entirely different conditions from those of human individuality. Reincarnation is a modern idea, which he places around 1830 or 1848 in certain French socialist circles. The book is not settled for either wording (best guesses Man and His Becoming, Multiple States, The Spiritist Fallacy) [U]; "materialized" in the English is odd and may be a translator's word [U]. Cite the date range only as Guenon's claim. Historians place the modern Western doctrine with Leroux, Reynaud, Fourier and Kardec between the 1830s and 1860s [C], but arguments for repeated lives in the West long precede 1830 (Plato's Phaedo and Myth of Er; Lessing, 1780) [M], so a sentence of the form "reincarnation was invented in France in the 1830s" would be wrong in the essay's voice (G1-3).
- His argument [S]: a total being cannot occupy the same state twice, so it cannot return to the corporeal state. The reason, from memory [M], is that each possibility of manifestation is realized once, and repetition would limit Universal Possibility; it follows from his Infinite. Claimed memories and the Hindu rebirth texts are explained by psychic elements of the dead individual separating and passing into other beings, which matters no more than the matter of a dissolved body building other bodies [S]. In The Spiritist Fallacy he adds that spirit messages mirror the milieu of the sitters: English spirits deny reincarnation and French spirits accept it [C; the phrase is probably his irony]. He held that Orientalists read reincarnation into texts that have no such content, and that the distinction holds even in Buddhism, which he then regarded as heterodox [C].
- What scholars say [M]. The Upanishads (Brihadaranyaka 4.4.5, Chandogya 5.10), the Gita (2.22, the person who changes clothes) and the Pali canon speak of rebirth in human, animal and other forms, and most specialists read them as rebirth in the ordinary sense. Guenon needs them to mean change of state; I found no result showing how he read the Gita verse [U]. Shankara on Brahma Sutra 3.1 has the departing jiva take another body [M], so "change of state" is the school's gloss. His wider claim that no tradition teaches rebirth is not accepted by specialists [M].
- Use [P02]. The founder of the movement Alex relies on denied that the traditions teach lifetimes in the Theosophical sense. He accepts survival, many states and transmigration as change of state, but not return to this world. Alex should not claim the perennialists agree on reincarnation. Better: say what the school teaches and why, and put lifetimes down to his axiom.

Karma [P02, G1-3]. A reported sentence says the word karma quite simply means action and nothing else, and that it never had the sense of causality [C; book unknown, best guess the Theosophy book, U]. Reviewer Szimhart says he shows that popular ideas of karma and reincarnation owe more to Theosophy than to Hinduism or Buddhism [C]. The sentence is about the word and overstates if read as denying moral consequence. Counterweights: Brihadaranyaka 4.4.5 (as one acts, so one becomes) and Anguttara Nikaya 6.63 (intention is what I call kamma) [M]. He is no witness for "something I did to another part of myself". He is a witness for the caution that popular karma talk is a modern overlay.

Buddhism, and a change of mind [P02]. In 1921 he called Buddhism a heterodox deviation, because Hindu orthodoxy is defined by acceptance of the Veda; the Introduction has a chapter "Concerning Buddhism" under "Orthodoxy and Heterodoxy" [C, listing]. A result says he followed Shankara in condemning it as a heresy [C]; a critical statement says his Hindu informants were acutely anti-Buddhist [C]. A fragment in which he is reported to call Buddhism the least interesting of the Eastern doctrines, and to say Europeans exaggerate it, comes from a longer sentence whose book is unknown [U]; do not quote. Coomaraswamy's Hinduism and Buddhism (1943) argued that Buddhism agrees with the essence of Hinduism; Pallis and Schuon pressed the same point; a result drawing on Oldmeadow says Guenon authorised amendments to his references to Buddhism in earlier works, and he called the book an important work in Etudes traditionnelles in 1946 [C]. P02 offers this as an honest example of the movement correcting itself, and as a model for the rule never to say a tradition "fits badly".

Against Theosophy, and what he shared with it [P02, G1-3]. Asked in September 1920 to write against the Theosophical Society [C]. The reported arguments: (a) the name "theosophisme", to show it has nothing to do with real theosophy in the Western esoteric line; (b) Blavatsky's biography, the Mahatmas and the Society for Psychical Research report of 1885 (Hodgson), whose use was one-sided because the report has since been contested (Harrison, "J'Accuse", Journal of the SPR 53, 1986) [M]; (c) Esoteric Buddhism (Sinnett, 1883) and Besant's esoteric Christianity are misnamed; (d) karma and reincarnation are modern fabrications; (e) the early Society was a political agent of British imperialism, which is Guenon's accusation and sits badly with Besant founding the Home Rule League in 1916 and presiding over the Indian National Congress in 1917 [M]; (f) seances reach only astral remains, on which he and Blavatsky agree [C]. Smoley notes that Guenon's thought resembles Theosophy in important ways: both hold that a central esoteric teaching underlies all religions [C]. Georges Meautis answered in 1922 that Guenon used his sources selectively and lumped Blavatsky with later Besant and Leadbeater "Neo-Theosophy"; Leslie Price said he employed a dossier as a polemicist and not as a historian [C]. P02's use: one sentence, the school's founder wrote a whole book against the Theosophists while holding their central idea.

How he compares [P02, G1-3].
- He starts from a metaphysical principle and not from a comparison of texts. The doctrine is stated first in Sanskrit vocabulary and the other traditions are read as saying the same in other terms [C]. Sedgwick: his approach was theological and not historical; Legenhausen: tenuous comparisons based on a prejudiced selection of materials [C].
- Symbols carry the equation. The cross and its axes (vertical for the hierarchy of states, horizontal for the extent of one state) stand for Universal Man, the being in full communion with all states. The concept comes from Muslim esoterism (al-Insan al-Kamil) and he finds it as Purusha in the Hindu Vedas and Upanishads, Adam Kadmon in Kabbalah, the King (Wang) in the Far East, and Christ in the Christian world (the last is the summary's naming) [C].
- He divides each tradition into outer and inner and compares inner with inner, looking for the inner face in the initiatic or mystical stream [C]. He tests the term in its original language, a regular lineage, and metaphysical and not psychological content; the Theosophy book uses these tests negatively [C].
- Differences are adaptation and decline. A tradition may lose its inner side; he held that the Christian West had, and he thought the Christian sacraments had been lowered to an exoteric level with the esoteric rites kept apart. Schuon repudiated this, and it was the central point, on one account, of their split in 1948 to 1950 [C]. The real opposition for Guenon is the traditional world against the modern West and not East against West [C].
- Unity stands at the level of principle and the practitioner stays in one form. He blamed his early occult experience, where ill-assorted doctrines could not make a stable edifice [S]. The Introduction has chapters on "Official Orientalism" and "The Science of Religions" as targets [C]. Alex's method of quoting many traditions as witnesses is what Guenon opposed in practice, and a fair sentence would say so (P02).

Other comparisons [C]. The Esoterism of Dante (1925) reads the Divine Comedy as an initiatic map beside Rosicrucian, Masonic and Hermetic symbolism and the Fedeli d'Amore; Insights into Islamic Esoterism and Taoism treats Sufism as the heart of Islam and Taoism as the inner side of the Chinese tradition; The King of the World has chapters on Shekinah, Metatron and Melki-Tsedeq beside Agarttha; he recognised Kabbalah as genuine esoterism but wrote little on it (P02).

Influence and criticism [C unless marked]. Coomaraswamy corresponded with him and called him not an Orientalist but what the Hindus would call a guru [second-hand]. Schuon read him young, wrote The Transcendent Unity of Religions (1948; English 1953) [M], met him once in 1938 [one source repeated in P04, so U; keep out of the essay] and split with him in 1948 to 1950 over the Alawiyya order and over Christianity. Pallis and Lings belong to the circle; Evola followed and disagreed on organised religion and the "left hand path"; Eliade took his idea of tradition but built a general theory of religion. Huston Smith's remark calling him a great prophet of our time comes second-hand through a review page and should be dropped (P02, G1-3, P09). Critics: Sedgwick says he made no pretence of following the standard methods of Indology and treated Hinduism as a repository of spiritual truth and not as a body of beliefs that changed over time; Sylvain Levi rejected the thesis for ignoring history; Legenhausen finds the movement too reactionary and nostalgic; Poznanovic (Kom, 2014) and Dugin (1998) wrote critical studies [C, titles only]. P02's summary: a weak historian of religion and a coherent system read for its metaphysics.

### 3.3 Ananda K. Coomaraswamy

Life [P03]. Born 22 August 1877 in Colombo, died 9 September 1947 in Needham, Massachusetts; doctorate in geology, University of London; from 1917 at the Museum of Fine Arts, Boston, until his death [V]. The exact title and date of his post (first Keeper of Indian Art in 1917, or Fellow for Research in 1917 and Keeper later) is [U]; say only "worked at the Museum of Fine Arts in Boston from 1917 until his death". Father the Tamil legislator Sir Muthu Coomaraswamy, mother English, director of the Mineralogical Survey of Ceylon from 1903, founder of the Ceylon Social Reform Society (1905) [M]. He came to metaphysics from geology and the history of Indian and Sinhalese art, and from the 1930s read Sanskrit, Pali, Greek and Latin sources side by side. His claim is that the doctrine of the Veda, the Upanishads, the Buddha's discourses, Plato, Plotinus, Eckhart and the Sufis is one doctrine in different languages, and that the agreement comes from a common source in a metaphysical truth that does not change and not from borrowing (P03).

Works [V unless marked]. Buddha and the Gospel of Buddhism (1916); The Dance of Siva (1918); The Transformation of Nature in Art (1934); Hinduism and Buddhism (Philosophical Library, New York, 1943); Figures of Speech or Figures of Thought (1946); Time and Eternity (Artibus Asiae, Ascona, 1947); "Recollection, Indian and Platonic" and "On the One and Only Transmigrant" (Journal of the American Oriental Society, Supplement 3, 1944, 43 pages); A New Approach to the Vedas (1933 [M]); The Bugbear of Literacy (year U); Christian and Oriental Philosophy of Art is the 1956 Dover title of Why Exhibit Works of Art? (1943) [M]; The Living Thoughts of Gotama the Buddha, with I. B. Horner (1948) [M]. Collections: Selected Papers, ed. Roger Lipsey (Princeton, Bollingen Series, 1977), vol. 1 Traditional Art and Symbolism, vol. 2 Metaphysics, vol. 3 Lipsey's biography [V]; The Door in the Sky (Princeton, 1997) [V]. Volume 2 is the one to open. Its contents include The Vedanta and Western Tradition; Who is "Satan" and Where is "Hell"?; Sri Ramakrishna and Religious Tolerance; The "E" at Delphi; Recollection, Indian and Platonic; On the One and Only Transmigrant; Self-Naughting; Self-Sacrifice; Play and Seriousness; Measures of Fire; Vedic "Monotheism"; Vedic Exemplarism; On the Indian and Traditional Psychology, or rather Pneumatology [V; list partial]. Nearly every theme of Part two has an essay there (P03).

The central pairing: the Self and the self. A scripture that says "self" speaks in one of two senses: the Self (Atman, Spirit, Plato's nous, Eckhart's spark, the only knowing subject, one in all) or the self (the contingent, affected "I"). Much of his reading shows scriptures of different traditions using this pair and being misread when it is lost. Figures he lines up [V for the Mundaka birds, M for the rest]: the two birds on one tree, one eating and one watching (Rigveda 1.164.20, Mundaka 3.1.1, Shvetashvatara 4.6; the line about the eater being freed from sorrow is the next verse, Mundaka 3.1.2 [M]); the chariot of Katha 1.3.3 to 9 and Plato's chariot in the Phaedrus; the Gospel sayings in John 12:25, Mark 8:34 and John 5:30; and Delphi's "Know thyself" read as "know thy Self". Two phrases as printed in summaries [V]: Atman used reflexively for oneself in any sense or for the spiritual Self, the only knowing subject, to be told from "the affected and contingent I"; and a Brihadaranyaka line he cites, "How couldst thou know the knower of knowing?" (the Sanskrit is Brihadaranyaka 2.4.14 and 4.5.15 [M]; the English is his rendering as printed, check). Source: Lipsey, "The Two Selves: Coomaraswamy as Man and Metaphysician", Studies in Comparative Religion 6(4), 1972 [V]. P03: this names what Alex's machine picture is after; the small self is the program inside its walls and the Self is the machine.

The one and only transmigrant [P03, G1-3, P16].
- The 1944 paper opens on a dictum of Shankara (Brahma Sutra Bhashya 1.1.5): "Verily, there is no other transmigrant but the Lord" [V; advaita-vision.org citing the paper's opening; "Verily" may be a website's or translator's word, so check the paper's opening before quoting]. What goes through all lives is the one Spirit in all beings and no separate individual soul makes a journey of its own. The Upanishadic ground is the Brihadaranyaka line that there is no other seer, hearer, thinker or knower than the Self (3.7.23; also 3.8.11) [M]. The thread-spirit (sutratman, Gita 7.7) is used in his other papers and probably here [M/U]. Whether the paper cites a given list of parallels is [U]; do not write "he cites X in this paper" until someone has opened it. Plato and Plotinus are linked in the paper [V].
- Use [P03]. The answer to Alex's question "if there is only one mind, who carries over?" is: only the one does. Price: the line says the individual is not what carries over, so it fits the axiom better than it fits a soul that continues and grows. Decide whether Part two wants the saved-state picture, the one-transmigrant picture, or both labelled as two tradition-level answers.
- Whether he repeated Guenon's rejection of individual reincarnation is unsettled [U]. His own formulas imply that no individual soul travels, so the safe sentence is that his paper says the only transmigrant is the Lord (G1-3 V7). Do not cite him for "my soul comes back".

What transmigrates in Buddhism: the 1916 view and the later view [P03]. In Buddha and the Gospel of Buddhism (1916), section "Samsara and Kamma" (Heidelberg digital library, pages 0158 to 0163), he wrote of "the transmigration of character, of personality without a person", and that samsara "is not for Gautama the wandering of any thing" [V, as printed in a summary; G1-3 lowers "personality without a person" to U]. A third line, "empirically speaking nothing, nothing that we can call anything, transmigrates", is [V] with the source work uncertain (1916 or 1943). Popular thought, Brahmanical or Buddhist, conceives the doctrine "animistically" as an individual soul passing on [V summary]. Caution: "transmigration of character" is an older formula of the English Pali school (T. W. Rhys Davids), so the 1916 line may quote or endorse it; read the passage for quotation marks before writing "he wrote" [M]. By 1943 he reads Buddhism as consistent with Vedanta. P03 calls this a real expert changing his mind, and the cleanest statement of the Buddhist version "no fixed soul, yet something continues". The Milindapanha lamp (the flame lit from another flame, neither the same nor another) belongs here [M].

Buddhism consistent with Vedanta [P03, G1-3]. In Hinduism and Buddhism (1943) he reads the Buddha's "not self" as aimed at the empirical self, the five aggregates, and not at the Self; the Buddha works by elimination as the Upanishads do with "not this, not this"; the Arhat is in the end the Spirit within all beings [V gist]. Another printed phrase for the Arhat's goal is brahma-bhutena-atmana, "with the self that is Brahma-become", pointing to the first line of the Kena Upanishad [V]. A line about the Subject being No-thing reached by a process of elimination comes from a mailing list quoting him and G1-3 says do not use it. Theravada commentary reads Pali brahma-bhuta as "become holy" or "become the best" and not "become Brahma" [M]. Objection: Walpola Rahula (What the Buddha Taught) wrote that some try to smuggle the idea of atta into the Buddha's teaching, naming Coomaraswamy and Horner [V gist; exact words, page and target text U, perhaps their 1948 Living Thoughts]. Studies of the Pali atta say it cannot be simply equated with the Upanishadic Atman; Conze compared it to the Samkhya purusha [V gist]. The mainstream reading is that early Buddhism denies any abiding self [M]. Use [P03]: name Coomaraswamy as the Western bridge and Rahula as the named objection; do not write "scholars agree with him on Buddhism".

Recollection and forgetting [P03]. "Recollection, Indian and Platonic" (1944) sets Plato's anamnesis beside the Indian smrti and Pali sati for knowing what one is. Forgetting is avidya, recollection is the return (title and date V; content beyond the title M). It ties Alex's veil paragraph to the river of forgetfulness in the Myth of Er.

Self-naughting and self-sacrifice, the letting-go essays [P03]. "Self-Naughting" and "Atmayajna: Self-Sacrifice" (Harvard Journal of Asiatic Studies 6, 1942, pp. 358 to 398 [U]) read the Vedic sacrifice as an inward offering of the self to the Self, beside the Christian "deny himself" (Mark 8:34, John 12:25) and Eckhart's self-annihilation [V titles, M content]. From the Satan essay, as printed in the Matheson Trust PDF: "we dare not deny Satan until we have denied ourselves, as everyone must who would follow Him who said and did nothing 'of himself'" [V as printed; the PDF says first printed in "The Review of Religions" in 1947, which he doubts, since he recalls Columbia's "Review of Religion", so the journal name is U]. The same essay cites Boehme's saying that the soul hath heaven or hell within itself and the kingdom of heaven within [V]. P03: the traditions' letting go under one name, tied to the Self and self pair. Check the Self-Naughting text before quoting.

Maya and lila [P03, G1-3]. Maya is not simply illusion but the divine power of measuring out the one into forms, from the root ma, to measure or build. He ties matter and mother to the same root; that etymology is his own and not mainstream (mainstream derives Latin mater from the Proto-Indo-European word for mother), so attribute it to him and do not state it as fact [M]. The made world is not unreal; the mistake is taking the made thing as self-existent. On the question why a complete God would create, lila: God works without a why, like a child at play. He sets Brahma Sutra 2.1.33 beside Eckhart's sunder warumbe, Heraclitus fragment 52 (Aion a child at play) and Plato's Laws 803. Note that 2.1.32 is the objection (a creation with no purpose) and 2.1.33 (lokavat tu lilakaivalyam) is the reply. Trap: "the rose is without why" is Angelus Silesius (Cherubinischer Wandersmann, 1657), not Eckhart [M]. His paper "Lila" (JAOS 1941) [M/U]; "Play and Seriousness" and "Measures of Fire" (titles V). A secondary article credits him with comparing Eckhart's work without a why to the Upanishadic teaching of action with no ulterior aim [V summary].

Eckhart and the Upanishads [P03]. He treats Eckhart as the clearest Western voice for the doctrine of the Self and equates Eckhart's ideas with the basic philosophy of the Vedas, Manu and the Gita [V summary]. The claim that he called Eckhart's sermons "the Upanishad of Europe" was removed as unconfirmed. The formula Shankara : Brahman : Ishvara : Atman :: Eckhart : Godhead : God : Soul is Rudolf Otto's (West-oestliche Mystik, 1926), not his [V, as printed in a result].

The Sun door and the Symplegades [P03, P18]. A family of essays on the opening through the sun by which a person leaves the cosmos: "Svayamatrnna: Janua Coeli" (Zalmoxis vol. 2, 1939 [U]), "Symplegades" (1944, in the Sarton homage volume), and "The Sun-Kiss" (JAOS 60, 1940 [M]) [titles V]. The argument as P03 knows it [M]: Mundaka 1.2.11 says the pure go through the door of the sun to where the immortal Self is; Christ says "I am the door" (John 10:9); Isha 15 speaks of a golden disc covering the face of truth; in Apollonius Rhodius the Argonauts send a dove through the clashing rocks. One symbol, a gate passable only to the unburdened. This is the traditional counterpart of Alex's door and, further out, of the escape velocity at the corner of the small square, but Alex's door is in the middle of his chart and Coomaraswamy's is the exit from the cosmos; say so.

Time and Eternity [P03]. Though we live in time, deliverance lies in eternity; all religions separate the merely everlasting from the eternal (Artibus Asiae, 1947) [V]. Guenon reviewed it [V that the review exists]. Eckhart's nunc stans link is [U]. If Alex wants "the separation ends" not to mean "after a long time", this is the book: return is out of time and not later in it.

Karma and second birth [P03, P16]. In Hinduism and Buddhism he treats karma as the law of action and consequence, different from fatalism, and takes the Upanishadic identity of the Self in all as the ground of ethics [wording unverified]. He also read rebirth as a second birth inside this life: the twice-born (dvija), initiation, Eckhart's birth of God in the soul, John 3:3 [M; a sustained thesis of his, U]. This supports the essay's treatment of John 3:3 and of the door as birth inside a life.

Paths to one summit [P03]. In The Bugbear of Literacy he describes a mountain with several gateways at its foot, each opening on a separate path, and says that until you reach the summit you cannot see that every path leads to the same point. The essay ends by refusing conversion: "never let us approach another believer to ask him to become 'one of us' but approach him with respect as one who is already 'one of His.'" [V, as the summary printed it; check the wording in the book]. A companion for the elephant and the lake, and it fits Alex's stance of asking no one to adopt anything. The jacket of Hinduism and Buddhism says that the Indian tradition is one of the forms of the Philosophia Perennis, embodying universal truths to which no one people or age can make exclusive claim [V; jacket copy or his own words unknown, so do not quote it as his]. The Ramakrishna essay cites Rigveda 1.164.46 as "They call Him by a multitude of names, He Who is only One" [V as printed].

Theosophy, Guenon and the break [P03]. See 2.4. His mature Traditionalist writing followed his correspondence with Guenon in the 1930s [V, Oldmeadow and Stefanoaia].

Reception [P03, G1-3]. Traditionalists give him with Guenon and Schuon as one of three founders; Roger Lipsey is a leading authority on him [V]. Mircea Eliade wrote "Some Notes on Theosophia Perennis: Ananda K. Coomaraswamy and Henry Corbin", History of Religions 19(2), 1979, pp. 167 to 176, as a review of the Selected Papers; Eliade's judgement was not seen [V]. Sedgwick places him among the Traditionalists who shaped the academic study of religion, and Robert Horvath's critique says Sedgwick treats him poorly [V, summaries only]. Huxley's anthology quotes him often [M]. Criticism: Rahula and the Pali scholars on anatta (above); his son Rama Coomaraswamy, a Catholic, attacked perennialism as an ideology [M]; the standard complaint, recalled and not verified, is that he reads three thousand years of texts as one voice and tilts toward Advaita [M]. He is respected for his range, his precision and for bringing Indian metaphysics to Western humanists [M].

How he compares [P03, P11, G1-3].
- The unit is a sentence or a symbol, not a religion. He picks one formula (the two birds, the door of the sun, "no transmigrant but the Lord") and follows it through texts in the original languages, setting an Indian line, a Greek line, a Latin or German line side by side, then a short comment. Figures of Speech or Figures of Thought (1946) states the principle: a symbol is a precise language, not decoration [V title, M].
- He explains the sameness by a common source in doctrine and not by borrowing or common psychology. Alex differs: he proposes a common logic. Coomaraswamy would call the logic the doctrine itself but deny that a doctrine can be judged from outside every tradition [M].
- He explains differences by audience and level: a teaching for householders differs from one for monks; the Buddha's no-self addressed people who took the empirical self for the Self; paths differ at the foot and meet at the top [M, V gist].
- He distrusts evolutionism, psychologism and historicism, which flatten old doctrine into early groping; Alex's arrow looks like progress and a Traditionalist may bristle, though they accept ascent on an individual path [M]. He refuses syncretism and walks one orthodox path [M]. Both limits matter for Alex's witness approach.

### 3.4 Frithjof Schuon

Life [P04, P05]. Born in Basel on 18 June 1907, died in Bloomington, Indiana, on 5 May 1998 [M]. Parents' religions, the year of his initiation, how he came to be a sheikh and the year the correspondence with Guenon began are [U]. Family of German and Alsatian origin, lived in France, Switzerland and finally the United States, so check a biography before choosing a nationality word; his school calls him a metaphysician and not a philosopher [M] (P05). He read the Upanishads, the Gita and Shankara young, worked as a textile designer in Paris, went in 1932 to Mostaganem in Algeria to meet the Sufi sheikh Ahmad al-Alawi (1869 to 1934), lived in Lausanne from the 1940s and moved to Bloomington in 1980 [M]. One line of biography is enough. The biography carries controversy, so quote the book and not the man (P04).

Books [M unless marked]. De l'unite transcendante des religions (Gallimard, 1948); English The Transcendent Unity of Religions (tr. Peter Townsend, Faber, 1953; revised 1975 with a foreword or introduction by Huston Smith, label and edition U). T. S. Eliot, a director of Faber, praised the book; the gist is safe, the wording is not [M, U for wording]. Later Quest Books and World Wisdom reprints. Also: Language of the Self (1959); Logic and Transcendence (French 1970, English 1975); Esoterism as Principle and as Way (French 1978, English 1981); Survey of Metaphysics and Esoterism (English 1986; the shortest complete statement); The Eye of the Heart (French 1950); Understanding Islam (French 1961, English 1963); Sufism: Veil and Quintessence (1980 French, 1981 English); Gnosis: Divine Wisdom (1959); Stations of Wisdom (1961); In the Tracks of Buddhism (English 1968); Light on the Ancient Worlds (English 1966, tr. Lord Northbourne; the French date is U); Christianity/Islam: Essays on Esoteric Ecumenicism (1985); To Have a Center (1990); The Play of Masks (1992); Form and Substance in the Religions (French 1975, English 2002); Castes and Races (1957). Spiritual Perspectives and Human Facts is 1953 in French and 1954 in English as recalled [U]. Prayer Fashions Man (2002) is a posthumous anthology compiled by James Cutsinger, not an original book. Nasr's reader is The Essential Writings of Frithjof Schuon (1986); the later World Wisdom reissue is titled The Essential Frithjof Schuon (2005); check the cover [M]. The doctrines are spread over some twenty books and no treatise (P05).

Method [P04, P05, G1-3]. Schuon deduces and does not gather. The following seven steps are a reconstruction from memory, not a list Schuon gives, and the order is the file's; do not write "Schuon's five steps" or "seven steps".
1. Fix the grid first: the Absolute and the relative; Beyond-Being, Being, Existence; discernment and concentration. It comes from Vedanta and Sufi vocabulary and is stated before any tradition is examined (critics' description; Schuon would say it is what the Intellect sees).
2. Separate layers inside each tradition: doctrine, law and ritual for the crowd, and metaphysical doctrine and method. Compare like with like. Eckhart goes with Shankara and Ibn Arabi; the catechism goes with the temple cult and the Sunni law. An esoteric text is never set against an exoteric one.
3. Translate each doctrinal term to the universal word: Atma, Dhat, Gottheit and the Tao are four names of one thing at the inner level; Ishvara, Allah the Lord and God the Father are three names of another [M; individual pairs U].
4. Explain differences by emphasis. Each form brings out one face of the Real and closes the others on purpose; as recalled, Islam stresses the Absolute and the Intellect, Christianity the Infinite as love and Incarnation, Buddhism the Void and compassion [U on where he says so].
5. Explain exclusivism as function. A form claims to be the only truth because it must save a whole people; the inner layer relaxes the claim.
6. Exclude counterfeits (syncretism, Theosophy, spiritism, New Age eclecticism). Marks of an authentic tradition: divine origin, transmitted spiritual influence, a doctrine or way, sacred art [U on the list].
7. Forbid mixing. The unity is seen from inside one form, never from a collage.

Why divergence is willed [P04, M for the gist; Quran 5:48 and 49:13 are what Muslim pluralists cite, and whether Schuon does is U]. The Infinite contains every possibility, so it appears in many forms and no one form exhausts it. Human receptivity differs by people and age, so a message is shaped to its receiver. A form that stressed its own relativity would no longer save anyone; a form that called itself one of several would lose its force. The contradictions are real at the outer level and are no error. The transcendent unity lies above the forms and cannot be found by averaging them (he rejects the lowest common denominator, and Huxley's highest common factor). The images of radii of a circle meeting at a centre and of a spectrum are stock perennialist furniture, not attributable to his text [U]. The window image (a window that admitted all the others would stop working) is the file's, not Schuon's (S5).

The Sufi triad sharia, tariqa, haqiqa (Law, Way, Truth) serves as the template for all traditions [M]. His pair salvation and deliverance: exoteric salvation (saved from damnation, admitted to Paradise) against esoteric deliverance (the end of the illusion of separation, identity with the Self); Paradise is the public face and union the inner [M] (P05, S3). This licenses Alex's correction of 2026-10-07 that popular teaching may not carry the idea and the inner teaching does; say it is Schuon's reading and the template is Sufi.

Metaphysics [P05, P14, G1-3].
- The Absolute and the Infinite. Reality is Absolute (it alone is real and depends on nothing) and, since nothing outside it can limit it, also Infinite. The Infinite holds every possibility, including that of a relative that looks like not-the-Absolute, a reflection and not a rival. A third term, Perfection or the Good, names the quality of the overflow. That possibility is realised as Maya. The world is therefore neither a mistake in the Absolute nor an addition to it; it is what infinity includes. This is the cleanest named answer to why a whole would separate (P05, G1-3) [M for the argument; U for the Sat-Chit-Ananda mapping and any Trinity link]. Take one sentence from Survey or Logic and Transcendence, with edition and page.
- Three degrees: Beyond-Being (the Essence, no qualities, no person; his word in place of Guenon's Non-Being), Being (the same Reality as personal God and Creator, with the Names), Existence (the manifested world) [M]. The point is to stop two camps quarrelling: the Advaitin who says there is only the impersonal Self and the monotheist who says there is a Lord speak of different degrees of one Reality. Usual pairs: Nirguna and Saguna Brahman, Dhat and Allah the Lord, Gottheit and God, Ein Sof and the Sefirot [M; Jewish and Taoist pairs U as his wording; the Allah pairing is not clean because "Allah" is also used for the Essence].
- Maya is neither real nor unreal, a veil and a mirror. The world is real relative to the beings in it and unreal relative to the Absolute; it veils and reveals the Principle, since every form is a symbol. A dream is illusion relative to waking and reality relative to the dreamer. He links this to the Sufi formula that the world is "He and not He" (Ibn Arabi's school). Lila is the joyful side. Illusion is taking the part for the whole [M; Buddhist and Eckhart pairings U]. The best source in the movement for "the illusion that feels real", fitting the computer picture: a process is real inside the machine and is the machine seen from one corner (P04, P05).
- Atma and the ego. The deepest subject in each person is Atma, the one Self. The ego is a ray that thinks it is a source, a reflection that has forgotten it is one; it is to be transcended and not crushed; pride is the ego claiming to be an absolute [M]. The Sufi saying "whoever knows himself knows his Lord" is a Sufi saying that hadith critics do not accept as Prophetic; Sufis accept it as true in meaning [M]. Call it "the Sufi saying".
- The Intellect, seated in the Heart. A faculty that knows the Real directly; reason is its shadow; at its summit the Intellect is uncreated, which he reads through Eckhart's saying about something uncreated in the soul. That saying was condemned in the bull In agro dominico of John XXII, 27 March 1329, after Eckhart's death [M; article number and Latin U]; it is the usual point of Christian and Muslim objection to Schuon, so cite Eckhart as a witness with that caveat. Intellection is the immediate seeing of an evident truth, as an axiom is evident to a mathematician; he sets it against reason and against visions or psychic experience, which he distrusts as proof. Use: it explains how an axiom can be offered without proof and still be checkable by looking inward. For Schuon metaphysical certainty is immediate, which is more than Alex claims, so Alex's modest posture should stay (P04, P05).
- The five divine presences (Hahut, Lahut, Jabarut, Malakut, Nasut) from the school of Ibn Arabi, systematised by Qunawi [M]; whether Schuon follows exactly this list is U. They are a ladder of worlds and not of personal stages, so do not map them onto plus one to plus four (P05).
- The fall in two senses and the return. The cosmic going-out of Maya, which is the world, and the human loss of the Centre, which is forgetfulness of the Self. The return is remembrance and interiorisation, and deliverance is identity and not arrival: moksha is not travelling to a place, the Self never left. The goals the traditions name are one event: moksha, fana, theosis, nirvana, with Paradise as the outer form [M; Buddhist and Taoist pairs U]. Present this as the comparer's equation and say Buddhists in particular do not all accept it (P05). He also accepts that ages decline from a golden age, which is ours the Kali Yuga [M]. Alex's second and third clauses map onto the two senses (P05).
- Ethics. Charity and humility are ontological and not only moral; the neighbour is the Self; knowledge without virtue sours into spiritual pride; the golden rule is the moral corollary of "that thou art" [M, citations U]. His triad of virtues (humility, charity, truthfulness is a guess) is [U]; do not name it in print without the book.
- The Way. Discernment of Real from illusory, concentration on the Real above all by invoking a Divine Name (japa, dhikr, the Jesus Prayer, the nembutsu), and virtue, humility first [M; the fana, nirvana and kenosis pairing in his words U]. This is the best source for Part three. Islam means surrender, and the Shahada is a two-step cut through illusion: negation of all that is not God, then affirmation of what is [M]. Remark: Schuon reads the word Islam as naming the universal religious act, the ego's surrender to the Real (a paraphrase, wording from memory) [M]. That reading is a ready line for letting go.
- Buddhism. Nirvana corresponds to the Absolute and Sunyata to Beyond-Being; the denial of a self is read as a denial of the empirical ego; samsara is Maya; he gives weight to Pure Land as a path of grace; he insists Buddhism has its own revelation [M; what status he gives Theravada U]. Here the comparer equates things that many Buddhist specialists keep apart: write "Schuon equates" (P04).
- Christianity, Islam, Judaism, Greek religion [P04]. Christianity: the outer layer is the dogma of the unique Incarnation and the Church as the only road, the inner layer the line of Eckhart, the Greek Fathers and hesychasm, read as the same gnosis; the Trinity mapping is U. Islam: the Shahada, islam as surrender, tawhid read as the oneness of Being taught by Ibn Arabi. Trap: the phrase wahdat al-wujud is not found in Ibn Arabi's own writings as far as the files know; it was applied to his teaching by his school and as a charge by critics, so write "the teaching later called wahdat al-wujud" [M]. Judaism: little space; do not cite Schuon for Judaism, use Schaya, Scholem or Idel and check them; "I am that I am" read as Being is an interpretation (the Hebrew verb is imperfect, so "I will be what I will be" is equally standard) [M]. Greek: the Good beyond being (Republic 509b), anamnesis as the Intellect remembering, Plotinus' One as the Absolute [M].

Rebirth, karma and Theosophy [P04, P05, G1-3]. The item most worth checking, and one where the files have no Schuon sentence. As recalled [M], he rejects reincarnation as popular Western reincarnationism (the same person comes back to improve), links it to evolutionism, accepts rebirth as a traditional doctrine of Hinduism and Buddhism but reads it as the fate of a psychic residue or a change of state, and holds that a human individuality is not repeated; at death the body dissolves, the soul passes through intermediate states and the Spirit returns to the Principle. His positive account of the soul after death is [U]; so is whether he softens this later and whether he names Theosophy or Blavatsky. Karma: the cosmic law that actions return to the actor, valid within the relative and dissolved by knowledge; a weak and [U] paraphrase. Do not credit Schuon for the "fingers" version; that is Alex's reading of one-Self doctrine, better supported by Coomaraswamy. Do not present Schuon as a witness for lifetimes until his text is read. He is a witness for "something continues after death", for "the traditions disagree on the form", and for "the popular form is not the inner doctrine".

Agreement and differences with Guenon [P05, P02]. He accepts Guenon's frame and changes the emphasis. Agreement: one primordial tradition, exoteric and esoteric layers, the Intellect, Advaita as the clearest statement, rejection of Theosophy, spiritism and reincarnationism, cyclical decline, hostility to modernity. Differences: (a) Guenon's Non-Being becomes Beyond-Being and the pair Absolute and Infinite is Schuon's own; (b) Christianity: Schuon repudiated Guenon's view that the sacraments had sunk to the exoteric level; (c) Buddhism: Schuon treats it as an authentic revelation; (d) emphasis: devotion, beauty and virtue and the prayer of the Name stand beside knowledge, where Guenon is more austere. With Coomaraswamy: shared doctrine of one Self and many selves, Platonic recollection, Eckhart as a Western Vedantin, metaphysics before psychology; differences in method and temperament (Coomaraswamy a philologist, Schuon a sheikh who held that unity is seen from inside one form). No Schuon text criticising the one-transmigrant doctrine is known (P05).

The circle, the order and the criticism [P04, P05]. Circle: Burckhardt, Lings, Pallis, Nasr, Smith (Forgotten Truth), Perry (Treasury), Leo Schaya, Harry Oldmeadow, Patrick Laude, Reza Shah-Kazemi; the order, the Maryamiyya, grew from the Alawi line and centred on Lausanne and later Bloomington, and the Alawi heirs reportedly did not accept it as part of their order; membership of individuals is U. World Wisdom Books (Bloomington) publishes the school. Allegations by former members in 1991 about ritual events at Bloomington brought an Indiana investigation and, as recalled, no charges; every detail is U, and Sedgwick covers the record; do not repeat unchecked. Critics make four points: circularity (the grid decides what counts as inner, so a disagreeing tradition is called exoteric or heterodox and the thesis cannot fail), bias (the grid is Vedanta and Sufism laid over the rest; Theravada, Confucian and most Jewish and Christian practice are read through it), ahistory, and politics and person (distrust of modernity and democracy; Castes and Races, 1957) [M]. Defenders (Smith, Nasr, Oldmeadow, Laude, Shah-Kazemi) say he gave the clearest account of how a person can stay loyal to one religion and understand the others, and that the unity is one of the Intellect and not of experience, which shields it from the usual objection to common-experience theories [M]. Katz's 1978 target is the common-core thesis of Stace, Huxley and similar writers, and whether he engages Schuon is unknown [U]. John Hick's pluralism is a neighbour of Schuon's and not a critic [M]. P05: the doctrine can be used with care; the name carries baggage, so the essay need not rest on him.

### 3.5 Where the three agree and differ

| Topic | Guenon | Coomaraswamy | Schuon |
|---|---|---|---|
| Source of agreement | One primordial Tradition; only regular lineages count [C] | Common source in a doctrine that does not change; not borrowing [M] | Unity is transcendent, above the forms; one Reality and one Intellect [M] |
