| Method | From principle, in Sanskrit terms; symbols; inner with inner [C] | Parallel sentences and symbols in the original languages [M] | Fix the ladder of reality, then compare like with like [M] |
| Why a whole separates | Universal Possibility includes manifestation [C, M] | Maya as measuring out; lila, play without a why [M] | The Infinite includes the relative; Maya [M] |
| The end | Realization of an identity always there [C] | Recollection; out of time [V, M] | Deliverance as identity; fana, moksha, theosis, nirvana as one event [M, U] |
| Lives | Transmigration is change of state; no return to this state [C] | The only transmigrant is the Lord [V]; his own position on reincarnation U | Rejects Western return [M]; positive account U |
| Buddhism | Heterodox in 1921, amended later [C] | Essentially Vedanta [V] | Own revelation; sunyata as Beyond-Being [M] |
| Theosophy | Book-length attack [C] | Member in 1907, then left [V] | Counterfeit as a school position; his own words unchecked [M, U] |
| Mixing | Follow one regular tradition [C] | Refuses syncretism [M] | Forbids mixing [M] |

### 3.6 What the school would say to Alex's method

From P02 (lead 10), P04, P05, P06, P12 (lead 5) and G1-3 [M unless marked]:
- All three require one tradition in practice and would not endorse witnesses collected without a home tradition. Alex has no lineage and quotes many traditions, which is what Guenon opposed. Alex need not apologise, but should not claim their backing for his approach. A fair sentence says so once.
- They would object that metaphysics is intellectual intuition and not an axiom with deductions, that lifetimes in the Western sense are not the teaching, and that initiation is required.
- They disliked the Theosophists, and Guenon documented it. The pair "perennial philosophy and Theosophy" joins two camps that disliked each other.
- Their cosmos declines while individuals climb, and they reject modern evolutionism. A reader who knows the school will hear "evolution" in the arrow, so one sentence helps: the scale is for a person and makes no claim that the world is getting better (P05, P18).
- Also safe: they compare "from above", at the level of principle, and compare esoteric with esoteric. Alex can use their results as witnesses and say that he does (P06).

## 4. The wider Traditionalist circle

Source: P06 (with P02, P04, P05, P07, P09, G1-3), all [M], [U] or [C] as marked. No modern author is quoted.

What they add. The first generation wrote the grid. The second generation lived inside one tradition and used the grid to explain it, and through it the others: Burckhardt and Lings wrote Sufism and sacred art, Nasr carried the school into the university, Pallis wrote Tibetan Buddhism, Schaya Kabbalah, Sherrard Eastern Orthodoxy, Smith physics, Vedanta and Catholicism, Raine Blake and the Platonic poets, Northbourne farming and the critique of progress. Needleman reaches toward the school from Gurdjieff's work and is a bridge and not a Traditionalist; Upton is a late polemicist. Anthologists of the comparing kind (Huxley, Perry, Stace, Wilson) line up passages by theme; these authors take one doctrine (the Unity of Islam, the Ein Sof of the Kabbalah, the void of Buddhism), show its structure from inside, and then name its equivalents in a sentence or a footnote. The unit of comparison is the structure of a doctrine [M] (P06).

Not all of them practise inside one form in the same sense: Schaya's own affiliation is unknown [U]; Needleman was a Gurdjieffian; Raine a poet and scholar; Northbourne a landowner and writer; Smith moved from Vedanta to Catholicism [M]. The school's rule from Guenon and Schuon is that a path needs a tradition with a valid lineage and that understanding the others follows. Alex is the opposite case, so he should say he reads them as witnesses and claims none of their authority (P06).

Usefulness to Alex is uneven. On the veil, the illusion and the return they are strong. On lifetimes and karma they are weak witnesses: the school followed Guenon in rejecting reincarnation as the West understands it (the same individual returning to earth) and reads rebirth as change of state or as a doctrine peculiar to one tradition. Which of the second generation is the most rigorous is an impression of reception only [U]; do not rank them in the essay (P06 finding 3).

The shared grid [M]. A doctrine in three layers (the Absolute or Beyond-Being, Being as the personal Lord, manifestation) and a person in three (spirit, soul, body); each tradition split into outer form and inner content. In Islam they use the hadith of Gabriel (islam, iman, ihsan: submission, faith, excellence or vision) as the same split in Islamic terms [M, high]. Use: a table of equivalent terms, labelled the school's grid, with the note that specialists keep several of these apart (P06).

Titus Burckhardt. Swiss, born Florence 1908, died 1984 in Lausanne; school friend of Schuon at Basel; lived in Morocco, took the Sufi path in the Shadhili-Darqawi-Alawi line, and later worked on the preservation of the old city of Fez (a UNESCO campaign in the 1970s [U]) [M, details U]. Works [M, years U]: Introduction to Sufi Doctrine (German Vom Sufitum, 1953; English 1959, tr. D. M. Matheson; printed with and without "An"); Alchemy (German 1960; English 1967, tr. William Stoddart); Sacred Art in East and West (English 1967); Fez, City of Islam (1992); Art of Islam: Language and Meaning (1976); Mirror of the Intellect (1987); Mystical Astrology according to Ibn Arabi (French 1974, English 1977 [U]), which treats Ibn Arabi's planetary heavens as stages (P18); partial French translations of Ibn Arabi's Fusus al-Hikam and al-Jili's Universal Man. Doctrine [M for the doctrine, which is Ibn Arabi's as the school presents it; U for Burckhardt's own cross-references to Vedanta]: the Sufi reading of the Shahada as a statement about reality (nothing is real but the Real); the world as the self-disclosure of the One; the Perfect or Universal Man (al-insan al-kamil) as the point where the world knows its source; the Essence beyond all Names, with the Names unfolding the cosmos; the return by the passing away of the sense of a separate self (fana) and abiding in the One (baqa); the path in stages (fear, love, knowledge [U that he frames it so]). P06: the closest second-generation statement of Alex's three clauses; cite as "the Sufi account as Burckhardt gives it" after checking passages. Alchemy reads the Hermetic, Arabic, Chinese and Indian arts as one symbolic science whose work is transformation of the soul, criticises Jung's reading as projection, and uses black, white and red for dissolving, purifying and fixing [M thesis, U details]; Sacred Art argues that a form supports contemplation.

The Sufi ideas on the veil that Burckhardt and the school use [M, where Burckhardt cites them U]. First, the world is a veil (hijab) that also reveals. Second, the hidden-treasure saying, that God was a hidden treasure and loved to be known, so created. Hadith scholars (Ibn Taymiyya, and as recalled al-Zarkashi and Ibn Hajar) judge it not authentic as a hadith; Ibn Arabi and the Sufis accept it by unveiling (kashf) though they grant it has no sound chain. Call it a saying, not a verified hadith; it supplies a reason for separation. Third, Ibn Arabi teaches that creation is renewed at each instant (tajdid al-khalq) and that the Divine self-disclosure never repeats in the same form (la takrar fi al-tajalli), so a form cannot literally return and nothing is lost; this is the Sufi reason for caution about reincarnation and runs beside Guenon's change of state. Chittick, The Sufi Path of Knowledge (1989), is the careful English reading [M] (P06, G1-3).

Martin Lings. English, 1909 to 2005; Oxford; taught in Lithuania; went to Cairo in 1939 and met Guenon (date and order of conversion, initiation and meeting U); became a Sufi in the Alawi line (name Abu Bakr Siraj ad-Din); later keeper of Oriental manuscripts at the British Museum and Library [M, details U]. Works [M]: The Book of Certainty (1952; subtitle The Sufi Doctrine of Faith, Vision and Gnosis); A Sufi Saint of the Twentieth Century (1961); Ancient Beliefs and Modern Superstitions (1965 [U]); What Is Sufism? (1975); Muhammad (1983); The Eleventh Hour (1987); Symbol and Archetype (1991). The Book of Certainty sets the Sufi scale of knowing in Quranic terms: ilm al-yaqin (Quran 102:5), ayn al-yaqin (102:7), haqq al-yaqin (56:95 and 69:51); the gloss "by report, by sight, by being" is the file's and not his wording, and the fire illustration (hearing of fire, seeing it, being burned) needs checking [M, U]. P06's use: a ready stage ladder in Sufi words, "heard about it, saw it, became it". The Eleventh Hour reads the present as the late stage of a long cycle (Hindu yugas, Hesiod's ages, the Quranic Hour) [M]. The school's picture of the hereafter (Guenon, Schuon) is that the physical world is the outermost layer of a nested cosmos and the other worlds are inward states, so Islamic (barzakh, the garden, the fire), Christian (purgatory, heaven, hell) and Hindu (the three worlds) after-death teaching are read as one doctrine in several vocabularies, and the answer to "where does a soul go" is into other states and not back into a body. That Lings sets these three side by side in a named book of his own is [U]; do not write "Lings says" until a passage is open (P06 finding 9).

Seyyed Hossein Nasr. Born Tehran 1933; MIT and Harvard (PhD in history of science); professor in Tehran and head of the Imperial Iranian Academy of Philosophy; Temple and George Washington University after 1979 [M]. Works [M]: Knowledge and the Sacred (Gifford Lectures, Edinburgh, 1981); An Introduction to Islamic Cosmological Doctrines (1964); Sufi Essays (1972); Religion and the Order of Nature (1996); The Heart of Islam (2002); The Essential Sophia (2006, with Katherine O'Brien); The Essential Writings of Frithjof Schuon (ed., 1986); editor of Sophia Perennis (Tehran, 1970s) and editor-in-chief of The Study Quran (2015). His line is scientia sacra, sacred knowledge held by the Intellect and carried in every tradition. Two points tied to Nasr and the philosophers he edited [M]: Mulla Sadra's substantial motion (the soul changes its very substance as it grows and so passes through stages of being; Sadra rejects tanasukh, transmigration into other bodies, but teaches that deeds take form in the afterlife; Nasr presents this in Sadr al-Din Shirazi and His Transcendent Theosophy, 1978); and Quran 7:172, the scene in which all Adam's descendants are drawn out before birth and asked whether God is their Lord, read by Sufis as the pre-existent state of all souls. A Muslim form of souls before and after life, and a stage doctrine that is not reincarnation; do not present substantial motion as growth through lives. How Nasr uses these in particular works is U. His role under the Shah has drawn criticism [U] (P06).

Marco Pallis. 1895 to 1989; born in Liverpool of a Greek family; mountaineer; studied Tibetan Buddhism with lamas. Works [M]: Peaks and Lamas (1939); The Way and the Mountain (1960); A Buddhist Spectrum (1980). His case as recalled: Buddhism has a real metaphysic, the Hinayana-Mahayana divide is overdrawn, Vajrayana is intact, the void is a way of speaking of the Absolute (Theravada scholars deny this). P02 reports that Pallis, with Coomaraswamy and Schuon, pressed Guenon to revise his early view of Buddhism [C]. On rebirth he defends it as a Buddhist teaching apart from the spiritist idea of an individual returning: a stream of consciousness without a fixed self, the bardo, the recognition of tulkus [U]. A World Wisdom page "Correspondence on Reincarnation" exists [C]; no one has read it, and its author and date are unknown, so do not name Pallis as its author until the page is open. It is the first thing to open for the lives section.

Leo Schaya. Swiss, died about 1985 [U]. L'homme et l'absolu selon la Kabbale (French 1958 [U]), English The Universal Meaning of the Kabbalah (1971, tr. Nancy Pearson [U]); La doctrine soufie de l'Unite (1962). He presents Kabbalah as the inner doctrine of Judaism and one expression of a universal metaphysics. The equations as recalled [M as the school's trope, U as Schaya's text]: Ein Sof with the Absolute of other traditions (Brahman beyond qualities, the Sufi Essence, the Tao); the ten sefirot with the Divine Names; Adam Kadmon with the Universal Man of Sufism and the Purusha of Hinduism; Shekhinah with the Sufi sakina (cognate words). Scholem and Idel read Kabbalah as a historical development and do not use the grid, and they do not read Ein Sof as Nirguna Brahman because Ein Sof stands in a dynamic relation to the sefirot [M]. Whether Schaya treats gilgul is unknown [U]. The Lurianic story is the tradition's own version of the axiom [M] (P06 finding 16): the Infinite withdraws to make room (tzimtzum), the vessels break (shevirat ha-kelim) and sparks fall into the world, and human acts gather the sparks and repair the whole (tikkun). Adam ha-Rishon, the first man, is described as containing every human soul, and this is a different figure from Adam Kadmon, who in Luria is the first configuration of light after the tzimtzum and not the first human. Transmigration (gilgul) is accepted in the Bahir, the Zohar and Luria. Chabad Hasidism reads Deuteronomy 4:35 literally and teaches in the Tanya that from God's standpoint the world has no separate existence; Chabad's own teaching distinguishes a lower and a higher unity and does not deny the world, and some scholars call the system acosmic (Elior [U]), so do not write "Chabad says the world does not exist". This is the strongest Jewish parallel to the axiom and to the fingers of one hand; check with Scholem and Idel.

Lord Northbourne (Walter James, 1896 to 1982; 4th Baron [U]). English landowner and farmer, generally credited with coining "organic farming" in Look to the Land (1940): a farm run as one living whole whose parts depend on each other [M]. In the Traditionalist circle from the 1950s [U]; Religion in the Modern World (1963) and Looking Back on Progress (1970) [M]. His translations (Guenon's The Reign of Quantity, 1953; Schuon's Light on the Ancient Worlds, 1965; Burckhardt's Sacred Art in East and West, 1967) are [U]. Use: a plain image for whole and parts, from someone who worked it out on a farm.

Philip Sherrard. English, 1922 to 1995, lived in Greece, Orthodox convert, co-translator of the Philokalia with Kallistos Ware and G. E. H. Palmer [M]. Works: The Greek East and the Latin West (1959); The Rape of Man and Nature (1987); Human Image: World Image (1992). Co-founder of Temenos with Kathleen Raine (1981). His thesis: the West lost the sacred cosmos when theology split God from nature, and Orthodoxy kept it (the world as theophany, deification as the human goal). Orthodox parallels to the axiom, whether Sherrard uses them or not [U]: Maximus the Confessor's pattern of division overcome by reunion (Ambigua 41), Gregory of Nyssa's restoration of all things (apokatastasis), and theosis. An Orthodox witness for return and letting go (hesychia, nepsis, kenosis). Rebirth is outside Orthodox doctrine; the pre-existence of souls associated with Origen was condemned in the sixth century, the fifteen anathemas being traditionally attached to the Second Council of Constantinople (553), though scholars debate whether they belong to a preliminary session or to the 543 synod [M]. Gregory of Nyssa, who hoped for universal restoration, is a saint of the Church.

Kathleen Raine. English poet and scholar, 1908 to 2003. Blake and Tradition (1968); Defending Ancient Springs (1967); Yeats the Initiate (1986) [M]. She traced Blake's debt to the Neoplatonic tradition through Thomas Taylor's translations. Blake's Albion is the primordial universal man who fell into division, whose four parts (the Zoas) quarrel until he wakes; the Platonic soul descends into generation and returns [M]. In English poetry, the whole divided into warring parts that must wake, close to the fingers picture.

Wolfgang Smith. Born 1930, mathematician, later a traditional Catholic and a student of Vedanta (order U). Cosmos and Transcendence (1984); The Quantum Enigma (1995); The Wisdom of Ancient Cosmology (2003). The physical universe of physics is an abstraction; reality is hierarchical, with causation from above [M]. He holds that evolution and the Big Bang are unsupported myths (the file's summary, not his wording) [U]. Mainstream physics and biology reject his claims; he appeared in the 2014 film The Principle, widely criticised for promoting geocentrism [U]; whether he is still living (perhaps died late 2024) is [U]. P06: use with care, or not at all; do not cite him for physics.

Jacob Needleman. American philosopher, 1934 to 2022, a Gurdjieff student who treated traditions as practical sciences of consciousness [M]. The New Religions (1970); A Sense of the Cosmos (1975); Lost Christianity (1980); Consciousness and Tradition (1982); editor of The Sword of Gnosis (1974) [M titles, U contents]. His theme is levels of being: what one can know depends on what one is. Gurdjieff held that the same life recurs for most people and that a soul is something one must develop and not something given at birth [M, from Ouspensky, locator U]. A contrast for a soul that continues, and a close relative of Alex's scale.

Charles Upton. American poet and Sufi-trained writer, born about 1948 [U]. The System of Antichrist (2001); Legends of the End (2004, prophecies from eight traditions); The Science of the Greater Jihad (2005) [M titles, U subtitles and years]. He argues that the New Age counterfeits tradition. Use: a warning of how this school will see Alex's project. Not a source for lives.

Historians and expositors: Harry Oldmeadow (Traditionalism: Religion in the Light of the Perennial Philosophy, 2000; Journeys East, 2004; Frithjof Schuon and the Perennial Philosophy, 2010) are the school's own guides [M]; Mark Sedgwick the outside historian; Reza Shah-Kazemi, Paths to Transcendence: According to Shankara, Ibn Arabi and Meister Eckhart (World Wisdom, 2006) the book-length model of putting inner voices side by side from the school's side [M]; Whitall Perry (1920 to 2005), A Treasury of Traditional Wisdom (1971) is in section 6.

How they compare [P06 §4, M]. (1) They start inside one tradition and state its central doctrine in its own technical terms (Burckhardt gives the Arabic words and explains them from Ibn Arabi before he says anything about Hinduism). (2) They then name equivalents in a sentence or footnote, using the shared grid. The comparison maps roles in a structure and needs no long quotation. (3) They sort differences into three kinds: differences of outer form (rites, laws, stories) as adaptation to a people and an age; differences of emphasis (Buddhism the void, Islam the Unity, Christianity love) as perspectives, each tradition choosing one face of one reality; differences that fit neither are blamed on degeneration or popular misreading, which is Alex's own correction. (4) They compare by practice as well as doctrine: Pallis and Lings point to invocation, stillness and the dying of the self as one operation in several traditions. Here the school stands closer to Alex than to the anthologists, since Part one's method (letting go) is itself the cross-tradition constant. (5) They reject psychological and historical reductions and syncretism in practice. (6) Where the comparer equates, say so: Ein Sof with Brahman or the Dhat is an equation drawn by the comparer; the void as the Absolute is the Traditionalist reading of Buddhism, which Theravada scholars deny; rebirth as the return of the same human individuality to earth, in sequence, is the Theosophical and spiritist reading, which the school itself rejects, while karma as a law of action and result is Indian and the school does not reject it (though Guenon reads it as "action" in a wider sense).

What scholars say [P06 §5, M]. Sedgwick treats Traditionalism as a real intellectual current opposed to modernity and records its anti-modern politics. Katz and the constructivists: no common core can be assumed; the school replies that the unity lies in the Intellect and doctrine, which sidesteps the objection and leaves open what evidence settles it. Specialists object to specific equations: Theravada scholars deny that nirvana is the Absolute and many say dependent origination is no One; Scholem and Idel treat Kabbalah as a historical development; within Islam Sirhindi's unity of witnessing against Ibn Arabi's unity of being, and the Salafi charge of pantheism, show the Sufi reading is disputed from inside. Zaehner (1957) is the best-known critic of the claim that mystical experience is one kind. Allegations about Schuon's circle touch the school's reputation, so present it as a source and not as a spotless authority.

Quotation traps in this area [P06 finding 24, M or U]. "He who knows himself knows his Lord" is widely cited as a hadith; hadith scholars (Ibn Taymiyya, al-Nawawi, al-Suyuti as recalled) say it is not established as a saying of the Prophet; Sufis cite it (some sources attribute it to Yahya ibn Mu'adh al-Razi [U]); label it a saying. "You are not a drop in the ocean; you are the entire ocean in a drop" is widely attributed to Rumi, and no source in the Mathnawi or Divan is known [U]. A genuine and much-cited line for many religions and one love is Ibn Arabi, Tarjuman al-Ashwaq, ode 11 (the verses saying that his heart has become capable of every form), in Nicholson's public-domain translation of 1911 [M, verse numbers U]; it speaks of love and not of a metaphysical One, and scholars differ on whether it is relativism, since Ibn Arabi stayed within Islam and wrote a commentary on the odes (G1-4, P13).

## 5. Huxley, placed fairly

Sources: P08 (the Huxley file), P07, G1-4, P06, P09, P12, S3. Alex's correction applies throughout: Huxley is one anthologist among many.

### 5.1 The book and the Foreword

The Perennial Philosophy (Harper and Brothers, New York, 1945; London, Chatto and Windus, 1946 [U]) is an anthology with continuous commentary. Short passages from mystics and saints of many traditions are arranged by theme and linked by Huxley's prose; it has no chapters by tradition [M].

The Foreword, as recalled and not cleared for quotation [M]. It names the perennial philosophy as a highest common factor in all earlier and later theologies (P07 first wrote "of the higher religions"; P08 corrected this: the factor is in theologies, and the thing is fully developed in the higher religions). It gives a three-part definition: a metaphysic that recognizes a divine Reality substantial to the world of things, lives and minds; a psychology that finds in the soul something similar to, or even identical with, that Reality; an ethic that places man's final end in the knowledge of the immanent and transcendent Ground of all being. The draft essay's "underlying" is a fair paraphrase of "substantial to" but not the same word; a quotation must use his word. Its ethic clause is about knowing the Ground and mentions neither neighbour nor reciprocity (G1-4). The Foreword also says the phrase was coined by Leibniz; that is wrong (2.1). Keep it a paraphrase until the Foreword is open (P08, G1-4).

The Foreword's idea of conditions [M for the idea, U for the wording; P08 f4]. Knowledge is a function of the being of the knower, so a change in the knower changes the nature and amount of knowing; the divine Reality can be directly known only by those who make themselves loving, pure in heart and poor in spirit. "Pure in heart" and "poor in spirit" are Beatitude phrases (Matthew 5:8 and 5:3); "loving" is not, so do not say the conditions "are the Beatitudes". This is the most useful single idea for the essay: it backs the claim that tension is a tax on the doors you can see, and the stance that the check is made by changing yourself and not by arguing. The files' own objection (theirs, not a named scholar's) is that the claim shields itself, because a critic who does not see it can be told he does not meet the conditions; Alex should not borrow the shield.

### 5.2 Chapter map

Titles as the files recall them (chapter numbers U; total about 27, [M, a little unsure]): 1 That Art Thou; 2 The Nature of the Ground; 3 Personality, Sanctity, Divine Incarnation; 4 God in the World; 5 Charity; 6 Mortification, Non-Attachment, Right Livelihood (punctuation U); 7 Truth; 8 Religion and Temperament; 9 Self-Knowing or Self-Knowledge (the files disagree); 10 Grace and Free Will; 11 Good and Evil; 12 Time and Eternity; 13 Salvation, Deliverance, Enlightenment; 14 Immortality and Survival; then Silence, Prayer, Suffering, Faith and others, whose order is U. A title "God in the Soul", listed once, was removed as unrecognised (P07 C1). Which passages stand in chapter 1 is U (P07 and P08 disagree). Print the real contents page before citing a chapter number. Chapter themes for the essay's sections [M titles]: the One (1 and 2); the self and its wall (9); soul and rebirth (14, which separates survival from immortality); ethic and golden rule (5); letting go (6, with Silence); union (13) (P07, P08).

### 5.3 Sources and lens

He read translations and was not a specialist, and says so [M, wording U]. Hindu material runs through the Upanishads, the Gita (Prabhavananda and Isherwood, 1944, introduction by Huxley), Shankara and the Ramakrishna literature (Nikhilananda's Gospel of Sri Ramakrishna, 1942) [M, extent U]. Christian sources: Eckhart, Theologia Germanica, Law, Boehme, John of the Cross, Teresa, Cloud of Unknowing, de Caussade, Fenelon, Francis de Sales, Angelus Silesius; secondary guides Underhill, Inge, von Hugel, Butler, Rufus Jones, Otto, James [M]; Buddhist and Taoist matter from Suzuki, Legge, Waley and Evans-Wentz [U]. Coomaraswamy is quoted often [M]; Guenon may be cited [U]. For Eckhart the usual source behind anthologies of that date is Franz Pfeiffer's 1857 German edition in C. de B. Evans's English (1924), a collection mixing authentic sermons with texts later judged not Eckhart's, and he may also have used Blakney (1941) [M for the Pfeiffer problem; U which translation]. Any Eckhart line reaching the essay by way of Huxley must be checked against Quint, Walshe or Colledge and McGinn.

His lens is the circle round the Vedanta Society of Southern California (Swami Prabhavananda, Isherwood, Gerald Heard), so mostly Vedanta in its Ramakrishna form, Mahayana Buddhism and the Christian contemplatives, with Sufis and Taoists as supporting voices and little from Judaism [M]. Isherwood met Prabhavananda in 1939 through Heard; print no year for his initiation [U]. Huxley and Krishnamurti met in 1938, and Huxley wrote the Foreword to The First and Last Freedom (1954) [M]. Scholars (Hacker, Halbfass, King) argue that Ramakrishna and Vivekananda's universalism is a modern "Neo-Vedanta" shaped partly in dialogue with the West, though Hacker and Halbfass also trace Hindu inclusivism to older forms; say "modern form", not "modern idea" [M]. One-clause caveat for the essay: his Ground sounds like Brahman because his teachers spoke Vedanta (P08).

His own contribution outside the anthology: The Doors of Perception (1954) takes the brain as a "reducing valve" filtering Mind at Large, an idea he credits to C. D. Broad and that goes back to Bergson (Matter and Memory, 1896) and Myers (1903). The book reports a mescaline session of May 1953 and was written after it; the valve idea is older and not a product of the drug state [M]. The title is Blake's, from The Marriage of Heaven and Hell [M]. The valve is a possible one-line reply to the view that the brain produces consciousness, and it is close to Alex's computer picture of one machine with many windows (P08, G1-4).

### 5.4 Method

(P08 §4, M.) (1) Groups by theme and not by tradition, so the same idea appears in four voices on a page. (2) Chooses first-person testimony of people he treats as knowers (saints, prophets, sages, avatars) and not the rules, laws or dogmas of a religion, which is why Judaism and ritual Islam hardly appear. (3) Uses Eckhart as the Western hinge (Godhead beyond God) and Vedanta as the master vocabulary (Ground, Brahman), then translates other traditions into those terms. He equates Atman, the Ground and the Tao; Eckhart specialists such as McGinn say his thought grows from Dominican scholasticism and Neoplatonism, so the equation is Huxley's (P07). (4) Explains difference by temperament (the Gita's three yogas; W. H. Sheldon's body types, a link that is U and a typology discredited as science, so cite the idea and not the authority), by idolatry (taking a symbol or doctrine for the Reality; chapter title U) and by history and decay of institutions. (5) Rests the right to speak on conditions in the knower. (6) He does not argue that the unity is a logical consequence of a first principle. Huxley subtracts to a common factor; Alex derives from one axiom and then shows examples. Say so, so that nobody reads Part two as Huxley's method. The method Part two actually resembles most (examples that fit, set side by side) is also its known weakness: resemblance by juxtaposition with no explanation of why it should hold (P08).

### 5.5 Where he is equating, and specialists keep apart

Chapters 1 and 2 [M]: chapter 1 builds on the Chandogya Upanishad's tat tvam asi (6.8.7) and sets beside it Luke 17:21 and Eckhart's spark or ground of the soul; chapter 2 equates Brahman and Atman, Eckhart's Godhead against God, nirguna against saguna Brahman, the Tao, and the Mahayana Dharmakaya or Suchness. A Buddhist scholar would not accept Dharmakaya as God or as Brahman; Schuon would say the Godhead and Allah-as-Lord sit at different levels. Write "Huxley equates", not "they are the same". Luke 17:21 can be read "within you" or "among you" (P08, P09). That Huxley took "Ground" from Eckhart's grunt is U; Tauler and Boehme (Ungrund) also use it.

### 5.6 What scholars say

(P08 §5, M.) Zaehner: Huxley folded theistic and nature mysticism into a monism and was too Vedantic (Mysticism Sacred and Profane, 1957, using The Doors of Perception as his case; later Drugs, Mysticism and Make-Believe, 1972). Stace: the common core is real but needs a better argument. Katz: no experience is unmediated, with the single sentence usually quoted, "There are NO pure (i.e. unmediated) experiences", at about p. 26 of his essay in Mysticism and Philosophical Analysis (1978) (recalled wording, keep the parenthesis, do not quote until the page is open) [M]. Forman: pure consciousness events answer Katz. Hick: accepts that experience is shaped by tradition and keeps "one Real, many responses". King (1999): the modern idea of a mystic East is partly a construct of Neo-Vedanta and Orientalism. Hanegraaff (1996): as recalled, he traces perennialism in the New Age to Renaissance Platonism and Hermetic sources and discusses Huxley's book as a major channel. A verification pass removed him from a sentence that paired him with King on a Neo-Vedanta construct, so do not make that pairing; check his pages before using him [U]. Sedgwick treats Traditionalism and its perennialist label as a twentieth century current. Fair to Huxley: he claimed to be an anthologist and not an authority, and the book has stayed in print since 1945 [M].

What the Traditionalists make of Huxley: no sourced statement from Schuon, Guenon, Coomaraswamy or Nasr about Huxley has been found by anyone in this run, and none may be invented [U]. Structure only: they require an orthodox form and initiation; prefer sophia perennis; build levels and compare only at the same level, which his juxtaposition ignores; distrust drugs (so his mescaline writing marked him as an unreliable guide [U]); and read the languages [M]. If the essay says what the movement thinks of Huxley, it can say only that the stricter school came earlier and asks more of a comparer (P08).

### 5.7 Why the facts support Alex's view, and what stays his judgement

Alex's remark is that plenty of other books belong to the movement and Huxley is not even the best. The first half is a plain fact. For the second half the files supply these facts, all [M] unless marked, and then stop:

1. Not first. For the phrase, Steuco (1540) and Leibniz (1714) came before. In the twentieth century the experience comparers (Bucke 1901, James 1902, Underhill 1911, Otto 1926) and the Traditionalist library (Guenon from 1921, Coomaraswamy in the 1930s and 1940s) precede him. Schuon's first book (1948, English 1953) follows Huxley's by three years, so the accurate statement is that the school came earlier and Schuon the author did not (P08 correction 7).
2. Not the strictest. The strict school requires an orthodox form and, for realization, initiation, in which Huxley had no interest [C, Wikipedia as recorded in P02]. He worked from English translations, was not a specialist, had no levels, and set passages side by side (P08, P07).
3. Not the largest. Perry's Treasury (1971) is over a thousand pages and sorted by level; Wilson's World Scripture (1991) is wider (section 6). Huxley's is shorter and more widely read.
4. Other books do particular jobs better (the menu in 5.8).
5. His Foreword's error on Leibniz, and the Pfeiffer problem for Eckhart.
6. Scholars criticise him from two sides: the experience critics (Zaehner, Katz) say he mistook one kind of mysticism for all; the school's side says he lacked method, sources and levels.

On the other side, and equally factual [M]: a good door and the best known one for English readers; a rich map of themes and of primary mystics; a three-part definition that is easy to use; and one idea (knowledge depends on being) that does real work.

What stays Alex's judgement. "Best" is a judgement. No source in the files ranks the books, and the answer depends on the job; the files give "best for what" and rank nobody. "Best known for English readers" is a fact about reach and is fair. If the essay says Huxley is not the best, it should read as Alex's opinion, with the reasons above. Do not write that the Traditionalists dismiss Huxley (no source). Do not write "Huxley made it famous" as the first line about the movement; the files suggest naming the founders first and then saying Huxley made the phrase known to English readers (P08, P07, P12, G1-3). The files' suggested order for a sentence in the essay: the founders (Guenon, Coomaraswamy, Schuon), then the readable map (Smith), then the big anthology (Perry), then Huxley as the 1945 door for English readers; say once that these authors disagree with each other on rebirth, method and Theosophy, and that Alex uses them as witnesses and not as a team (P06, P08, P12).

### 5.8 Best for what: a menu, not a ranking

Compiled from P06 §1A, P07 §H, P08 §9, P09 §6A, P11 §9, P12 §9, G1-4 §9. All titles and years [M] unless marked; check each against a library record. "Best" is a judgement; this is a menu.

| Job | Book or author |
|---|---|
| The founding statements of the school | Guenon, Introduction generale (1921), Man and His Becoming (1925); Schuon, De l'unite transcendante des religions (1948) |
| The metaphysical argument for the One and manifestation | Guenon, Les Etats multiples de l'etre (1932); Schuon, Logic and Transcendence (1970), Survey of Metaphysics and Esoterism (English 1986), The Play of Masks (1992) |
| Why the forms differ | Schuon, The Transcendent Unity of Religions |
| Range and scholarship in the source languages; checked parallels | Coomaraswamy, Hinduism and Buddhism (1943), Time and Eternity (1947), Selected Papers (1977) |
| The biggest themed anthology | Perry, A Treasury of Traditional Wisdom (1971); the widest, with disagreement visible, Wilson (1991); the shortest, Novak (1994) |
| A readable map of the whole | Smith, Forgotten Truth (1976) |
| The academic voice from inside | Nasr, Knowledge and the Sacred (1981) |
| Sufi doctrine; Buddhist; Jewish | Burckhardt and Lings; Pallis, A Buddhist Spectrum; Schaya, Universal Meaning of the Kabbalah |
| Three named teachers side by side | Shah-Kazemi, Paths to Transcendence (2006); two teachers, Otto (1926; English 1932); structure without identity, Izutsu, Sufism and Taoism |
| The philosophical case that mystical reports agree | Stace, Mysticism and Philosophy and The Teachings of the Mystics (1960) |
| A staged path from many traditions | Underhill, Mysticism (1911, public domain); Bucke, Cosmic Consciousness (1901) |
| The most modest stance for an outsider | James, The Varieties of Religious Experience (1902) |
| Stages and levels with development | Wilber (critics call it loose) |
| History of the phrase; of the school | Schmitt (1966); Sedgwick (2004); Oldmeadow (2000, sympathetic) |
| The best-known critics | Zaehner (1957); Katz (1978) |
| The axiom in plain modern prose | Watts, The Supreme Identity (1950), The Book (1966) [M]; Schrodinger and Einstein's 1950 letter [M-, unchecked] |
| A door for English readers | Huxley (1945) |

### 5.9 Traps around Huxley

(P08, G1-4, P07; M unless marked.) "Huxley coined the phrase perennial philosophy" is false. "Leibniz coined it" is Huxley's own error. The line "there are things known and things unknown, and in between are the doors" circulates credited to Huxley; it is recalled as a Jim Morrison line built on Huxley's title, and the title comes from Blake. The Doors of Perception (1954) and Heaven and Hell (1956) are two books, often cited as one. Huxley was not an initiate, not a Traditionalist and not a Theosophist, so Guenon's demand for initiation and the school's attack on Theosophy do not apply to him. A line circulating as "Huxley said" is often someone he was quoting. Forgotten Truth, Transcendent Unity and Perry's Treasury are different books from his.

## 6. The anthologies and reference works

Source: P07 (main), G1-4, S3, P06, P08. All [M] or [U].

What an anthology does. It takes a short passage from a scripture or mystic, files it under a theme, and files a passage from another tradition under the same heading. The reader does the equating by reading the passages in a row, and the anthologist's commentary says what the row shows. Strong and fast. The weakness is built in: the headings are the anthologist's, the excerpts are cut from their context and chosen for fit, and the translations often carry the equation already. For a metaphysical claim such as Alex's axiom, a row of passages illustrates and does not prove. Textual scholars add that a line of Eckhart read alone sounds like Advaita and, read in its Dominican sermon, belongs to a Christian theology of the soul (P07 §1, §5).

Entries.
- Whitall N. Perry, A Treasury of Traditional Wisdom (1971). A very large thematic anthology, well over a thousand pages, with Perry's commentary between blocks of quotations, from the Schuon circle [M]. Simon and Schuster, New York [M]; the London publisher (George Allen and Unwin, or Perennial Books for a later reissue) and a Foreword by Huston Smith in the first printing are U; a later World Wisdom reissue with a Smith foreword is [M]. Sources: Hindu, Buddhist, Taoist, Islamic, Christian, Jewish, Greek, Native American, perhaps Zoroastrian and Egyptian [U]. Movement as recalled from the Principle through manifestation and man to the way and the end [U]. It sorts its passages by level, unlike Huxley's, and is stricter because it draws on traditional authorities. Whether it has a rebirth section or takes a stand on Theosophy is unchecked [U]; the school's hostility to Theosophy is Guenon's and the school's, and Perry's own treatment is U. Not "the school's answer to Huxley" (no source). Use: the best themed quotation source inside the movement, and a source-finder; open the contents and check that its notes give the original texts [U].
- W. T. Stace, The Teachings of the Mystics (1960), a short anthology with introductions [M], companion to Mysticism and Philosophy.
- Andrew Wilson (ed.), World Scripture: A Comparative Anthology of Sacred Texts (Paragon House, 1991). Committee-built, thousands of passages (4,000-plus from 268 texts [U]) under topics from Ultimate Reality through the human condition to ethics, including cause and effect, rebirth and the afterlife, and a Golden Rule collection; wider than Huxley or Perry (Sikh, Jain, Zoroastrian, Baha'i, Native American, African); no metaphysical thesis; sponsored by the International Religious Foundation, set up by Sun Myung Moon of the Unification Church, which a sceptic will want to know [M]. A web edition exists [U]. Use: find Golden Rule parallels, then verify each in a primary text. It leaves disagreement visible.
- Philip Novak, The World's Wisdom: Sacred Texts of the World's Religions (HarperSanFrancisco, 1994): arranged mainly by tradition, with an introduction to each and selections; he invites comparison and does not do it [M]. Frederick Franck's The Buddha Eye (1982) is an anthology of the Kyoto School and does not bear on the essay [M].
- Jacob Needleman (ed.), The Sword of Gnosis (Penguin, 1974): essays and texts, several Traditionalists among the authors [M, contributors U]. Martin Lings and Clinton Minnaar (eds.), The Underlying Religion (World Wisdom, 2007), essays by Schuon, Coomaraswamy, Guenon, Nasr and Smith; Nasr and O'Brien (eds.), The Essential Sophia (2006) [M]. These are essays and not quotation rows.
- Mircea Eliade, From Primitives to Zen: A Thematic Sourcebook of the History of Religions (Harper and Row, 1967), a thematic sourcebook from a historian of religions, not a perennialist in the strict sense [M]. Ninian Smart and Richard Hecht (eds.), Sacred Texts of the World (1982) [U]. These are the safer places to check that a passage means what an anthologist says.
- Ken Wilber (ed.), Quantum Questions (Shambhala, 1984), mystical writings of Heisenberg, Schrodinger, Einstein, Planck, Eddington, Jeans, de Broglie and Pauli; where many readers met Schrodinger as a mystic [M]. The Holographic Paradigm (1982) [M].
- On the Theosophical side: Blavatsky (comp.), Gems from the East (1890) [U]; Bhagavan Das, The Essential Unity of All Religions (Benares, 1932; later Theosophical Publishing House reprints), I believe collecting scripture passages under shared headings [M title, U editions and contents]; Head and Cranston, Reincarnation: An East-West Anthology (1961) and The Phoenix Fire Mystery (1977), partisan anthologies since Cranston was a Theosophist, which repeat the myth that the Council of 553 banned reincarnation [M] (P07, P16).
- Reference works that are not perennialist and are the right tools for checking a quotation. James Hastings (ed.), Encyclopaedia of Religion and Ethics (1908 to 1926), thirteen volumes with the thirteenth the index; by specialists, often split by tradition, with no thesis that the religions are one; dated on some points (Buddhism, Jainism) [M]. Max Muller (ed.), The Sacred Books of the East (Clarendon, 1879 to 1910): fifty volumes (49 plus an index), excluding the Bible and the Jewish canon on purpose, no thematic filing; volumes the essay can use include the Upanishads (1 and 15), Bhagavadgita (8), Dhammapada (10), Jaina Sutras (22 and 45), Milinda (35 and 36), Texts of Taoism (39 and 40), the Quran (6 and 9) [M, single volume numbers U]. Public domain, Victorian in style; quotable once the wording is checked. Nicholson's Rumi translation is public domain in the UK; check later volumes in the US [U] (P13).
- Campbell edited The Portable Jung (Viking, 1971; tr. R. F. C. Hull) [M]; his own work is in section 8.

Six moves the anthologists make (P07 §4, M). (1) Theme first: the heading is the anthologist's; each excerpt was chosen for fit. (2) A translation key: one vocabulary becomes the key and the others are translated into it (Huxley's Ground and Godhead, Smith's four levels, Perry's Principle, manifestation, Intellect). (3) Witnesses and experience: Huxley, Stace and Underhill treat mystics as reporters and ask whether the reports agree; closest to Alex's "witnesses and not authorities", and needs the further step of showing the reports are of the same thing, which Zaehner and Katz dispute. (4) Outer and inner layers (Perry, Smith, Schuon): powerful and hard to test, since a disagreeing text can always be assigned to the outer layer. (5) Structure over content (Campbell, Wilber): the risk is that a shape fits almost anything. (6) Many hands (Wilson, Novak, Hastings, Muller): specialists speak for their own tradition and agreement and disagreement get equal room.

A worked row in the anthologists' manner, for the essay's karma step (P07 §4, S3). Heading: the other is oneself. Passages: Isha Upanishad 6 and 7, Gita 6.29, Leviticus 19:18, Matthew 22:39, Shantideva on exchanging self and other, the hadith of the believers as one body. Commentary: the ethic follows from the metaphysic. What the row hides: Leviticus and Matthew ground love in a shared status under God, Shantideva in the lack of a fixed self and the ownerlessness of suffering, the Isha in identity. The draft essay already says most speak first of a community, which is good practice. For Alex the honest uses: say the headings are his; take each passage from a named primary translation and not through an anthology; add the "what the row hides" line wherever the grounds differ; state his selection rule (mystics, closely read scripture or different vocabulary, and say which) (S3).

## 7. Huston Smith

Source: P09, with P07, P04, P05, P08, P10, P12. No file saw a search result for Smith, so everything is [M] or [U]; no exact quotations.

Who and what. Huston Smith (1919 to 2016), born in Suzhou, China, to Methodist missionaries; PhD at Chicago; taught at Washington University in St. Louis, MIT, Syracuse and Berkeley [M]. He did two jobs that the movement usually splits between people. In The World's Religions he is the fair, warm guide who presents each tradition from inside, mostly in its public and historical face, and leaves the comparing to the reader. In Forgotten Truth he is the advocate who lays out one shared map. He meant the two read together: a reader with only the first may think "they say the same thing" is false; a reader with only the second may think the traditions interchangeable. Two faces, public and inner, are Alex's own pair [M] (P09).

Books [M unless marked]. The Religions of Man (Harper, 1958; probably Harper and Brothers [U]) revised as The World's Religions: Our Great Wisdom Traditions (HarperSanFrancisco, 1991; a 50th anniversary edition, HarperOne 2009 [U]). A "Phil Cousineau" credit on the 1991 text is doubtful: he edited later Smith collections [U]. Forgotten Truth: The Primordial Tradition (Harper and Row, 1976), reissued in 1992 (HarperSanFrancisco [U]) as Forgotten Truth: The Common Vision of the World's Religions, which is a changed subtitle and not a new title. The scope note's idea that Forgotten Truth was "later retitled The World's Religions in part" merges two different books. Also Beyond the Post-Modern Mind (1982; 1989); Why Religion Matters (2001); Cleansing the Doors of Perception (2000); The Way Things Are (2003, ed. Cousineau); Tales of Wonder (2009, with J. Paine); "Is There a Perennial Philosophy?", Journal of the American Academy of Religion 55 (1987), whose claim is that the perennial claim concerns doctrines about levels of reality and not experiences [U].

The primordial tradition. "Primordial tradition" (la Tradition primordiale) is Guenon's own term, used in his 1920s books, so the title phrase comes from the Traditionalist school and not from Smith [M]. Schuon's usual words for the same thing are sophia perennis and religio perennis. Smith names Schuon as his main source, with Guenon and Coomaraswamy behind him, and draws on Huxley's anthology too [M]. His stated reason for preferring "tradition" to "perennial philosophy" (tradition says the vision is handed on in living communities; primordial says it is older and wider than the modern West) is [U].

The four levels of reality [M for names, order and nesting; U for his wording of the intermediate level and the diagram's form]. Terrestrial: the physical world, what the senses and physics describe. Intermediate: the psychic or subtle realm, dreams and imagination, and in many traditions the place of spirits and the soul after death. Celestial: the gods and God as Person, Lord and Creator, with the heavens. Infinite: the Godhead beyond all form and distinction. Each level includes the one beneath it, so the cosmos is concentric circles with the terrestrial in the middle and the Infinite as the circle containing all. Higher means more real in the sense of more inclusive and less limited, which is different from better. In Smith's account the modern mind kept the smallest circle and decided the others do not exist (Beyond the Post-Modern Mind). The equivalents used: the Infinite is Brahman without attributes (nirguna), Eckhart's Godhead (Gottheit), the Tao that cannot be named, Ein Sof, and in Mahayana the dharmakaya or emptiness; the celestial is Ishvara, Allah as Lord, YHWH, the Father [M standard in the movement; whether Smith lists exactly these U]. The phrase "Great Chain of Being" was popularised by Arthur Lovejoy's 1936 book, which did not coin it (Pope's Essay on Man, 1733, has "vast chain of being"); Wilber later called the same idea the Great Nest because the higher levels enclose the lower [M]. Cautions: his levels are levels of worlds and not stages of growth, so they do not map onto the arrow from minus four to plus four; the nesting has the same geometry as Part one's small square inside the big map, an honest parallel (P09).

The four levels of selfhood. Body, mind, soul, spirit, matching the four levels of reality, so a human being is a microcosm. Spirit is the Self (Atman in Vedanta); the central claim of the Hindu inner teaching is that Atman is Brahman, so at its depth the Self of each person is not private. The ego is identification with the lower levels, the part mistaken for the whole [M]. P07 reports that Smith maps the Vedanta states (waking, dreaming, deep sleep, turiya) onto the scheme; which state goes with which level is U. Parallels the movement draws: Chandogya 6.8.7, Eckhart's ground of the soul, the Sufi saying that whoever knows himself knows his Lord (not accepted by hadith scholars as the Prophet's), Mahayana Buddha-nature (which specialists keep apart from the Atman), Luke 17:21 (the Greek can mean "within you" or "among you", so the verse cannot carry the "Self within" reading alone) [M]. A possible answer to "who carries over, if there is one mind?": continuity belongs to the soul level and the spirit level is never born and never dies; this is P09's inference and not Smith's words.

Knowing. Following Schuon and Guenon, the human being has a faculty above reason. Reason works with concepts at a distance from its object; the Intellect (nous, buddhi, the eye of the heart) knows by identity, so to know the Infinite is in some measure to be it. On this account the traditions agree because they report from the same faculty about the same object [M, wording U]. Scholars dispute it (section 13).

Why the Infinite includes the finite: maya and the veil. Smith, following Schuon and Guenon's Universal Possibility, argues that a truly Infinite reality has nothing outside it to limit it, so it must include the possibility of limitation and so of the finite world; maya is that possibility seen from the side of the many, and the world is less real than the Infinite and not unreal. It is an argument from the logic of infinity and not from a text [M that Smith presents it, U for how and where]. Parallels: lila and maya (Brahma Sutra 2.1.33), the Kabbalists' tzimtzum (a sixteenth century Lurianic doctrine from Safed and not ancient, its literal reading disputed inside Kabbalah), the Sufi hidden-treasure saying (a hadith qudsi that hadith scholars do not accept as authentic, held true by Sufis such as Ibn Arabi by insight; say "the Sufi tradition says", not "the Prophet said"), Plotinus on the overflow of the One [M]. Use: another road to Alex's "knowing needs a gap": an infinite that could not include limits would be limited by that very fact. One sentence, credited to the movement and labelled an argument and not a proof.

Why the religions differ. Smith's apparatus: (1) the levels: a quarrel between a theistic and a non-theistic tradition is often a quarrel between two levels, the Person-God being celestial and the impersonal Brahman or emptiness being infinite; (2) Schuon's split of outer form from inner content: the traditions differ most in rites, stories and dogma and agree most at the centre [both M, Schuon's standard apparatus]; (3) temperament and task, as the four yogas are four paths for four temperaments, and (4) each tradition's own genius, with differences real and valuable and not to be flattened [both U as his own reasons]. The colleague's count of "four reasons" is a synthesis and not a list Smith numbers; do not quote "four". Use: some apparent disagreements are about which level is meant, which answers "Buddhism has no God while Islam has one" (P09, S5).

The World's Religions as a guide [P09 findings 8 to 11, M for the fours and the doctrine summary, U for exact treatment]. Each chapter presents a tradition on its own terms and in its best light. The Hindu chapter is organised through fours: four goals (pleasure, worldly success, duty, liberation), four paths for four temperaments (knowledge, devotion, selfless action, meditation), four stages of life; behind them Atman as Brahman, Brahman as sat, chit, ananda, the world as maya and lila, and karma as the moral law of cause and effect binding a soul to repeated births until moksha. The section heading "What Do Men Want?" and the paths as love, knowledge, work and psychophysical exercises are recalled [U]. Gita 2.47 (acting without clinging to the fruit); the embodied self passes through childhood, youth, old age and then takes another body is Gita 2.13, and the clothes simile (a person puts on new garments and discards the worn ones) is Gita 2.22, which a first draft had put at 2.13 [M]. The Buddhist chapter presents the Four Noble Truths and no self, and faces the question of what is reborn if there is no soul: a continuing stream in which character and momentum carry on without a fixed core, karma being action driven by intention. The arrow parable (Majjhima Nikaya 63) and the Milindapanha lamp being in his text is [U]. As recalled, he keeps emptiness and Brahman apart in the chapters and joins them only in the levels (P09). Rebirth and resurrection: he treats the Western pattern (one life, judgment, resurrection) as giving each life great weight and the Eastern pattern (many lives and release) as giving patience; whether he counts rebirth as part of the primordial tradition is not known [U]. Guenon and Schuon rejected the Theosophical form, so attribute nothing about lifetimes to Smith until his text is checked (The Way Things Are, 2003, and the Moyers series of 1996 are the places to look). Alex's own line, that the axiom needs only that development continues, is safer.

Beyond the Post-Modern Mind and Why Religion Matters [M thesis, U imagery]. The modern worldview rests on scientism, the belief that science is the only road to knowledge; postmodern thought exposes its weakness without replacing it. Smith proposes the traditional levels as a larger frame in which science is valid for the lowest level and silent about the others. A narrowing image (tunnel, telescope) is vaguely recalled and not known to be his. Use: the "two views" section could add Smith as one more voice who calls the first view an assumption; but many scientists judge his picture of science partial [U], so cite him for his position and not on physics.

Relations to others [P09 finding 13]. Schuon was his main teacher in ideas and a friend; Smith wrote a foreword or introduction (label and edition U; a 1975 Harper printing per P04) for an English edition of The Transcendent Unity and presented Schuon's grid in Forgotten Truth. He knew Huxley personally around 1960 when Huxley was at MIT [U]; a Smith introduction to a Harper reissue of Huxley (2004 or 2009) is [U]. Wilber popularised the same hierarchy as the Great Nest and added development, which Smith's static levels lack. P02's report that Smith called Guenon a great prophet of our time is second-hand and should be traced or dropped.

His practice [P09 finding 14]. Brought up Methodist in China, he studied Vedanta in St. Louis (introduced by Gerald Heard, taught by the Vedanta Society's swami) and practised for years; in 1957, on sabbatical, studied Rinzai Zen in Kyoto under Goto Zuigan and practised Zen for about a decade; later practised Sufi prayer, close to the Schuon circle; stayed a Christian and, as recalled, said that taking part was the only way to know a tradition [M for the three traditions plus Christianity; U for years, teachers and Sufi affiliation]. He joined the Harvard psilocybin work and wrote "Do Drugs Have Religious Import?" (Journal of Philosophy 61(18), 1964) arguing that such drugs can give experiences that look like the mystics' but that a religious life is larger than an experience; he returned to it in Cleansing the Doors of Perception (2000) [M for the paper and book; U for 1961 and his exact Harvard role]. Use: Smith quotes many traditions as witnesses because he stood in them; Alex's position differs. The experience-versus-life distinction lines up with Part one's "everyone visits, nobody stays".

Method [P09 §4, M]. Build a frame first (four levels of reality, four of selfhood, the Intellect), then place the traditions in it; the equation is made at the placing, before the reader sees a quotation. Read a disagreement as two levels (turns many hard cases into matches, and lets a disagreeing text always be assigned to another level, which is why critics call the method hard to test). Split outer from inner (from Schuon). Describe from inside (The World's Religions). Argue against modernity as much as between traditions: the traditions are grouped as people who agree about what modernity forgot, which is nearer to what Smith does than "all religions are the same". Argue the top-level claim (the Infinite must include the finite) and not only quote. Test by living it.

What scholars say [P09 §5]. Admirers: the clearest English account of the Traditionalist map and of each single tradition. Critics: circularity (the grid decides what counts as inner); bias in the grid (Vedanta and Sufi theory laid over the rest; Theravada, Confucian and everyday Jewish and Christian practice read through it; specialists keep emptiness and Brahman apart, and Smith's levels put both at the Infinite; say the comparer equates them); experience (Katz 1978, Forman 1990; the movement answers that the unity lies in the Intellect and its object, which critics find hard to test; Katz's target was the common-core thesis of the academic perennialists, Stace above all); history (Sedgwick treats Traditionalism as a twentieth century esoteric current; King and Hanegraaff argue the Western picture of one mystical East was partly built by anthologists and comparers); science (critics say Beyond the Post-Modern Mind gives a partial portrait of what scientists do [U]). Prothero's God Is Not One (2010) is the best-known popular argument that the religions ask different questions; whether it names Smith is unknown. Jonathan Z. Smith and Wilfred Cantwell Smith are different people from Huston Smith and easily confused with him. Honest summary: a careful, attractive voice for a minority reading and not a neutral referee; the textbook is more even-handed than the advocacy.

## 8. Wilber and the transpersonal line

Source: P10 (main), with P07, P08, P12, G1-3, G1-7. All [M] or [U]; no cleared quotations. Recalled wordings are marked "not cleared".

Why this wing matters. The Theosophists and Traditionalists compare doctrines. These people compare experiences, stages and patterns: Wilber with a stage table, Jung by amplification (a symbol set beside its parallels in myth, alchemy and religion), Eliade by morphology of the sacred, Campbell by one abstracted story, Grof by clinical reports, Maslow by interviews. Five things matter for Alex (P10 §1): several state his axiom's shape (Campbell's cosmogonic cycle, Grof's Cosmic Game, Wilber's Atman project, Jung's Answer to Job; Campbell's monomyth and Jung's Seven Sermons match loosely or only in part); they explain why the separate self feels real (Wilber's drawn boundaries, Jung's ego against Self); they supply the best cautions (the pre/trans fallacy, Engler's point that you need a self before you can let go of one, Jung's warning against imitating Eastern methods, Katz, Ferrer); none is neutral (Wilber ranks, Jung psychologises, Eliade and Campbell are accused of imposing universals); and the movement is wider than Huxley. The Eranos circle is an independent comparative school.

### 8.1 Ken Wilber

Phases. Wilber's thinking has phases (Wilber-1 to Wilber-5). The Atman Project (1980) and Up from Eden (1981) belong to an early phase he later revised, dropping parts of the Atman Project sequence and of the Up from Eden story. Cite the early books as "Wilber in 1980", not as his present view, and do not state his early stage sequence as fact [M].

- The spectrum of identity. Consciousness is a spectrum of identities, from unity at one end to a narrow self at the other; each step down is made by drawing a boundary, the first being the split between self and everything else. "Psychologia Perennis: The Spectrum of Consciousness", Journal of Transpersonal Psychology, 1975 (vol. 7, no. 2 [M]; a reprint in Eye to Eye is U); The Spectrum of Consciousness (Quest, 1977); No Boundary (1979). Levels as recalled, top down: Mind, transpersonal bands, existential level, biosocial bands, ego, shadow [names and count U]. Quest is the imprint of the Theosophical Publishing House (Wheaton) [M]; the fact that Wilber's first two books and, per P04, a Schuon reissue came from a Theosophical press is a small bridge between Alex's two movements, no more. Use: the nearest structural twin of "the wall is real inside the system and gone at the processor": a boundary that is drawn can be undrawn.
- The Great Nest. Reality has levels (matter, body, mind, soul, spirit); Spirit is both the highest level and the ground of every level; each level transcends and includes the one below, hence nest and not ladder (Sex, Ecology, Spirituality, 1995; A Brief History of Everything, 1996). Premodern traditions saw the levels but lacked modern differentiations (science, morality, art), so the old maps need updating. His authorities for the perennial core are, as recalled, Smith, Schuon, Coomaraswamy and Guenon [M]; Huxley's place in that row is U. Trap: the Nest is evolutionary, and the Traditionalists he cites hold a descending view of history (the Kali Yuga) and reject modern evolutionism; do not credit them with the nest or him with their anti-modernism. Use: Spirit as the ground of every level is Alex's one machine under all windows.
- The Atman project (1980). Every stage of growth is underneath a search for the whole (Atman, Brahman). Because the search starts from inside separation, it settles for substitutes (approval, power, possessions, even spiritual experiences); the real direction is the Atman telos, the actual return. Each level has its own substitute and its own fear of dying to the level below, so growth includes a small death. Use: Part one's Driven room with a name. Older cousin: Becker's Denial of Death (1973) [M; that Wilber leans on Becker U].
- Translation and transformation (early 1980s; A Sociable God 1983 [U for first appearance]; again as "translative" and "transformative" in his integral books). Change inside a level rearranges the furniture of the current self; change of level is the death of that self and a rebirth. Most self-help is translation. A near-exact match for Alex's polished program against the sandbox escape, and for Part one's driven person using meditation to be slightly more effective.
- Up from Eden (1981). The story of culture mirrors that of a person: early humanity lived in undifferentiated union (Eden is the state before the self was split out, not enlightenment); the Fall is the emergence of the separate ego, a necessary gain that came with dissociation; the way out is forward to an integrated, higher unity. Built on Gebser (The Ever-Present Origin, German 1949 to 1953, English 1985) and Neumann (The Origins and History of Consciousness, German 1949, English 1954) [M]. It answers "if we were one at the start and one at the end, what changed?" Do not state the stage story as fact.
- The pre/trans fallacy (ReVision vol. 3 no. 2, 1980; the later book reprint edition U). States before the rational self (pre-personal) and beyond it (trans-personal) are both non-rational, so people confuse them: reductionism reads the trans as the pre (Freud read the oceanic feeling, a phrase from Romain Rolland's December 1927 letter, in Civilization and Its Discontents, 1930, chapter 1, as a leftover of infancy); elevationism reads the pre as the trans (found in parts of Jung and in romantics who praise regression). Use: it names the objection a first-camp reader will raise and a trap on Alex's side (calling Drifting freedom); do not state undifferentiated infancy as fact (Daniel Stern 1985 argues infants have a sense of self earlier; Wilber's source is Margaret Mahler) [M].
- Eye to Eye (1983). Valid knowledge in any field has three strands: an injunction (do this), an apprehension (look and see) and a confirmation or rejection by others who did the same. He adapts the three eyes (flesh, reason, contemplation) from Hugh of St Victor and Bonaventure [M, attribution U]. He also argues physics does not prove mysticism. A frame for "what you can check": the experiment is letting go, you run it yourself, you compare notes with people further along. Whether this is science in the strict sense is disputed.
- Later maps. Integral Psychology (2000), with long comparison charts; Integral Spirituality (2006), the states-and-stages grid (Wilber-Combs lattice, after Allan Combs). Traditions differ in surface structure (symbols, stories, gods) and share deep structure (levels of consciousness). A passing state (gross, subtle, causal, nondual) can occur at any stage of development, so a beginner can touch a high state without being at a high stage; this explains "everybody visits, nobody stays". Advaita (Shankara) and Madhyamaka (Nagarjuna) are two idioms for one nondual realisation, Self and emptiness; the comparer equates things specialists keep apart, so say so, as the essay does for Buddhist interdependence. Gross, subtle and causal map on waking, dreaming and deep sleep from Vedanta. Which chart names which tradition is U; Kashmir Shaivism is named among nondual traditions, as recalled [U].
- Scholars: a system builder with a following, little cited in academic religious studies, self-taught outside the university [M]. Ferrer and Washburn say he ranks traditions on a nondual scale taken from Vedanta and Mahayana and leans on contested Western stage theories; his undifferentiated infancy comes from Mahler against Stern's research; he revised his early books. Critics call the stages loose.

### 8.2 Washburn and Ferrer

- Michael Washburn, The Ego and the Dynamic Ground (1988) and Transpersonal Psychology in Psychoanalytic Perspective (1994). The ego arises from a dynamic ground (the source of psychic energy) by repressing it. Mature spiritual life returns to that ground without destroying the ego (regression in the service of transcendence), then integrates at a higher level: a spiral and not a ladder. Alex's return could be a spiral through what the separation pushed down. Wilber answered with the charge of elevationism [M].
- Jorge N. Ferrer, Revisioning Transpersonal Theory (SUNY, 2002); "The Perennial Philosophy Revisited", Journal of Transpersonal Psychology 2000; with Jacob Sherman, The Participatory Turn (2008). Transpersonal theory treats spiritual experience as a private inner event, assumes one pre-given ultimate that all mystics reach, and ranks traditions by closeness to it (nondual above theistic). He proposes participatory pluralism: spiritual realities are enacted together by people and a generative Mystery (drawing on Varela's enactivism), several ultimates and liberations can be valid, and paths are judged by their fruits. He does not say the traditions are unrelated. The best scholarly form of the objection to "they all say the same thing"; a single ladder like the arrow from minus four to plus four is contestable. Wilber and followers answer that he still presupposes a shared referent and a hidden hierarchy [U]. Alex's convergence-at-the-inner-layer answers part of it; he should say "many" and name what specialists object to [M].

### 8.3 Carl Jung

[M unless marked.] Under the personal unconscious lies a layer shared by all (the collective unconscious) made of archetypes; the Self is the archetype of wholeness, conscious and unconscious together; the ego is a small part of it; individuation is the opposite of isolation (CW 9i; Aion, CW 9ii, 1951; Psychological Types, 1921; Memories, Dreams, Reflections, German 1962, English 1963; the "individuation does not shut one out from the world" passage in CW 7, locator U). He set Atman, the Tao and the Christ image beside the Self while insisting he described psychic facts and not metaphysics. Mandalas were his picture of the Self. The collective unconscious is inherited structure shared by all, weaker than one whole that experiences. Call Jung a witness of the pattern and not of the axiom.

- The rhizome. Jung likened life to a plant that lives on its rhizome, the visible part a brief summer, and the rhizome remains (Prologue of Memories, Dreams, Reflections). A picture of what endures beneath a short visible life. "Separate plants with one root" is the colleague's reading and Alex's possible use, not what Jung said.
- Seven Sermons to the Dead (Septem Sermones ad Mortuos, 1916, in the voice of the Gnostic Basilides; privately printed; the appendix number in Memories is U). The pleroma is the fullness where opposites cancel, the creatura is what is distinct, and the human being is both. Sermon 1 says distinctiveness is the essence of the creatura, that we must strive for it, and that we are in danger of falling back into the pleroma's indistinctness. So the Sermons fit clauses one and two (a fullness, and distinction as what makes the creature) and run against clause three. They do not say the whole becomes conscious through beings and they offer no return. An earlier file called them the closest Western twentieth-century statement of the axiom; that was an overreach and is corrected here.
- Answer to Job (1952, CW 11). God as an unconscious totality of opposites that human moral consciousness exceeds, so God must enter human life to become conscious. This fits "the whole needs separate beings to become conscious" and is silent on return; the nearest Jungian answer to "why would the whole do it". Both are mythic writing and not clinical results.
- Jung also wrote that the sole purpose of human existence is to kindle a light in the darkness of mere being (Memories, Dreams, Reflections, near the end; recalled wording, not cleared; the common paraphrase drops "mere"; the chapter is U).
- Eastern commentaries and his warning. Commentary on The Secret of the Golden Flower (Wilhelm, 1929); on the Bardo Thodol (written 1935; in later editions of Evans-Wentz, the 1957 third edition as recalled); Yoga and the West (1936); foreword to D. T. Suzuki's Introduction to Zen Buddhism (1939); the 1932 Kundalini seminar (published 1996). In the Golden Flower commentary he praises the art of letting things happen and ties it to Meister Eckhart's letting go of oneself and to action through non-action (wu wei) (recalled wording, not cleared; the German Gelassenheit may be the colleague's gloss; place U). In the Bardo commentary the deities are archetypes. His warning: the Eastern way works by dissolving the ego, which is risky for a fragile Western ego, and imitation can be an escape. It fits Part one's "ease back to the middle first". Jung translates every symbol into psychology, which specialists reject (Coward, Jung and Eastern Thought, 1985; Noll, The Jung Cult, 1994, reads him as a religious founder).
- Alchemy and the one world. Alchemy is a Western record of individuation: nigredo, albedo, rubedo, and the coniunctio, the union of opposites; in Gerhard Dorn, as Jung read him, the last union is with the unus mundus, the one world, which Jung linked to synchronicity and to a level where psyche and matter are not yet apart (Psychology and Alchemy, 1944, whose dream series was the physicist Wolfgang Pauli's; Mysterium Coniunctionis, 1955 to 1956; Synchronicity, 1952). Unus mundus is the nearest Jungian match to "one machine".
- Rebirth. "Concerning Rebirth" (CW 9i; Eranos 1939) lists five kinds: metempsychosis, reincarnation proper, resurrection, rebirth or renovatio, and participation in the process of transformation (which he then splits into ritual and direct experience) [M for the shape, U for detail]. Jung says the idea of rebirth is one of the primordial affirmations of humankind. He treats them as psychic facts and does not rule on metaphysics. In Memories, Dreams, Reflections (the chapter "On Life after Death") he wrote, as recalled, that he could imagine having lived in earlier centuries and met questions he could not yet answer, and been born again because he had not fulfilled the task given to him (recalled wording, not cleared); the explicit link to karma is U. P16 reads the bardo commentary as an initiation process, and "reversed initiation" is U. Jung gives Alex a clean way to separate the senses of "rebirth" before claiming one. A circulating line, "Who looks outside, dreams; who looks inside, awakes", is recalled as a 1916 letter to Fanny Bowditch and not a published essay [U]; do not quote.

### 8.4 Grof, Maslow, Assagioli

- Stanislav Grof. The psyche has biographical, perinatal and transpersonal domains; the perinatal domain has four matrices tied to stages of birth (union with the mother, no exit, the death-rebirth struggle, delivery with ego death and rebirth); beyond lie identification with other beings, past-life scenes, archetypes, cosmic unity, the Void. In The Cosmic Game (SUNY, 1998) Absolute Consciousness creates the universe as a game to explore itself, forgetting its nature as it plays. Also Realms of the Human Unconscious (1975); Beyond the Brain (1985); The Holotropic Mind (1992); Spiritual Emergency (1989, with Christina Grof). The data come from psychedelic and breathwork sessions and the scheme is untestable; memories of birth conflict with what is known of infant memory; Wilber disputes that perinatal states are transpersonal at all. His perinatal "birth" is not the school's "birth" after plus four; avoid the pun. A clinician's version of lila, a companion to Brahma Sutra 2.1.33. Alan Watts's The Book: On the Taboo Against Knowing Who You Are (1966; with the colon after "The Book") is a non-clinical relative with the same hide-and-seek picture [M].
- Abraham Maslow. The core of religion is the peak experience of founders and later mystics, which is natural and can be studied; organised religion preserves it in doctrine and often loses it; differences between religions are largely local language and myth. Later he separated brief peaks from the calmer lasting plateau, and self-actualisers into the merely healthy and transcenders. Toward a Psychology of Being (1962); Religions, Values, and Peak-Experiences (1964; 1970 edition adds the plateau); The Farther Reaches of Human Nature (1971); "Theory Z", Journal of Transpersonal Psychology, 1969 [M]. Peak against plateau fits "everybody visits, nobody stays", and in a psychologist's words the institution keeps the form and loses the peak. Small informal samples and weak support for the hierarchy (Wahba and Bridwell, 1976) [M].
- Roberto Assagioli. Psychosynthesis (1965); The Act of Will (1973); Transpersonal Development (1991). The person as an egg-shaped field (lower, middle, higher unconscious, a field of awareness, a conscious I, a transpersonal Self of which the I is a reflection) with a permeable boundary ("psychological osmosis", wording U). His disidentification exercise (I have a body, feelings, thoughts but am none of them; find the centre that remains) resembles neti neti, which is P10's comparison. He moved in Theosophical and Alice Bailey circles [M, weaker]; extent of doctrinal debt U; not for the essay without a source. Osmosis through a membrane matches Alex's point that the wall leaks (Meltdown and Spectre); disidentification is a tidy, checkable letting-go method for Part three.

### 8.5 Mircea Eliade and Joseph Campbell

- Eliade [M]. Religious phenomena are comparable as patterns: the manifestation of the sacred (hierophany), the world axis, sacred time, the coincidence of opposites (from Nicholas of Cusa). Initiation is everywhere a ritual death of the old person and birth of a new one; myths often tell of an original unity that broke (heaven and earth parted, the high god withdrew: the deus otiosus) and rites aim to return to the beginning. Patterns in Comparative Religion (French 1949, English 1958); The Myth of the Eternal Return (French 1949; English 1954 under that title, later reissued as Cosmos and History); Shamanism (French 1951, English 1964); Yoga: Immortality and Freedom (French 1954, English 1958); Birth and Rebirth (1958). In the yoga book samadhi is enstasis (standing within), not ecstasy. Indian liberation is freedom from the cycle, which differs from the archaic return to the origin by ritual; do not blur Indian exit from the cycle with Alex's return. Use: initiation as death and rebirth describes passing the door, and the deus otiosus is a mythic form of "the whole forgot itself". Critics: J. Z. Smith and Russell McCutcheon (the sui generis sacred; universal patterns erase context); ethnographers say his shamanism over-generalises from Siberia; his 1930s Romanian political ties are documented, their extent and meaning disputed.
- Campbell [M]. The Hero with a Thousand Faces (1949) finds one pattern in hero stories: departure (separation), initiation, return. The crisis of the middle is atonement, read as at-one-ment ("Atonement with the Father"); the return, bringing the gift back to the community, is the hardest part. In the monomyth proper "return" means return to the ordinary world and not to the One. At the large scale the same pattern is the cosmogonic cycle (Part II): the one emanates into the many and the many dissolve back into the one. So the cosmogonic cycle is the strongest single match to the three clauses, and the monomyth's return is a weaker match; cite Part II for the axiom and Part I for the shape of a life. Stage names (the call, the first threshold, the belly of the whale, the road of trials, atonement with the father, apotheosis, the ultimate boon, refusal of the return, master of two worlds) are from memory; Part II chapter titles (Emanations, The Virgin Birth, Transformations of the Hero, Dissolutions) are U. His seventeen stages are his; the twelve-stage version is Christopher Vogler's adaptation for screenwriters. "Monomyth" is a word from Joyce's Finnegans Wake. He called myth the penultimate truth because the ultimate cannot be put into words (recalled; locator unknown; do not quote).
