He read the Western God and man duality as metaphor taken literally and said mystics who experienced identity were punished (naming Eckhart and al-Hallaj is U). He often cited the Rigveda sentence that the real is one and the sages name it variously. The Masks of God (1959 to 1968); The Power of Myth (with Bill Moyers, 1988). Critics: the monomyth fits some stories and ignores others; Maureen Murdock, The Heroine's Journey (1990), answered its male bias; Brendan Gill's article "The Faces of Joseph Campbell" (New York Review of Books, 28 September 1989) accused him of conservative and antisemitic leanings, a charge contested by defenders; say "was accused" if used. Campbell edited the English Eranos volumes, whether he attended the conferences is U. Campbell's monomyth is the usual bridge between the comparers and popular culture, and Star Wars is the standard example [M].

### 8.6 Neighbours

- Jack Engler, in Transformations of Consciousness (1986, with Wilber and Daniel P. Brown): one must be somebody before being nobody; a stable self is needed before letting go of one, and skipping it gives bypassing. The chapter is "Being Somebody and Being Nobody"; the line often credited to Engler was not seen in his text (wording not cleared). The best support for "ease back to the middle first" [M].
- M. H. Abrams, Natural Supernaturalism (1971): the out-and-return pattern (Neoplatonic exitus and reditus) from Plotinus through Christian scholastics to Hegel and the Romantics. A scholarly name for the shape of the axiom [M].
- Evelyn Underhill's five stages are in section 9.
- Eranos: conferences at Ascona from 1933, founded by Olga Frobe-Kapteyn (the name suggested by Rudolf Otto, as recalled [U]); drew Jung, Eliade, Scholem, Corbin, D. T. Suzuki, Neumann and Zimmer. Her Theosophical links are recalled and weak [M, details U]; the link is the bridge a reader might like and is worth checking. Proof that twentieth-century comparison was more than a Huxley-style anthology.
- Goleman (1977), Walsh (1999) and Tart (1975) compare practices across traditions [contents U], useful for Part three. Daniel P. Brown's "The Stages of Meditation in Cross-Cultural Perspective" (in Transformations of Consciousness, 1986) compares Theravada, Mahamudra and Patanjali and argues for one deep structure under different surfaces [M]. Daniel Goleman's The Varieties of the Meditative Experience (1977; reissued as The Meditative Mind, 1988) lines up meditation stage maps from several traditions [list U].

### 8.7 Methods and traps in this wing

Methods [P10 §4]: Wilber builds tables (a column a level, a row a tradition) and explains differences by deep and surface structure; Jung amplifies; Eliade uses morphology; Campbell abstracts one story, borrows Bastian's universal elementary ideas against local ethnic ideas, adds Jung's archetypes, and explains difference by culture and metaphor taken as fact; Grof sets clinical reports beside traditional maps; Maslow sets interviews beside founders' accounts; Assagioli sorts techniques by which part of the person they train. Common to all: they compare structure and sequence and not doctrine, and place differences in culture, vocabulary or the level at which a tradition speaks. The shared weak spot: the comparer decides what counts as the same. They stand closer to the scholarly mainstream when they say traditions share structure than when they say they share experience.

Traps [M or U]: Jung's "kindle a light" wording (drop "mere" and you misquote); the Seven Sermons credited with the third clause; Campbell's monomyth return read as return to the One; Watts's title colon; "Cosmos and History" as the 1954 title; Wilber's Great Nest credited to the Traditionalists; the rhizome as "separate plants, one root"; a line "the cave you enter holds the treasure you seek" credited to Campbell (not placed; "follow your bliss" is his) [U].

## 9. The comparers of mystical experience

Source: P11 (main), with G1-4, P12, P07, P08, P18. No file saw a search result for these writers, so everything is [M] or [U] unless marked [C]; recalled wordings are not cleared.

The branch. The perennial movement's doctrinal books compare metaphysics by level, and the movement's strict core says psychological study of mystical states misses the point. The comparers of mysticism compare experiences and the paths to them. They are psychologists, theologians and philosophers of religion, writing between 1901 and 1990, and they disagree with each other more sharply than the perennialists do (P11 §1). Positions: one line says there is a shared core (James, with qualifiers, Underhill, Bucke, Stace): mystics report that the barrier between self and whole thins or falls, that this feels more real than ordinary life, that it resists description and brings joy and a change of character, and differences are interpretation, vocabulary or temperament. A middle position keeps parallels and differences (Otto, Smart). The other line says differences are real (Zaehner: at least three kinds of experience; Katz: no experience is unmediated), and Forman answers Katz. Almost nothing here concerns lifetimes or karma. What Alex can use: James's filmy screen and his reason for modesty; Underhill's five stages as a human-scale ladder beside the arrow; Bucke's three levels as an early evolutionary scheme; Stace's two types of unity experience; and the Katz debate, which he should know and answer.

### 9.1 William James (1842 to 1910)

The Varieties of Religious Experience (Longmans, Green, 1902; Gifford Lectures, Edinburgh, 1901 to 1902; public domain) [M].
- Four marks of mystical states: ineffability, noetic quality, transiency, passivity; the first two suffice to call a state mystical. Across Hindu, Neoplatonic, Sufi and Christian sources and what he calls Whitmanism he finds one recurring note, the overcoming of the barrier between the individual and the Absolute, and says the agreement should make a critic stop and think. The unanimity passage is in Lectures XVI or XVII; my lean is XVI by about two chances in three, so the safe form is "Lectures XVI and XVII ('Mysticism')" [U]. The three closing conclusions are at the end of XVII: mystical states are authoritative for the person who has them; they give outsiders no duty to accept them uncritically; they break the claim of the sense-bound, rationalistic mind to be the only kind. Differences go into the "over-beliefs" added afterward (Lecture XX and the Postscript); he allows a dark counterpart he calls diabolical mysticism [wording unchecked]. The line that the mystical classics have neither birthday nor native land is a saying James passes on ("as has been said"), not his own coinage [M]. The essay's current paraphrase of James is right in gist and leaves out Whitmanism. His second conclusion is the best possible authority for Alex's stance that the essay is not a proof (P11, G1-4).
- The screen. Ordinary waking consciousness is one type among others lying right beside it, parted by the filmiest of screens (Lecture XVI, after the passage on alcohol and nitrous oxide). The word is "filmiest", not "thinnest" or "flimsiest", which is how it circulates; quote only after the page is open [M]. The keynote of mystical states is reconciliation, as if the opposites that make our trouble melted into unity (lecture U, probably XVI). A one-line veil image, public domain.
- The wider self. Lecture XX says the conscious person is continuous with a wider self through which saving experiences come, and that the "more" with which we feel connected is on its hither side the subconscious continuation of our conscious life [M, recalled, not cleared]. The nearest classic sentence to "the whole experiences separation through lives" (G1-4).
- Human Immortality: Two Supposed Objections to the Doctrine (Boston: Houghton, Mifflin, 1898; the Ingersoll Lecture, given 1897 [U on year]). The brain's dependence on thought does not show it produces thought; the brain may be "permissive" or "transmissive", like a lens, prism or coloured glass (he borrows Shelley's dome of many-coloured glass) [M]. Offered as a possibility and not a demonstration. A respectable reply from 1898 to Alex's first worldview, in a lineage with Myers, Bergson, Broad and Huxley's reducing valve.
- Surrender, saintliness, fruits. Self-surrender as the vital turning point of the religious life (Lecture IX or X [U]); the mind-cure gospel of relaxing and letting go (Lectures IV and V) [M]; the marks of the saintly life in Lecture XI (a feeling of being in a wider life and a conviction of an Ideal Power; a friendly continuity with it and willing self-surrender; elation and freedom as the outlines of the confining selfhood melt; a shift of the emotional centre toward loving and harmonious affections), recalled and not cleared [M]. His test is by fruits, which a reader can apply without believing anything, so it supports "what you can check". The best match in this slice for letting go.
- Traditions he cites: Hindu yoga and Vedanta (Vivekananda on samadhi), Buddhist dhyana, Sufism (Al-Ghazali), Christian mystics (Ignatius, Teresa, John of the Cross), Plotinus, Tennyson, Whitman, Symonds; Bucke too.
- Critics: starts from experience but with a Protestant, individual, private view of religion; Proudfoot (1985) argued descriptions are shaped by the describer's concepts and that ineffability claims shield experience from question.

### 9.2 Evelyn Underhill (1875 to 1941)

Mysticism: A Study in the Nature and Development of Man's Spiritual Consciousness (London: Methuen, 1911; public domain) [M].
- Definition: mysticism is the art of establishing a conscious relation with the Absolute, an organic process of growth, with love and will as method, sharply distinct from magic and occultism (the magician wants to get, the mystic wants to give). Her four tests: active and practical; wholly spiritual aims; the One a living object of love and the method love; union a definite state of enhanced life reached by a long process, the Mystic Way [M gist, U wording]. "Mysticism is the art of union with Reality" is Practical Mysticism (1914), not the 1911 book [M]. Her 1913 book The Mystic Way is a different work from Part Two of Mysticism, "The Mystic Way"; the 1913 book may be where the stages as the life of Christ re-enacted in the soul belong [U].
- The five stages: the Awakening of the Self; Purification; Illumination; the Dark Night of the Soul (some summaries call the fourth "Surrender"); Union (the Unitive Life) [M names and order; her own heading words U]. The Part Two chapters as recalled [U]: an introduction, The Awakening of the Self, Purification, The Illumination of the Self, Voices and Visions, Introversion (recollection and quiet), Introversion (contemplation), Ecstasy and Allied States, The Dark Night of the Soul, The Unitive Life. She extends the old threefold way (purgative, illuminative, unitive, from Pseudo-Dionysius with earlier forms in Origen and Evagrius; the Latin tags are scholastic) with an awakening in front and a dark night before union. The stages overlap and the soul can swing back. Union is active and fertile: the great mystics return as agents (Catherine of Siena, Teresa, Francis) and the self is completed and not annihilated [M gist, U wording]. Main maps: Teresa's seven mansions (Interior Castle) and John of the Cross's night.
- Sources: overwhelmingly Western (Plotinus, then the Christian line from Pseudo-Dionysius through Eckhart, Ruysbroeck, Suso, Tauler, Julian, the two Catherines, Teresa, John of the Cross, Boehme, Blake and Jacopone); thinner Sufi and Hindu material. The pattern's generality is her claim and not all specialists accept it. She also wrote the introduction to Kabir (One Hundred Poems of Kabir, India Society, London, 1914; Songs of Kabir, Macmillan, New York, 1915, translated by Tagore with her help). Before 1911 she had passed through occult circles (a short membership of a Golden Dawn order, as recalled [U]) and Mysticism draws a hard line against the occult. Von Hugel became her spiritual director about 1921, after the book.
- Use and caution: a five-stage ladder with a clear low point before the top, and an ending that is work in the world. Her scale measures the person's relation to the Absolute; Alex's measures how wide a piece of the whole the person is at home in; do not map one onto the other. The dark night is where what carried you so far stops working, which echoes the wall at the corner of the small square (P11's observation, not hers). Critics: still the standard English handbook of the stages but built from Western Catholic sources; Grace Jantzen (Power, Gender and Christian Mysticism, 1995) and Michel de Certeau (The Mystic Fable) argue "the mystic" as a private inner experience is a modern construct.

### 9.3 Richard Maurice Bucke (1837 to 1902)

Cosmic Consciousness: A Study in the Evolution of the Human Mind (Philadelphia: Innes and Sons, 1901). Born in England, raised in Ontario, Canadian psychiatrist and Whitman's friend and biographer [M]. Three levels: simple consciousness (animals), self consciousness (humans, with language and abstraction) and cosmic consciousness (a third level appearing sporadically and due to become common). The founders of the great religions had it, and scriptures and creeds are what it left behind. Marks as recalled [U]: a subjective light, moral elevation, intellectual illumination, a sense of immortality, loss of the fear of death and of the sense of sin, suddenness, onset between about 30 and 40. His own illumination came in 1872, at about 35, in England, told in the third person [M]. Cases (order U): Buddha, Jesus, Paul, Plotinus, Mohammed, Dante, Las Casas, John of the Cross, Francis Bacon, Boehme, Blake, Balzac, Whitman, Edward Carpenter. He equates the state with Nirvana and the Kingdom of Heaven. "Cosmic consciousness" is his coinage and not an Eastern or Theosophical term. The earliest author in this slice with an arrow of stages and a next stage the species is moving toward; a historical version of "the traditions agree because people woke up". Caution: doubtful entries, hand-picked and almost all men; dated evolutionism. His term passed to Ouspensky's Tertium Organum and, I believe, to Maslow [U] (P11, G1-4).

### 9.4 Rudolf Otto (1869 to 1937)

Lutheran theologian; The Idea of the Holy (1917); West-oestliche Mystik (Gotha, 1926); English Mysticism East and West: A Comparative Analysis of the Nature of Mysticism (tr. Bertha L. Bracey and Richenda C. Payne, New York: Macmillan, 1932); India's Religion of Grace and Christianity Compared and Contrasted (1930), which sets Ramanuja beside Lutheran grace [M]. Eckhart beside Shankara, term by term. Parallels: the divine ground or spark of the soul in Eckhart and the atman in Shankara; the Godhead above God and the nirguna Brahman above Ishvara; the creature as nothing without the divine; knowing that is a becoming one. The formula Shankara : Brahman : Ishvara : Atman :: Eckhart : Godhead : God : Soul is Otto's own [C, from P03's first pass]. Differences as recalled [U]: in Eckhart the Godhead is dynamic and overflowing, with the birth of the Son in the soul, and the creature is a real creation; in Shankara the Absolute is static being and the world is maya. He treats the two as comparable types stamped by their religions and not as identical. The cleanest worked example of a point-by-point comparison by an author honest about limits; critics say a Lutheran yardstick makes Eckhart the fuller form; Eckhart's own theology was in part condemned in 1329 (bull In agro dominico, after his death), so equating him with the Upanishadic view is itself a claim. Eckhart's Gelassenheit (releasement) and Abgeschiedenheit (detachment), and Sermon 86 on Martha and Mary, where working Martha is read as the mature one acting from the ground Mary is still seeking, give a medieval word for letting go and a text where the point of it is better action; whether Otto treats them is U. Rights: the 1932 English translation may still be in copyright [U].

### 9.5 W. T. Stace (1886 to 1967)

British civil servant in Ceylon, then Princeton philosopher. Mysticism and Philosophy (Philadelphia: Lippincott, 1960); The Teachings of the Mystics (New York: New American Library, 1960) [M]. All mystical experience contains a universal core in two types; cultures differ in the interpretation added afterward.
- Extrovertive: (1) the unifying vision, all things are One, seen through the objects of sense; (2) a more concrete sense of the One as an inner life in all things. Introvertive: (1) the unitary consciousness, pure consciousness emptied of sensory and conceptual content, the One or Void; (2) non-spatial and non-temporal. Both share marks 3 to 7: a sense of objectivity or reality; blessedness, peace, joy; a feeling of the holy or divine; paradoxicality; alleged ineffability [M; exact wording and the chapter title U]. Both types occur in East and West; "introvertive is Eastern, extrovertive is Western" is wrong.
- Method: separate experience from interpretation; ignore the cause of the experience (the principle of causal indifference); the empirical self falls away and what remains is the universal Self. "I became one with God", "I am Brahman" and "I saw the Void" differ only in doctrine; personal union with God is an interpretation of the introvertive experience, a move against Zaehner.
- Use: the extrovertive type, the world seen as one living whole, fits Alex's axiom of a whole experiencing separation; the introvertive type fits "the separation ends". Alex can name both and say they are two doors to one claim. Critics: circular (cases that do not fit are called not mystical), a hidden monist bias; his work underlies the questionnaires of modern psychedelic research (Pahnke's Good Friday study 1962, Hood's M-scale 1975). Stace's examples may come from his own anthology and not "via James and Underhill" [U] (P11, P12).

### 9.6 R. C. Zaehner (1913 to 1974)

Spalding Professor of Eastern Religions and Ethics at Oxford 1952 to 1974; Catholic convert 1946 [M]. Mysticism Sacred and Profane: An Inquiry into Some Varieties of Praeternatural Experience (Clarendon, 1957); At Sundry Times (1958); Hindu and Muslim Mysticism (Athlone, 1960); a Gita commentary (1969) reading it as theistic; Concordant Discord (Gifford Lectures, 1970), more conciliatory, proposing that faiths are interdependent and not identical; Drugs, Mysticism and Make-Believe (Collins, 1972; US title Zen, Drugs and Mysticism [U]), a later attack on drug mysticism that again takes Huxley as its main target. Three types: nature mysticism (all in one, one in all; his coinage "panenhenic"; in his reading Huxley's mescaline experience), monistic mysticism (the soul isolated and finding itself: Samkhya-Yoga, Advaita, much of Buddhism), theistic mysticism (loving union with a personal God: Christian, Sufi, bhakti). He held that the theistic is not a version of the monistic and ranked it highest, which is a theological position, so he is an honest but not neutral witness. In the 1960 book he argues that the monistic strain in the early Sufi Bistami came from Vedanta (through his teacher Abu Ali of Sind [U]) and that theistic Sufis such as Junayd corrected it into a sober form in which the self returns and abides (fana then baqa); later scholars, including Carl Ernst, contest the Indian-influence thesis [M, Ernst U]. Title trap: Mysticism Sacred and Profane is not Eliade's The Sacred and the Profane (German 1957, English 1959). Use: the standard statement that union of love and union of identity are different things; a Christian or Muslim reader will raise it against the second view. Fana then baqa is the Sufi form of union then return (P11, P12, P08).

### 9.7 Smart, Happold, Katz and Forman

- Ninian Smart (1927 to 2001). Reasons and Faiths (1958); "Interpretation and Mystical Experience", Religious Studies 1 (1965); The Religious Experience of Mankind (1969), with his dimensions of religion (six in 1969: doctrinal, mythic, ethical, ritual, experiential, social; the seventh, material, added later in Dimensions of the Sacred, 1996) [M]. Phenomenology alone cannot compare mystical experiences, because each tradition's doctrine helps define what the experience is: Buddhist nirvana and Vedantic Brahman are not the same without the doctrines around them. He separates a numinous strand (awe before an Other: the prophetic, theistic traditions) from a mystical, contemplative one (the Indian traditions). The middle position, an early step toward Katz. He is not the usual citation for family resemblance applied to religion (that is Alston and Saler).
- F. C. Happold (1893 to 1971), Mysticism: A Study and an Anthology (Penguin, 1963; revised 1970): a popular synthesis treating mysticism as a dimension of consciousness across ages and religions [M]; contents and his school U. A student's door into the debate, not a source to cite.
- Steven T. Katz. "Language, Epistemology, and Mysticism" in Katz (ed.), Mysticism and Philosophical Analysis (London: Sheldon Press; New York: Oxford University Press, 1978; the essay runs about pp. 22 to 74). Later edited volumes: Mysticism and Religious Traditions (1983, with his essay "The Conservative Character of Mystical Experience"), Mysticism and Language (1992), Mysticism and Sacred Scripture (2000). His famous sentence is a flat claim in capitals that there are no pure (that is, unmediated) experiences, at about p. 26, stated as his single epistemological assumption; the shortened online form drops the parenthesis, so do not quote it without it, and not until the page is open [M, recalled]. Tradition, concepts and training shape the experience before, during and after, so the same words from a Buddhist and a Christian do not name the same experience. He treats Stace's split of experience from interpretation as untenable, argues that the Jewish goal, devekut (cleaving to God), differs from Christian union and from Buddhist emptiness, and that such goals are trained for. His target is the common-core thesis in general (Stace, Huxley, Underhill, Otto and similar writers); whether he engages Schuon is unknown, and Schuon's thesis is a different one (unity at the level of metaphysical principle and not sameness of experience) [M, U]. Dominant from about 1978 to 1990, then challenged by Forman and by Ferrer. Use for Alex: if Katz is right, the traditions do not report the same experience but can still report the same structure. Alex's claim, the same principles in different words, survives Katz at the level of structure and not of raw experience; saying that out loud is useful (P11, P12, P05).
- Robert K. C. Forman. The Problem of Pure Consciousness: Mysticism and Philosophy (Oxford University Press, 1990, as editor); The Innate Capacity: Mysticism, Psychology, and Philosophy (1998); Mysticism, Mind, Consciousness (SUNY, 1999). Katz assumes every experience has content for concepts to shape; mystics in many traditions report wakeful awareness with no thought, image or sense of self, a pure consciousness event (PCE); with no content there is nothing for tradition to mould, so the PCE can be the same across cultures while the interpretation differs. He calls the way it is known knowledge by identity. He may treat forgetting and letting go (Eckhart's Gelassenheit) as the mechanism that produces a PCE [U]. Opposed: Paul Griffiths, On Being Mindless (1986), and an essay in Forman's own volume [U], "Pure Consciousness and Indian Buddhism" as recalled: Indian Buddhist scholastics debated the attainment of cessation as a state where mind is absent, not as pure consciousness; Sharf adds that reports of a contentless state are retrospective and checked by tradition. Forman is the best scholarly ally for a limited cross-cultural claim (P12).

### 9.8 Others

[P11 §2.15, M unless marked.] W. R. Inge, Christian Mysticism (Bampton Lectures, 1899), with an appendix of many definitions (twenty-six, [U]); Friedrich von Hugel, The Mystical Element of Religion (1908); Nathan Soderblom's pair mysticism of infinity (Indian, Neoplatonic) against mysticism of personality (Christian, prophetic) [dates and work U, keep out of the essay unless checked]; Gershom Scholem, Major Trends in Jewish Mysticism (lectures 1938, book 1941), who stressed that the Jewish mystic rarely loses the sense of the gap between creator and creature, against Moshe Idel (Kabbalah: New Perspectives, Yale, 1988, and later books), who argues Jewish mystics do speak of union; this bears on Alex's rule for the Abrahamic traditions: specialists disagree, and Idel supports "they say it in the mystical layer"; Michael Sells, Mystical Languages of Unsaying (Chicago, 1994), who compares by the language of negation in Plotinus, Eckhart, Ibn Arabi, Porete and Pseudo-Dionysius; Bernard McGinn's The Foundations of Mysticism (1991) defines mysticism by consciousness of God's presence and the way of life that prepares for it, but it is volume 1 of a history of Western Christian mysticism, so it is a model of care and not a cross-tradition definition (P12). Alan Watts (The Supreme Identity, 1950), Sarvepalli Radhakrishnan, Bede Griffiths (Return to the Centre, 1976), Raimon Panikkar (The Unknown Christ of Hinduism, 1964), Thomas Merton (Mystics and Zen Masters, 1967) and James are each a person who knew one tradition from inside and read it against another; useful as witnesses to the claim and not as proofs (P07 f22). D. T. Suzuki, Mysticism: Christian and Buddhist (1957): Eckhart beside Zen [M] (G1-4). Evans-Wentz's Tibetan Book of the Dead (1927) is a Western editor's doorway to Tibetan after-death teaching with his own Theosophical frame; Donald Lopez (The Tibetan Book of the Dead: A Biography, 2011) shows it was shaped by Vedantic and Theosophical language [M].

The Traditionalist objection to this whole branch [P11 §2.15]. For Guenon and Schuon, psychological study of mystical states misses the point, since the criterion is metaphysical doctrine and intellectual intuition. Guenon used "mysticism" for a lesser, devotional path set against initiation and gnosis [M, wording U]. The movement asks what the traditions teach about reality; the comparers ask whether the mystics' reports agree and why. An essay that wants both can set a doctrine voice (Schuon, Coomaraswamy, Smith) beside an experience voice (James, Stace, Underhill) and say it is doing so.

Methods of this branch [P11 §4]. James collects first-person reports into one pool, sorts by features and builds the marks by induction, with differences put into over-beliefs and the test of value being fruits. Underhill reads the Christian mystics as a body, extracts a path from their lives and then holds that other traditions fit it. Bucke lists the people he judges to have had the same awakening, tabulates marks and derives an evolutionary story; selection is his weak point. Otto takes one pair and compares term by term in two parts, parallels and differences, explaining differences by the religion each stands in. Stace defines shared marks in a table, splits them into two types, separates experience from interpretation. Zaehner sorts by type, ranks the types, and uses his own theology as the yardstick. Smart maps the dimensions of each tradition. Katz starts from one tradition's training and shows how it shapes the experience. Happold anthologises.

Rights [P11 lead 14]. James (1902), Underhill (1911) and Bucke (1901) are public domain; Otto's 1932 translation may not be; Stace, Zaehner, Smart, Katz and Forman are in copyright, so quote briefly and check each sentence in print.

Three Eckhart traps [P11 §10, G1-4, P18]: the eye with which I see God is the eye with which God sees me is genuine Eckhart (Sermon 12, "Qui audit me", as I recall; number U); the line that if the only prayer you ever say is thank you it will be enough is widely credited to him and unsourced [U]; anthology lines from Pfeiffer's 1857 edition may not be Eckhart's; check against Quint. Eckhart's "without why" is his own (sunder warumbe; Quint's sermon 5b); the "rose without why" is Angelus Silesius.

## 10. Non-Western precedents

Source: P13 (main), with G1-4, G1-7, P01, P03, P16, P17, S3, S5. All [M] or [U] unless marked [C]; no wording is verified.

Outside the West, "the traditions say the same thing" is older and more practical than the Renaissance and Theosophical story. It appears as a hymn line (the Rigveda), a parable (the Udana), scripture (Gita, Qur'an), state policy (Ashoka, Akbar), ritual (Gandhi's prayers, shared shrines in Japan), a doctrine of manifestations (honji suijaku, Baha'i) and a personal experiment (Ramakrishna, Dara). Six explanations of why the forms differ keep recurring: different names for one thing; partial views of one whole; different paths to one summit; one source appearing in many forms; different levels of readiness; going under the forms to the heart. Each is a claim by the comparer, not by the traditions. Two points matter for Alex. First, almost every reconciler has a top: Dara reads Vedanta in Sufi words, Vivekananda puts Advaita highest, Kukai puts Shingon highest, Zongmi puts the Buddhist One Mind teaching highest, the Baha'i scheme puts the latest revelation last and best. Alex's top is his axiom, and the essay can say so. Second, the thinkers closest to his three clauses (one ground, a veil or forgetting, a return) are the metaphysicians: Ibn Arabi, the Awakening of Faith with Zongmi, Aurobindo, Wang Yangming. The tolerance voices (Akbar, Kabir, Nanak, Gandhi, Ashoka) mostly reconcile to stop violence and give an ethic, which suits the karma and fingers argument. Some keep rebirth (Sikhs, Jains, Aurobindo), Buddhists keep it without a fixed soul, and the Baha'i faith rejects it, so the lifetimes step is not unanimous even among reconcilers (P13 §1).

### 10.1 India

- Rigveda 1.164.46, "ekam sat viprah bahudha vadanti" (the transliteration as remembered; the sandhi form "vipra bahudha" also circulates and neither is an error). A riddle hymn says the inspired poets speak of what is one in many ways and lists Indra, Mitra, Varuna, Agni, Yama and Matarishvan as names (the heavenly bird Garutman is in the first half) [M]. Sat means what is, the real. Griffith 1896 is the second edition (first 1889 to 1892 [U]); Jamison and Brereton 2014. Later Hindus, and Coomaraswamy (P03 reports), cite it as a plea for tolerance; Muller's word for the Vedic pattern was henotheism. The English slogan "Truth is one, the wise call it by many names" is a loose rendering, often credited to Gandhi or Vivekananda, who quoted it; say "what is one". It was not written for pluralism.
- Bhagavad Gita 4.11 (however people approach me, I meet them so), 7.21 (whatever form a devotee wants to worship with faith, I make that faith steady), 9.23 (devotees of other gods worship me too, but by the wrong rule, avidhipurvakam), 7.23 (their fruit is limited) [M, as remembered]. Inclusivist: other worship reaches Krishna, imperfectly. Do not write "the Gita says all religions are equal". Vivekananda and Gandhi extend it to other religions.
- Udana 6.4, the blind men and the elephant. The Buddha tells it about quarrelling sectarians who cling to views on whether the world is eternal or the soul is the body; a king has blind men touch one part each; each names the elephant from that part; they come to blows [M]. The original point is against clinging to views on unanswerable questions, with the Buddha as the one who sees; it is not an original claim that all religions are parts of one truth. Later a Jain, Hindu, Sufi and Western pluralist picture; a Jain text that tells it cannot be named [U]. Lesslie Newbigin (1989) noted that the pluralist reading needs a teller who sees the whole elephant; Hick used the elephant (P13, S5).
- Jain anekantavada. Reality has many sides; each viewpoint (naya) is partial and true from where it stands; statements are qualified (syat, "from a standpoint") [M]. Siddhasena Divakara, Sanmati-tarka (about the fifth century); Haribhadra (eighth century), Saddarshanasamuccaya; Mallisena, Syadvadamanjari (1292); Umasvati, Tattvartha Sutra 5.21 (souls help one another). A verse attributed to Haribhadra says he has no bias for Mahavira nor hatred for Kapila, and one should accept whoever speaks reasonably; it is usually cited from the Lokatattvanirnaya (1.38) and not the Saddarshanasamuccaya [U]. Limits: Shankara's Brahmasutra commentary (about 2.2.33 to 36) attacks it, Jains hold only their path liberates and souls are many; Matilal (1981) and Long (2009) call it perspectivism with a truth to find. The draft's line that Jain thinkers later used the story for many-sidedness is [U]; the doctrine of many-sidedness is safe.
- Ashoka, Rock Edict 12 (about 256 BCE): do not honour only your own sect and condemn others; concord is good, so that all may listen to one another's teaching [M].
- Akbar (r. 1556 to 1605). The Ibadat Khana at Fatehpur Sikri (1575), first for Muslim scholars, from about 1578 for Hindus, Jains, Zoroastrians and Jesuits too; Abu'l-Fazl articulated sulh-i kull ("peace with all"); Sanskrit works went into Persian (the Mahabharata as Razmnama); an inscription on the Buland Darwaza (1601) gives a saying of Jesus that the world is a bridge to cross and not to build on [M]. Modern: Truschke, Culture of Encounters (2016); Moin, The Millennial Sovereign (2012). Comparison by debate did not yield sameness but a policy of peace. Historians doubt that Din-i Ilahi was a religion.
- Dara Shikoh: see 2.2. Also Mukalama-i Baba Lal wa Dara Shikoh, his dialogues with a Hindu ascetic (about 1653 [M]). Be fair: he rendered Brahman in Sufi words, and historians warn against making him a modern secular hero. Quran 18:60 does not name the figure Moses meets; Khidr is the tradition's identification.
- Kabir (fifteenth century, dates uncertain), a weaver of Varanasi who mocks Hindu and Muslim orthodoxy and points to a God beyond names, within. Widely attributed lines: the Hindu says Ram and the Turk says Rahim and they fight without knowing the secret; God is not in temple, mosque, Kaaba or Kailash but near you, as oil is in sesame and fire in flint; when the pot breaks the water in it merges with the water around it; the drop merges into the ocean, which all know, but few know the ocean merging into the drop [M as widely quoted]. The pot and water and the ocean in the drop are the best folk images for "the separation ends" and "the whole lives in each part". Sources: the Bijak, the Kabir Granthavali, the Guru Granth Sahib; Vaudeville, A Weaver Named Kabir (1993); Hess and Singh, The Bijak of Kabir (1983); Tagore and Underhill, Songs of Kabir (1915) worked from a Bengali collection (Kshitimohan Sen) and is not good evidence for what the early texts say. Write "attributed to Kabir".
- Guru Nanak (1469 to 1539) and the Guru Granth Sahib. Guru Arjan compiled it in 1604; Guru Gobind Singh's recension adding Guru Tegh Bahadur's hymns is usually dated about 1705 to 1706 [U]; the Guru-ship passed to the book in 1708. An earlier "final form 1678" is unsupported. It includes Gurus' hymns and those of about fifteen other saints (Kabir, Ravidas, Namdev, the Sufi Baba Farid): a scripture that is an anthology by design. It teaches one formless God (Ik Onkar), karma and rebirth, and release through the Name and living by God's order (hukam). A Guru Arjan hymn (Ramkali, about page 885 [U]) lists Ram and Khuda, pilgrimage and Hajj, Veda and Quran. "Na koi Hindu na Musalman" is a janamsakhi saying, not scripture [M]. Japji pauri 4, closing line (page 2): the body comes by karma and liberation by grace [M]. Kabir at page 1349 (from one light the world arose, who is good, who is bad) and Guru Arjan at page 1299 (none is my enemy or stranger) [U pages]. Sikh scholars reject "Hindu-Muslim synthesis" as the origin: the revelation came to Nanak.
- Ramakrishna (1836 to 1886). Said to have practised Tantra, Vaishnava devotion, Advaita under Totapuri, Islam (a few days, about 1866) and Christianity (about 1874), reaching the same realization by each [disciples' accounts, U]. Images: the roof reached by stone stairs, wooden stairs, bamboo or rope; one lake with many landing steps where Hindus, Muslims and Christians draw the same water and call it jal, pani and water [M]. The slogan yato mat tato path ("as many faiths, so many paths") is as remembered; the part-2 notes could not confirm it verbatim in the Kathamrita [U]. Sources: The Gospel of Sri Ramakrishna (Mahendranath Gupta; Nikhilananda's English, 1942), a disciple's diary in translation, so write "reported"; Saradananda, Lila Prasanga. Critics: the practices were short and inside a Hindu frame. The lake and the roof are the best "same thing, different words" images from someone who tried the paths.
- Vivekananda: see 2.2. Neo-Vedanta: Vivekananda (Jnana Yoga, "Maya and Illusion", 1896) calls maya a statement of the facts of experience and not a theory (recalled; U; do not print).
- Rammohan Roy (1772 to 1833). Belief in one God is the natural faith of all; rituals, idols and miracles are accretions; strip each religion to one God plus ethics. Tuhfat al-Muwahhidin (Persian, about 1804); Upanishad translations (1816 to 1819); The Precepts of Jesus (1820); the Brahmo Sabha (1828) and 1830 trust deed opening its house to all creeds [M]. The Enlightenment method, the opposite of Alex's, which keeps the strange parts (karma, lifetimes).
- Rabindranath Tagore (1861 to 1941): the infinite expresses itself in the finite and the finite returns by love; Sadhana (1913); The Religion of Man (Hibbert Lectures 1930, book 1931); Songs of Kabir (1915). Optional poetic voice.
- Sri Aurobindo (1872 to 1950). The One involved itself into matter and forgot; it evolves back through life, mind and supermind; the world is a real manifestation and not an illusion (he is not an Advaitin on maya); the soul (the psychic being) continues through lives and grows. The Life Divine (serial in Arya 1914 to 1919; book 1939 to 1940); The Synthesis of Yoga; Essays on the Gita; The Secret of the Veda; The Problem of Rebirth (1952). The nearest structural match to Alex's three clauses in this slice (involution, ignorance, evolutionary return, rebirth); also supports a staged scale like the arrow. On the levels above mind he lists Higher Mind, Illumined Mind, Intuition, Overmind, Supermind; the Mother announced on 29 February 1956 that the supramental had manifested [M] (P13, P16, P18).
- Gandhi. The ashram's daily prayer used Hindu hymns, Gita verses, Quran recitation, a Buddhist chant, a Parsi prayer and Christian hymns ("Lead, Kindly Light") [M]; religions are different roads to one point, all true and imperfect (sarva dharma sambhava); he changed "God is Truth" to "Truth is God" (1931). Hind Swaraj (1909), chapter on Hindus and Muslims (chapter X [U]); Young India; Harijan; the Dandi March bhajan with "Ishwar Allah tero naam" (who added the line U). His reading of the Gita as anasakti (non-attachment in action) bridges to Part one's letting go, and the bhajan "Vaishnava jana" (one who feels another's pain as his own) bridges to the fingers [M]. His Autobiography says the Key to Theosophy sent him back to his own scriptures [M gist, chapter U] (S3). Trap: many apocryphal English one-liners online; quote only from Young India, Harijan, Hind Swaraj or the collected works.
- Sarvepalli Radhakrishnan (1888 to 1975). The Hindu View of Life (1927); Indian Philosophy vol. 2 (1927); An Idealist View of Life (London: Allen and Unwin, 1932; Hibbert Lectures of 1929); Eastern Religions and Western Thought (1939); The Bhagavadgita (1948) and The Principal Upanishads (1953), each with Sanskrit, translation and notes setting Western parallels beside the text. He argues Shankara does not teach that the world is simply unreal and defends karma and rebirth in Western philosophical terms; ranked Advaita highest. The "cards" image (the past deals the hand, we play it freely) circulates credited to Nehru as well as to him; do not print under either name until a page is found [U]. An Indian comparer doing the Huxley job with notes and not a collage (G1-4, G2-3, P13).
- Hazrat Inayat Khan (1882 to 1927), The Unity of Religious Ideals (a volume of The Sufi Message, first printed 1929 [U]); Bhagavan Das (1869 to 1958), The Essential Unity of All Religions (Benares, 1932), an Indian Theosophist's version of the anthology method [contents U]. Do not cite for content until opened. Sri Yukteswar's The Holy Science (1894) sets out seven realms (lokas) in harmony of Christian and Hindu scriptures; Yogananda's The Second Coming of Christ (2004) was published after his death and edited by his organisation, so its wording is partly the editors' [M].

### 10.2 The Islamic world

- Rumi (Masnavi, Nicholson's numbering) [M for the stories, U for numbers]. Three stories. The elephant in a dark house (Book III near line 1259): people feel it and call it a fan, pipe, pillar or throne, and a candle in each hand would end the disagreement; the point is the limits of sense perception and not a plurality of religions. The grapes (Book II near 3681): four men share one coin, a Persian, an Arab, a Turk and a Greek, and quarrel because each names a grape in his own language (angur, inab, uzum, istafil); someone who knew all four languages would reconcile them. The cleanest "different words, one thing" parable. Moses and the shepherd (Book II near 1720 to 1820): God tells Moses to leave a shepherd's naive prayer alone; each community has its own idiom and God looks at the heart. Quote Nicholson (public domain in the UK; check later volumes in the US [U]) with book and line. A famous Divan poem saying he is neither Christian, Jew, Magian nor Muslim: whether it is his own is U, and Coleman Barks's English versions are loose. Rumi's lines on dying as mineral, plant, animal and man (Masnavi III, about 3901 to 3906) describe the soul's rise through forms; Chittick and others read them as the arc of ascent and not reincarnation or Darwin [M].
- Ibn Arabi (d. 1240). Tarjuman al-Ashwaq, poem 11 (Nicholson 1911): his heart has become capable of every form and he follows the religion of Love wherever its camels turn; the date is U (composed in Mecca about 1201 to 1202 or later); his commentary on the poems, usually called Dhakha'ir al-A'laq [U on spelling], says they are mystical, which the essay should not hide. Fusus al-Hikam (1229): do not tie yourself to one creed and reject the rest, God is too wide for one creed and each community worships the God of its belief (proof text Quran 2:115, wherever you turn is the face of God); the Adam chapter, where the Real wishes to see the essences of the Names in a comprehensive being, the human, so the One needs a mirror to know itself (the nearest classical statement of "a whole that wants to know itself cannot do it from nowhere"); the Noah chapter, that describing God only as beyond the world or only within it limits Him and the knower holds both. The cosmos is "He and not He", an imagination (khayal) real as self-disclosure (tajalli); the barzakh (Quran 55:19 to 20) is the pattern of the imagination; Corbin called the realm of images "imaginal". He denied tanasukh (return into this world) as most Muslims did and taught continual transformation after death; the descent and ascent arcs (nuzul, su'ud) of his school fit the start and return clauses [M]. Translations and studies: Austin (1980), Chittick (1989, 1994), Corbin (1958, English 1969), Izutsu (1983). "Unity of being" (wahdat al-wujud) is a later school's label; Sirhindi (1564 to 1624) offered "unity of witnessing", the oneness being in the seer's experience; the formula goes back, as recalled, to Ala al-Dawla Simnani (d. 1336) [M, U]. That reading is closer to "the separation ends in experience".
- Qur'an. 2:285: believers make no distinction among any of God's messengers. 5:48: to each community God gave a law and a way; had He willed, He would have made one community; so compete in good works; to God is your return, and He will tell you what you differed about (the clause "to God is your return" echoes the third axiom clause). 2:62 and 5:69: those who believe in God and the last day and do good have their reward. 2:115, 49:13. Classical commentators read these inside the Abrahamic frame, and some held 3:85 narrowed 2:62; pluralist readers include Ibn Arabi, Rumi, Shah-Kazemi and Ayoub. Do not write that 5:48 or 2:285 say the religions are one. Also 2:156 (to Him we return), 7:172 (the primordial covenant), 89:27 to 30 (O self at peace, return to your Lord) [M] (P13, P15, S5).

### 10.3 China, Korea, Japan

- The Chinese "three teachings are one" and the Confucian one body [M, sources U where marked]. The Xici commentary of the Yijing: under heaven the end is one and the thoughts many, the return one by different roads. A Song emperor, Xiaozong, is reported to have said: Buddhism for the mind, Daoism for the body, Confucianism for the world (usually traced to his Yuandao lun in the Fozu tongji [U]). Lin Zhao'en (1517 to 1598) founded a Three-in-One teaching in Fujian. Neo-Confucians borrowed from Buddhism: principle is one, manifestations many (Cheng Yi, Zhu Xi), one moon in all waters (from Yongjia Xuanjue). Zhang Zai's Western Inscription (1020 to 1077): Heaven is my father, Earth my mother, all people my siblings. Wang Yangming (1472 to 1529), Inquiry on the Great Learning (1527): the great person forms one body with Heaven, Earth and all things, selfish desire makes the mind small, and the pang at a child about to fall into a well shows it (Mencius 2A6). He is the best non-Western parallel to two fingers of one hand and to the small and big square, and he names the cause of separation. Caution: Timothy Brook (1993) says the formula was often rhetoric or joint worship and not a claim of identical doctrine; Berling, The Syncretic Religion of Lin Chao-en (1980). Confucian one body is graded love, close people first, and not numerical identity (Mencius rejected Mozi's equal love) (P13, P17). Check Wing-tsit Chan.
- East Asian Buddhist One Mind. The Awakening of Faith in the Mahayana (a Chinese text of the sixth century, attributed to Asvaghosha): One Mind has two gates, true thusness and arising and ceasing; unawareness stirs a thought, a world of separate things appears, and "initial awakening" returns to "original awakening"; the picture of wind moving ocean water into waves not apart from the water [M]. Zongmi (780 to 841) ranked Confucian, Daoist and Buddhist teachings in his Inquiry into the Origin of Humanity (about the 820s [U]) and argued that Chan and the doctrinal schools agree; Wonhyo (617 to 686) wrote on harmonizing doctrinal disputes from One Mind; Gregory, Tsung-mi and the Sinification of Buddhism (1991). The East Asian text closest to the three clauses, and an answer to "what about Buddhism". Limits: Critical Buddhism (Hakamaya, Matsumoto; Hubbard and Swanson, Pruning the Bodhi Tree, 1997) argues "one ground" thinking contradicts dependent origination and is non-Buddhist; Theravada and Madhyamaka do not accept a One Mind. Say "some Buddhist schools say it like this, and others dispute it".
- Japan [M]. From the eighth century shrines and temples merged. Honji suijaku says Buddhas and bodhisattvas are the original substance and the kami their local traces (Amaterasu with Dainichi; Hachiman as a bodhisattva); Ryobu Shinto and Sanno Ichijitsu Shinto built it out; in 1868 the state ordered the two separated (shinbutsu bunri) and attacks on Buddhism followed. Kukai (774 to 835) set out ten stages of mind (Jujushinron, 830), from animal appetite through Confucian and Daoist stages and the Buddhist vehicles in order to the Shingon stage, a staged scale that includes other traditions as earlier rungs, which is the design of the arrow. A Laozi phrase, "soften your light, join the dust" (wako doujin), was used to explain how Buddhas appear as kami. A mountain-and-moon proverb (many paths up, one moon at the summit) is often credited to Ikkyu [U]; the root, branches and fruit saying (Shinto root, Confucianism branches, Buddhism fruit) credited to Yoshida Kanetomo has an older form linked to Prince Shotoku [U]. Teeuwen and Rambelli, Buddhas and Kami in Japan (2003), show it was a hierarchy with Buddhas first; Kuroda argued "Shinto" as a separate religion is largely modern; Hakeda, Kukai: Major Works (1972).

### 10.4 Baha'i progressive revelation

The Founders are Manifestations of one God, like successive risings of one sun; their spiritual teaching is one and their social laws change with the age; humanity is like a child being educated. The soul begins at conception and goes on after death without returning to this world. Baha'u'llah, Kitab-i-Iqan (1861, leaning on Quran 2:285); Abdu'l-Baha, Some Answered Questions; Shoghi Effendi, Gleanings (1935) [M]. Baha'u'llah's Seven Valleys (about 1860, Baghdad) answers Attar's scheme (P18). The clearest "one source, many forms" with a time axis; it conflicts with Alex (it rejects reincarnation and ends with its own founder on top), and many Muslims reject the scheme, so an included tradition can refuse it [M] (P13).

### 10.5 What scholars say

[P13 §5, M.] Paul Hacker (died 1979) argued Hindu tolerance is mostly inclusivist: it absorbs the other and ranks it lower. The term comes from earlier work (a 1957 essay on religious tolerance and intolerance in Hinduism, as recalled [U]); "Inklusivismus" appeared in Gerhard Oberhammer (ed.), Inklusivismus: Eine indische Denkform (Vienna, 1983), a volume after Hacker's death, with an English selection in Philology and Confrontation: Paul Hacker on Traditionalism and Modernism, ed. Wilhelm Halbfass (SUNY, 1995); publication details U. Halbfass (India and Europe, 1988) and Richard King (Orientalism and Religion, 1999) say the Ramakrishna and Vivekananda universalism is a modern Neo-Vedanta shaped in dialogue with the West; Hacker's inclusivism is older than that modern form. The Gita verses fit Hacker's pattern. Recent historians (Gandhi 2020, Truschke 2016, Moin 2012) place Dara and Akbar inside Persianate ideas of sovereignty and mysticism and not as modern secular syncretists. Vaudeville, Lorenzen and McLeod question "synthesis of Hinduism and Islam" for Kabir and Nanak. Newbigin on the elephant; Prothero (God Is Not One, 2010) that the religions ask different questions. Brook on the three teachings; Critical Buddhism on One Mind; Teeuwen and Rambelli on honji suijaku. Fair summary (P01): the agreement is partly real, partly made, and the makers had a stake.

Rebirth among the reconcilers [P13 lead 12]: Hindus, Sikhs, Jains and Aurobindo keep it; Buddhists keep it without a fixed soul; Baha'is and mainstream Islam reject it.

Lines from the files' leads for the Part two paragraph [P13 §6, unchecked]: Dara in one paragraph (a prince sits with a Hindu teacher, finds a verbal dispute, and names his book after the meeting of two seas); Rumi's grapes as the plainest parable; Ibn Arabi's Adam chapter as the classical form of a whole that wants to know itself; Kabir's pot and water for "the separation ends"; Wang Yangming's child at the well for the fingers; Aurobindo's involution and evolution for the nearest structural match; the Awakening of Faith with the Critical Buddhism caveat; Kukai's ten stages beside the arrow; Gandhi's common prayer as practice; and one sentence that every comparer has a top, said before a reader says it. Rigveda 1.164.46, Gita 4.11 and Udana 6.4 are used for pluralism and none was written for it.

## 11. How the movement looks at the subject

### 11.1 The One, and why it becomes many

Source: P14 (main), with G1-7, P05, P13, P16, S3. All [M] or [U] unless marked; no wording verified.

The scheme. The perennial authors share a scheme more than a doctrine. It has three rungs and a return. At the top is a Source with no qualities that no name reaches: Brahman without qualities, Eckhart's Godhead, the Essence (Dhat), Ein Sof, the One of Plotinus, the nameless Tao and, in the comparers' reading, the Void or Dharmakaya. Below it is the same Reality turned toward the world as a Lord with names: Ishvara, God, Allah as Rabb, the Sefirot. Below that is manifestation: Maya, Existence, the many. The human self (Atman, the Intellect, the spark, "something uncreated") is where the Source is present in the creature, which is what "that art thou" points at. Then comes the return: moksha, fana, theosis, nirvana, reditus (P14 §1).

Four answers to "why does the One appear as many" (P14 §1).
1. Necessity: the Infinite cannot exclude anything, or Goodness overflows (Guenon, Schuon, Plotinus, Plato's Timaeus, Dionysius).
2. Desire, play or delight (Brihadaranyaka 1.4, Taittiriya, lila, Aurobindo, Watts, Wilber).
3. Relation: the Names need someone to be named to, or the Infinite withdraws to make room (Ibn Arabi, tzimtzum).
4. Refusal: at the top the question cannot be asked (Gaudapada, the Buddha, the Rigveda's creation hymn).
A second question, why I do not remember, gets its own answers: audacity (Plotinus), sleep and merging (Chandogya), hiding and recognition (Kashmir Shaivism). P14's advice: offer two kinds of answer and name them, a logic (the Infinite includes the possibility of the finite: Guenon, Schuon) and a motive (Brihadaranyaka 1.4, the Names, play). Alex's "a whole that wants to know itself needs a gap" is the motive kind; the Guenon and Schuon logic is a different one, a logic with no motive. State which he means. Smith's argument from infinity (7) is a third road to the same place.

Honest limits (P14 §1). The equations are the comparers' and not the traditions'. Buddhists, Jewish scholars, Orthodox and Catholic theologians and many Islamic scholars each keep a line between Source and many that the comparers erase. "Advaita" means "not two", which is not "one". Guenon fought Theosophy and Western reincarnation by name, and Schuon's school followed, so the two movements are not allies [M]. The first version's "by name" overstates it for Schuon [U].

The authors' own accounts (details in sections 3, 4, 7, 8).
- Guenon: the supreme Principle is beyond Being; manifestation follows from Infinity with no motive and no choice; the true Self is touched by none of the indefinitely many states. Specialists add: his Vedanta is Shankara's Advaita, one school among several, some of which read the world as real.
- Schuon: Absolute, Infinite and Maya inside Atma; three degrees; "fix the level before you equate".
- Coomaraswamy: one Self, the only transmigrant, lila; Pali scholars (Collins 1982, Rahula) reject his reading that the Buddha denied only the empirical self.
- Smith and Perry: four levels; Prothero (2010) argues the religions pose different problems; religious studies mostly treats the "primordial tradition" as a philosophy and not a finding.
- Burckhardt, Lings, Schaya, Nasr, Shah-Kazemi: the same grid on Islam and Kabbalah; Chittick's Ibn Arabi is the careful scholarly reading; Scholem and Idel do not read Ein Sof as Nirguna Brahman.
- Huxley's chapters 1 and 2 state the metaphysic, using Eckhart's Godhead and God to reconcile impersonal and personal traditions; for argument use Guenon, Schuon, Coomaraswamy. Underhill puts the Absolute, the ground and spark of the soul and a five-stage way at the centre; James lists the shared notes of mystic reports and stops short of metaphysics.
- Wilber: Spirit is ground and goal, involves into matter by stages of self-forgetting, then evolves back; the self-other boundary is drawn by the mind. Watts pictures God playing hide and seek with himself. Aurobindo: Brahman involved itself in matter for delight and evolves back through life, mind and supermind; the world is a real manifestation. Specialists add: Ferrer (2002) rejects Wilber's fixed hierarchy; Aurobindo is not an Advaitin on Maya.

The texts they lean on.
- The Upanishads on why [M, verse numbers M except Taittiriya U]. Chandogya 6.2.1 to 3: Being alone, one without a second, thought "may I be many". Taittiriya 2.6: he desired to be many and be born. Brihadaranyaka 1.4.1 to 4: the Self alone looked round, was afraid, then was not delighted, wished for a second, and divided himself in two, from which couples and then all pairs followed. Mundaka 2.1.1: as sparks leap from a fire, so beings come forth from the Imperishable and return into it. Gita 15.7: the individual soul is a fragment of the Lord's own self. Translators such as Olivelle stress that the Upanishads speak in many voices and that Advaita is one organised reading; Advaitins treat the Brihadaranyaka story as told from the level of appearance. The essay quotes 1.4.10 only; 1.4.1 to 4 is the most direct text on the why: loneliness, a wish for a second, self-division. It is a story with a motive, where Guenon and Schuon give a logic with none.
- The Upanishads on forgetting and the two birds [M]. Chandogya 6.9 and 6.10: as the honey of many trees does not know which tree it came from, and rivers do not know they were water from east or west, so creatures who merge into Being in deep sleep or death do not know it, and return as what they were, tiger, worm, gnat. This gives the traditional reason for "why you don't remember" and the link to lifetimes in one passage. Katha 2.1.10 and 11: one who sees plurality goes from death to death. Brihadaranyaka 2.4.14: where there is duality, as it were, one sees another. Mundaka 3.1.1 (and Svetasvatara 4.6, Rigveda 1.164.20): two birds on one tree, one eats, one looks on, the program and the processor. The line that the eater is freed from sorrow on seeing the other is the next verse, Mundaka 3.1.2 (Svetasvatara 4.7).
- Maya, lila, "never happened", recognition [M, Gaudapada's locator U]. In the Rigveda (6.47.18, quoted at Brihadaranyaka 2.5.19) Indra goes about in many forms through his maya, so maya first meant power or craft. Brahma Sutra 2.1.33 (lokavat tu lilakaivalyam) answers "why create" with lila, play; 2.1.32 is the objection (a creation without purpose), so do not cite 2.1.32 for lila. Shankara's gloss, as recalled, compares it to the unmotivated activity of a king with every wish fulfilled, or to breathing. Gaudapada's Karikas teach that nothing has ever arisen, so the question belongs to the level of appearance. Kashmir Shaivism says the Absolute is free consciousness (svatantrya) that manifests, conceals and grants grace, the world is a real appearance of it and liberation is pratyabhijna, recognition of what one already is. Hacker and others hold that maya weighs less in Shankara himself than avidya and name-and-form and that "illusion" is a later reading [U]. "The question cannot be asked" is the Advaita answer and should be named.
- Plotinus, Proclus, Dionysius, Eriugena: procession and return [M, Dionysius's locator U]. Plotinus's One is beyond being and thought and is all things and none (Enneads V.2.1); being perfect it overflows without will or loss (V.2.1; III.8.10 for the spring image); souls forgot the Father through audacity (tolma) and the wish to belong to themselves (V.1.1); Ennead IV.9 asks whether all souls are one; the return is the flight of the alone to the Alone (VI.9.11). Proclus (Elements of Theology, props 30 to 35) gives the triad remaining, procession, reversion. Dionysius (Divine Names 4) speaks of God's ecstatic love taking him outside himself in creating. Eriugena's Periphyseon (write only "ninth century"; the dating varies in the 860s to about 870 [U]) makes the whole a circle ending in nature that neither creates nor is created, to which all returns. For Plotinus the overflow is necessary, the world is good (against the Gnostics, II.9), and there are three real hypostases, so he is not a monist in Shankara's sense. Pope Honorius III condemned the Periphyseon in 1225 (the repo notes report the date). The essay's three clauses are the Neoplatonic triad of remaining, procession and reversion; Aquinas's Summa is often read in the same pattern. Ennead IV.9 and V.1.1 are the nearest Greek lines to the axiom (P14, P16).
- Eckhart [M for ideas; sermon numbers U]. God (Got) is what the Godhead (Gotheit) is in relation to creatures; in the Godhead there is no activity and no distinction. He prays God to rid him of God; God and Godhead differ as heaven and earth; God "becomes" when creatures say "God"; God's ground and my ground are one ground; there is something in the soul that is uncreated; the eye with which I see God is the eye with which God sees me; the way is detachment (abegescheidenheit) and breakthrough. In Quint's numbering roughly 52 ("rid me of God"), 56 (God and Godhead), 5b (God's ground and my ground), 13 (the uncreated something) and 12 (the eye), all U; Walshe and McGinn number differently, so cite the sermon's opening words and the translator's number. Sermon 52, "Blessed are the poor in spirit", where in his first cause he had no God, is the best Christian statement of the One becoming two (P15). He also puts a breakthrough into the Godhead after the birth of the Son in the soul, so the birth is not last (P18). The bull In agro dominico (27 March 1329, 28 propositions, 17 condemned as heretical and 11 as ill-sounding [split U], issued after his death) lists the "uncreated" saying; whether the "eye" saying was raised in Cologne in 1326, as the essay says, is U. McGinn (2001) and Turner (1995) read the Godhead as the Trinity's ground and the union as by grace, with the Creator and creature line kept; Otto found deep parallels with Shankara and deep differences.
- Ibn Arabi and the Sufi tradition [M]. God is incomparable (tanzih) and similar (tashbih) at once, and the knower holds both. The world is "He and not He". The Names need objects to be exercised, and the Breath of the Merciful brings the possibilities into being. The cosmos is God's self-disclosure. Later followers distinguish the Essence's absolute unity (ahadiyya) from unity in the Names (wahidiyya). Hallaj's "ana al-Haqq" (executed 922) is read as God speaking through an annihilated self. The hadith of voluntary devotion (Bukhari 6502) says God becomes the servant's hearing, sight and hand. "I was a hidden treasure" is not accepted as a hadith. The phrase wahdat al-wujud is, as recalled, from later followers and not Ibn Arabi [U]; Sirhindi (d. 1624) argued for unity of witness only; Ibn Taymiyya attacked the school; Chittick (1989, 1998) is the careful reading. "The Names need someone to be named to" is the best classical Islamic "why", and Part two already has the hidden-treasure caveat.
- Kabbalah and Hasidism [M]. Ein Sof, the Infinite, is beyond all description. In Luria's account (Safed, 1500s), Ein Sof withdraws (tzimtzum) to leave room, a ray enters, the vessels break, sparks fall, and human work repairs them (tikkun). Adam's one soul contained all souls. Cordovero held that God is all reality though not all reality is God (a standard summary of his panentheism and not a quotation [U]). Habad (Shneur Zalman) reads tzimtzum as apparent from our side only: from God's side nothing exists besides Him; say "close to acosmism in the Tanya's reading" and not "teaches acosmism", since whether Habad is strictly acosmist is debated [U]. Scholem (1941) read tzimtzum as a divine exile into self; Idel (1988) disputes his reading of devekut and Gnostic roots; the Lithuanian opponents of Hasidism feared pantheism. In Judaism separation is a real rupture to repair and not an illusion. Tzimtzum and the broken vessels say why a gap is needed and that the gap is a wound to repair; this fits the fingers better than Maya does.
- Buddhism [M]. Perennialists equate Dharmakaya, Buddha-nature or the Void with the Ground (Schuon, Suzuki, Huxley's Clear Light). The closest Buddhist texts are the Awakening of Faith (One Mind, two gates, ignorance arising as a sudden thought), the Tathagatagarbha sutras, Huayan, Dzogchen (the ground, gzhi, and the clear light at death, recognised or missed) and Yogacara (the storehouse consciousness carrying seeds between lives). The Buddha declined to say what began samsara, and Nagarjuna denies any intrinsic nature, including a One. Critical Buddhism (Hakamaya, Matsumoto; Hubbard and Swanson 1997) calls Buddha-nature a non-Buddhist "locus" theory; Theravada has no ground; Lopez (2011) shows Evans-Wentz's Tibetan Book of the Dead was shaped by Vedantic and Theosophical language. The Awakening of Faith has Alex's structure; say Buddhists would not call it a One.
- Taoism [M]. Tao Te Ching 1 (the Tao that can be spoken is not the constant Tao); 25 (something formless and complete before heaven and earth); 42 (Tao gave birth to One, One to Two, Two to Three, Three to the ten thousand things; a "One becomes many" line the essay lacks). Zhuangzi 2: heaven and earth were born with me and the ten thousand things are one with me. Zhuangzi 7: Hundun (Chaos), the Emperor of the Center, died on the seventh day when the rulers Shu and Hu (Watson: Brief and Sudden), of the South and North Seas, drilled one of the seven openings of a human face into him each day, to repay his hospitality; the cost of differentiation in one short story. Graham, Roth and Ames caution against reading Tao as a metaphysical Ground; Izutsu (1966) compares structures without claiming identity.
- Christian scripture and what the churches keep [M]. Romans 11:36, Acts 17:28, Ephesians 4:6, Colossians 1:17, John 17:21, 1 Corinthians 15:28 (God all in all); Athanasius, De Incarnatione 54, section 3 (God became man so that we might become gods). Luke 17:21 is "within you" or "among you", and translators split; Thomas logion 3 supports "inside" [U]. Orthodoxy keeps participation and not identity, with Palamas's essence and energies; the Fourth Lateran Council (1215, canon 2, against Joachim of Fiore) says any likeness between Creator and creature contains a greater unlikeness. Orthodox and Catholic theologians do not accept the perennialist mapping of Godhead and Trinity onto Beyond-Being and Being. Say "participation" for Christianity. Philippians 2:7 concerns the Son emptying himself and not creation; later thinkers extend it (Moltmann 1985 borrows tzimtzum; Weil says God could create only by hiding; Masao Abe compares kenosis with Buddhist emptiness, 1990). Do not write that Philippians teaches creation as self-limitation.
- Theosophy: the Proem's third proposition (2.4). Western echoes: Schopenhauer's tat tvam asi as the basis of compassion; Emerson's Over-Soul (public domain, quotable once checked).

Indian systems beyond Advaita (G1-7, all [M] or [U]; no wording verified).
- Kashmir Shaivism, the "Recognition school", about a thousand years old, is the closest technical match to Alex's three clauses that the files know. "Kashmir Shaivism" is a modern umbrella for Shaiva traditions of Kashmir from about the 9th to the 11th century (Trika, Pratyabhijna, Spanda, Kula, Krama); the label was popularised, perhaps coined, by J. C. Chatterji (Kashmir Shaivaism, 1914) [U]. Texts: Vasugupta (Shiva Sutras; Spanda Karikas attributed to him or to Kallata); Somananda (Shivadrishti); Utpaladeva (Isvara-pratyabhijna-karika, early 10th century); Abhinavagupta (Tantraloka, Tantrasara, Paramarthasara; c. 950 to 1016 in many works, c. 975 to 1025 in Sanderson's dating, so "about the year 1000"); Kshemaraja (Pratyabhijna-hrdayam, 20 sutras; Abhinavagupta's pupil). Translations: Jaideva Singh (Pratyabhijnahrdayam: The Secret of Self-Recognition, 1963); Christopher Wallis (The Recognition Sutras, 2017).
- The core story. The ultimate is consciousness (citi, Shiva) whose nature is light (prakasha) joined to self-awareness (vimarsha) and whose essence is freedom (svatantrya). Out of that freedom it manifests the universe. In sutra 2 it unfolds (unmilayati, "opens like an eye or a petal") the universe on its own surface (svabhitti: one's own wall, canvas or screen). The world is the shining-forth (abhasa) of consciousness in itself, not illusion and not a second thing. Same structure as Alex's two windows on one machine; the match is structural; check wording in Singh or Wallis.
- Why: no outside reason. The whole manifests because it is free, and the manifestation is play (krida, lila) and delight (ananda; Abhinavagupta's wonder, camatkara). The mirror: a city appears in a mirror as if outside it though nothing is in the mirror but the mirror. The school objects to Advaita that a light without self-awareness would be inert, so the Absolute must have vimarsha and freedom; for that reason it will not call the world maya in the sense of illusion. A stronger answer than "knowing needs a gap", but the school says the whole never actually loses itself and the hiding is part of the play, which sits slightly against Alex's clause that the whole forgets for a while; flag it.
- Five acts. Shiva's activity is fivefold (panchakritya): srishti (emission), sthiti (maintenance), samhara (withdrawal), a fourth act of concealment (tirodhana or tirobhava, vilaya or pidhana), and anugraha (grace). In the Trika concealment and grace are one freedom seen twice. Grace is shaktipata, the descent of power, graded and not earned (Tantraloka chapter 13; "nine grades" U). Kshemaraja's list of five functions (abhasana, rakti, vimarsana, bijavasthapana, vilapana) and its mapping to the five acts are [U]. Coomaraswamy's "The Dance of Siva" (title essay of the 1918 book) shows the five acts in the Nataraja image: the drum for creation, the open hand for preservation, the fire for destruction, the planted foot on the dwarf for concealment (which he also glosses as giving rest), the raised foot for release; his source is Tamil Saiva Siddhanta (Unmai Vilakkam, Tirumantiram) and not Kashmir. A sentence for the essay: the tradition counts five things the whole does: it shows, holds, withdraws, hides and gives itself back.
- The veil in layers. Three malas bind the experiencer: anava (the primal limitation, "I am only this"), mayiya (seeing oneself as other than the world and other people, bheda-buddhi) and karma (being a doer who acts for results and carries their residue). Five kanchukas (cloaks): kala (limits power to act), vidya (knowledge), raga (fullness, so desire appears), kala as time (eternity), niyati (omnipresence into cause and place); they limit five attributes of Shiva (all-doing, all-knowing, fullness, eternity, all-pervasiveness), not to be confused with the five shaktis; the two "kala" are different Sanskrit words. In Tamil Saiva Siddhanta (Meykandar, 13th century) anava is a beginningless substance clinging to the soul like husk on rice, the three bonds have the same names (anava, karma, maya) but the third is matter and not the stain of difference; so the match is by name and not by sense. Alex's wall, screen-distance and karma map roughly onto smallness, otherness and doing; the mapping is mine.
- Contraction and expansion. The key technical word of the Pratyabhijna-hrdayam is sankoca, contraction; the individual experiencer is consciousness itself in contracted form, called citta (the mind) when narrowed by its objects (sutras 4 to 6 [U on numbers]); the opposite word is vikasa, expansion; in the Spanda tradition unmesha (opening) and nimesha (closing), and the first Spanda Karika (1.1 [U]) says in gist that the world's arising and dissolving are the opening and closing of the Lord's eyes. Sutra 17 makes the expansion of the middle the way to the bliss of consciousness and sutra 18 lists means including the contraction and expansion of power [U]. Sankoca is first a narrowing of the scope of awareness and power, and only secondarily a clench in the body; the bodily reading comes from practice texts and modern translators. Use: the tradition's own words for "braced" and "released".
- Recognition as method. Pratyabhijna means re-cognition: bondage is non-recognition, liberation is recognising what one already is, and nothing new is acquired. Four upayas: anupaya (no means), shambhavopaya (will or intent), shaktopaya (discernment), anavopaya (breath, body, form). The Vijnana Bhairava gives 112 techniques (resting in the gap between breaths; meeting fear, grief, surprise or a sneeze with open attention); the Spanda Karikas teach finding the ground in a surge of strong emotion. In Abhinavagupta grace comes first. Sutra 19 (the second last of 20) says lasting samadhi comes by recognising the identity with consciousness again and again on coming out of samadhi, so practice enters ordinary activity. Liberation while living (jivanmukti) is accepted. "Recognition" fits clause three better than "return". Caveat: Sanderson and others stress that recognition is tied to initiation (diksha) and ritual, so a purely psychological reading is a modern repackaging; Torella and Ratie: a rigorous philosophy of self-awareness argued against Buddhist epistemology and not a loose monism. Rebirth is assumed; karma mala is the residue of action and grace can cut through it unearned. The school ranks other systems as partial views from lower levels of the 36 tattvas; whether Hacker applied "inclusivism" to Abhinavagupta is U. Do not cite Kashmir for the fingers: no place is known where it derives non-harm from oneness. The perennialist authors mostly lean on Advaita, Sufism and Christian mysticism and show no sign of using this school [U]; Coomaraswamy's Shaiva source is Tamil; English work on Kashmir existed before 1945 (Chatterji 1914; K. C. Pandey 1935 [M]) and the main run of translations is from 1963.
- Ramanuja (Vishishtadvaita). One reality, Brahman as Narayana; souls (cit) and matter (acit) are real, eternal, and exist as his body (sharira), he their self (sharirin); inseparability (aprthak-siddhi); "that thou art" says the "you" has Brahman as its self and does not erase the soul. Dates traditionally 1017 to 1137, some modern historians place him about a lifetime later, c. 1077 to 1157 [U]. Sri Bhashya; Thibaut, SBE 48 (1904). Studies: Carman (1974); Lipner (1986). He attacks Shankara's avidya with a long list of objections (see 11.2). I believe he says the soul's own knowledge is boundless but contracted (sankoca) by karma in bondage and expands (vikasa) in release [U, about 80 percent]. Karma: God presides over the fruits (Brahma Sutra 3.2.38). Return: life with God in Vaikuntha, no merging. Surrender (prapatti) is central (Gita 18.66); the Vadakalai (Vedanta Desika) say the soul clings like a baby monkey to its mother, the Tenkalai (Pillai Lokacharya, Manavala Mamuni) say it is carried like a kitten (Mumme, 1988). The Indian witness for "the many are real, inside one whole"; Shankara's school says a real many cannot be one. The monkey and the cat is a ready picture for Part three: do I grip or am I carried.
- The bhedabheda family. Bhaskara (c. 8th or 9th century): Brahman really transforms into world and souls, and souls are Brahman limited by conditions (upadhis) such as body and mind; liberation is the removal of those limits; the closest Indian version of "the whole, with a limit placed on it". Nimbarka (12th or 13th century): a natural difference and non-difference, like the sun and its rays, or a snake and its coils. Chaitanya (1486 to 1534) taught devotion to Krishna; Jiva Goswami (Sat-sandarbhas) and Baladeva Vidyabhushana (Govinda Bhashya) formulated acintya-bhedabheda, inconceivable oneness and difference; the soul is a minute part of Krishna's power, a marginal power (tatastha shakti) that may face the spiritual or the material. Forgetting causes bondage (Chaitanya-charitamrita Madhya 20.117 [U locator]); basis Vishnu Purana 6.7.61 [U]; Shikshashtaka 3 (humbler than a blade of grass) is a letting-go verse; the aim is prema, loving service, kept in separation as much as in union; merging is the lesser goal. It fits clauses one and two and not "the separation ends"; Alex should say so.
- The Yoga Vasistha (and its core, the Moksopaya). A Sanskrit dialogue between Vasistha and Rama in six books; the one consciousness (cit, Brahman) is all there is; the mind through its own intention (sankalpa) projects world after world, each containing minds that project further worlds; ignorance is the mind's holding to its own projection; liberation is dropping it, even while living. Stories fitting "many lives of one": Queen Lila, who with a goddess's help sees her dead husband living another life in another world; Gadhi, who dips into water and lives a whole life as an outcaste in moments; Bhusunda the crow, who remembers many cycles of creation. Seven stages of knowledge and seven of ignorance (list U; it is in P18). Book 2 argues self-effort (purusakara) beats fate, since fate is only earlier effort [U on chapters]: a strong match for the agency theme. Translations: Venkatesananda (1984, 1993); Aiyer, Laghu Yoga Vasistha (1896), and an Adyar (Theosophical Publishing House) edition of Aiyer's translation may exist, a bridge to the second movement [U]. Scholars (Slaje and others) date the original Moksopaya to about the 10th century in Kashmir, the longer Yoga Vasistha being later [M, 70 percent].
- Upanishadic knots. Katha 2.3.14 and 2.3.15 (desires lodged in the heart released, the mortal becomes immortal here; the knots of the heart cut; the first is the same verse as Brihadaranyaka 4.4.7) and Mundaka 2.2.8: a plain Sanskrit "let go" under every system above, matching the tension-as-knot picture [verse numbers U].

The contraction vocabulary elsewhere (G1-7). Cousins and not twins: one names the scope of knowing, one a mood sent by God, one a cosmic withdrawal, one a sluggish mind, one a bodily quality. Alex can use the family resemblance if he says which is which.
- Sufi qabd (contraction, constriction) and bast (expansion): two states (ahwal) of the heart sent by God, and two divine names, al-Qabid and al-Basit; Quran 2:245 says God contracts and expands. Al-Qushayri, Risala (1046), translations by Knysh (2007) and von Schlegell (1990); Hujwiri's Kashf al-Mahjub (Nicholson, 1911). They rank above fear and hope, which face the future, while qabd and bast face the present moment; accept the state and do not fight it, since the traveller did not cause it; in bast keep manners [M, 75 percent]. Rumi treats qabz and bast in Masnavi Book 3 [U]. Ibn Arabi's breath of the All-Merciful is the same rhythm at cosmic scale, an out-breath that relieves the Names' longing to show themselves and makes the world; an in-breath that gathers it again is not a standard part of the doctrine [U]. Use: a state comes, you do not cause it, you do not push it away.
- Lurianic tzimtzum, and katnut (smallness, constricted mind) and gadlut (greatness, expanded mind), Hasidic terms for the states of the human mind: the closest Jewish pair to braced and released. Tzimtzum is a cosmic act leaving a void; sankoca is a limit on awareness within a fullness never absent. Scholem (1941); Idel (1988, who argues the early sources may mean concentration and not withdrawal [U]); Elior on Habad (1993); Green, Ehyeh (2003); Matt, God and the Big Bang (1996).
- Pali sankhitta (contracted) and vikkhitta (scattered), in the contemplation of mind in the Satipatthana Sutta (MN 10, DN 22), in pairs with lust and without, hatred and without, delusion and without, developed and undeveloped, freed and unfreed [M]. Translators differ ("contracted and distracted", "shrunken and distracted"). The commentary (Buddhaghosa) glosses "contracted" as the mind with sloth and torpor and "scattered" as the mind with restlessness [M, 75 percent], so Pali "contracted" is a dull, shrunk mind and not Alex's tense one. Cite it with that caveat or leave it out.
