- Chinese song (loose, free of knotted tension): a named discipline in Taiji (loosen the waist, in Yang Chengfu's ten essentials [M, 85 percent]), and in modern qigong "relaxation and quiet" methods [U]; classical roots Daodejing 76 (the living are soft, the dead stiff) and Zhuangzi's fasting of the heart-mind and sitting and forgetting. As a technical term mostly later than the classics.
- Western cousins [M]: Cusanus (De docta ignorantia, 1440), God as complicatio (enfolding) and the universe as explicatio (unfolding); Bohm, enfolded and unfolded order (1980); Reich, expansion and contraction as the body's two basic movements [wording U]; Aurobindo's involution and evolution. Mention one, not all.

### 11.2 The veil, the ego and the way out

Source: P15 (never verification-checked; first pass without search) and G2-3 (technical theories of illusion), with P05, P03, P04. All [M] or [U]; no quotation is cleared.

#### The story and the vocabulary

The perennial writers tell one story in three steps (P15 §1). The diagnosis: a false centre has formed, a "me" that takes itself for the whole and lives braced against the rest. The cure: the false centre is seen through, set down or starved, and what was always there shows. The evidence offered: tradition after tradition prescribes the same operations (discern, detach, die to self-will, surrender) under different names.

The words differ more than the comparers admit. Hindu texts speak of avidya (not-knowing), maya (the measuring and veiling power) and ahamkara (the I-maker). The Buddha speaks of craving, clinging and the view that there is a self. Paul speaks of the old man. The German and English mystics speak of self-will and of "I, me, mine". The Sufis speak of the nafs. Each names something a little different and the comparers equate them. That is the main honesty point for Alex. The word "ego" is modern and psychological; Paul's old man, the Pali self-view and the Sufi nafs (appetite, and also a self at peace) are not the same thing. Theosophy uses Ego for the reincarnating individuality and personality for the passing self (Key to Theosophy, 1889), so Alex's ego is their personality [M].

Two kinds of explanation of why the veil feels real run through the sources. One is a fault of knowing, a superimposition, as when a rope is taken for a snake. The other is a fault of will, a clutch: craving, self-will. Most traditions carry both. Alex's machine picture suits the first; his BE axis, tension as the wall held up, suits the second, and the traditions' own images (knot, tangle, thread, rust) are about holding. The comparers also pass on a warning: dying to self can turn cold (Zen's fox, the Free Spirit heresy, quietism). The best material is mostly in the traditions' own texts and not in the anthologies (P15 §1).

#### Technical theories of illusion as a misreading of something real (G2-3)

The essay says an illusion is a wrong reading of something real and that the wall between programs is real inside the system. Every tradition below has a technical version of that sentence; they differ on what the real thing is and what exactly is misread. The closest fits are Yogacara's three natures (structure), Shankara's superimposition (claim) and Candrakirti's two kinds of conventional (for "real inside the system"). The fits fail in four places: maya is not the Yogacara imagined nature; Madhyamaka and Theravada have no One; strict Advaita says no one was ever bound; Ramanuja asks the hardest question, whose ignorance it is (G2-3 §1).

1. Shankara's adhyasa (Adhyasa Bhashya, introduction to Brahma Sutra Bhashya 1.1.1) [M; the space example and the list of rival definitions U]. The object ("you", the not-self) and the subject ("I") are as opposed as darkness and light, so neither can really be the other; yet people lay the traits of each on the other. That is adhyasa. His working definition: the appearing, in the manner of memory, of something seen earlier in another thing. He lists rival definitions and says all schools agree that something appears with another thing's traits. He calls it natural, beginningless and endless, a false cognition that drives agency and enjoyment; the wise call it avidya, and fixing the real nature of the substrate is vidya. All Vedanta texts exist to remove this cause of suffering (anartha-hetu) and give knowledge of the oneness of the Self; "cause of the round of births" goes beyond the passage and is the later tradition's reading. Examples: shell taken for silver, the double moon, space taken to be blue [U]. Translations: Gambhirananda (Advaita Ashrama, 1965); Thibaut (SBE 34, 1890; SBE 38, 1896 carries the later adhyayas [U]); Apte (1960) [U]. Closest classical wording for "a wrong reading". One difference: Advaita holds the silver is neither real nor unreal, while the essay's wall is a real fact.
2. Why it feels real [M; sutra numbers U]. First, the superimposition is natural and beginningless, so nobody has an earlier moment of clear sight to compare with. Second, an error needs a substrate that is partly known: you see the "this" and miss the "what", so the shell's brightness really is there. Third, appearances are ranked by whether a later knowing cancels them (badha, sublation): a lamp cancels the rope-snake; no ordinary knowing cancels the world, only knowledge of Brahman. An older criterion in the Gita (2.16) and Gaudapada (Karika 4.31) is constancy: what is absent at the start and end is absent in the middle. Brahma Sutra Bhashya 2.2.28 to 32 answers the Buddhist idealists with the sublation point: waking differs from dream because waking objects are not cancelled while one is awake. The Theosophical Glossary definition (what changes is maya) follows the constancy criterion and not Shankara's sublation test.
3. Three orders of reality: pratibhasika (dream, rope-snake, mirage, cancelled within ordinary waking life), vyavaharika (the shared, stable world of daily transactions, cancelled only by knowledge of Brahman) and paramarthika (Brahman, never cancelled) [M]. The wall between programs is vyavaharika: not a hallucination, not the processor's view. Shankara uses the pair vyavaharika and paramarthika (Brahma Sutra Bhashya 2.1.14 and elsewhere [U]); the clean three-tier scheme with the name pratibhasika is post-Shankara (the Vedanta Paribhasa of Dharmaraja Adhvarindra, 17th century, states it plainly, but do not call it that book's invention [U]). Handbooks: Chandradhar Sharma, A Critical Survey of Indian Philosophy (1960); Eliot Deutsch, Advaita Vedanta (1969). Schuon's degrees of reality are the modern parallel. Scholars warn against reading the three tiers back into Shankara himself.
4. Vivarta against parinama [M; Hacker's conclusion U]. Parinama is a real change of the cause into the effect (milk into curd); vivarta is an appearance of the effect with no change in the cause (rope appearing as snake). Advaita says Brahman is the material cause of the world only in the second sense; later Advaita adds that maya, as a power, does transform really while Brahman as substrate does not. Roots: Brahma Sutra and Bhashya 2.1.14 and Chandogya 6.1.4 to 6 (the modification is a name arising from speech; only clay is real); Paul Hacker, Vivarta: Studien zur Geschichte der Illusionslehre des Advaita-Vedanta (1953). Hacker's conclusion is that the strict contrast belongs to Shankara's successors [U]. Closest classical match to "one machine, many windows", with a care: a window on a real screen is a real change in the hardware (parinama); the essay's point is vivarta-like, the machine has never heard of the distance, so the distance is a trait laid on the machine.
5. Anirvacaniya and the map of error theories [M; locators U]. Advaita says the snake is neither real (it is cancelled) nor unreal (it was seen and caused fear), so indescribable, anirvacaniya. The formal doctrine was built by later Advaitins (Padmapada, Prakasatman, Sriharsha, Chitsukha are the names usually given [U]); the five definitions of mithya are set out in Madhusudana Sarasvati's Advaitasiddhi (c. 1600), drawing on earlier authors, and which author gave which is U. Indian philosophers put the same test case, shell-silver, to every school (khyativada): Advaita, anirvacaniyakhyati (the silver is made by avidya, neither real nor unreal); Nyaya, anyathakhyati (a real silver elsewhere is wrongly attached to the shell; Kumarila's variant is usually named viparitakhyati), the nearest to "a wrong reading of something real" taken literally; Prabhakara Mimamsa, akhyati (two true cognitions, this and a memory of silver, are not told apart); Ramanuja, satkhyati (the shell really holds a little silver); Yogacara, atmakhyati (the mind's own form is projected outward); Madhyamaka, asatkhyati (a non-existent appears). A one-paragraph device: "illusion as wrong reading" is one answer among six, and the Nyaya one suits the wall better than Advaita's indescribable silver.
6. Avarana and vikshepa [M; locators and attribution U]. Ignorance has two powers: avarana veils the substrate (the rope is not seen as rope); vikshepa projects the appearance (the snake). Post-Shankara Advaita: Vivekachudamani (attributed to Shankara, widely judged later), Drig-Drishya-Viveka, Panchadasi, Vedantasara. Not seeing the machine (avarana), then the screen picture on top (vikshepa); credit the vocabulary to later Advaita.
7. The analogy set [M; verse numbers M for Karika 3.3 to 8, U elsewhere]. Rope-snake and shell-silver: a real substrate misread, and the lamp that cancels it. Dream: a world made by the experiencer, ranked by sublation. Clay, gold, iron: effect not different from cause (Chandogya 6.1.4 to 6). Pot-space: space is one and the pot only appears to cut it (Gaudapada, Mandukya Karika 3.3 to 8; as recalled 3.3 the pot-space analogy, 3.4 pots dissolve into space, 3.5 the dust and smoke verse, 3.6 forms and functions differ but space does not, 3.7 the jiva is not a part or a modification of the Self, 3.8 the sky looks dirty to children; all U; an earlier draft put the dust verse at 3.8). When one pot is filled with dust or smoke the others are not, which answers why pain stays private; the nearest classical match to private memory in a shared machine. Advaita says the jiva is not a part of Brahman, which has no parts, and reads Gita 15.7 ("an eternal portion of me") as a part "as it were", so the fingers language fits Ramanuja better than Shankara. Reflection: the sun in water (Brahma Sutra 2.3.50, "only an appearance"); the Vivarana school reads the individual as a reflection (bimba-pratibimba), the Bhamati school as a limitation (avaccheda). Wave and ocean: Brahma Sutra Bhashya 2.1.13; Ramakrishna and Vivekananda use it for the many in one. Limit: the tradition teaches by superimposition and then retracts it (adhyaropa and apavada; Gita Bhashya 13.13, Vedantasara). An analogy holds for one point and is dropped.
8. Ramanuja's seven untenabilities (Sri Bhashya on 1.1.1) [M; order and Sanskrit names U]. Avidya cannot be made coherent: it has no possible locus (not the individual, whose existence depends on it; not Brahman, who is knowledge); it cannot veil Brahman's nature; its own nature (neither real nor unreal) is incoherent; "indescribable" is an empty category; no valid means of knowledge establishes it; the knowledge that removes it would itself be unreal; and its cessation is either real, which makes a second reality, or unreal. His own view: all cognition is veridical (satkhyati); world and selves are real, the body of God. Vedanta Desika (Satadushani) presses the same attack. Vyasatirtha (Nyayamrta) is a Dvaita (Madhva) author whose attack Madhusudana's Advaitasiddhi (c. 1600) answers; it is not a direct reply to Ramanuja, whose objections earlier Advaitins (Chitsukha is the name recalled) answer [U]. The first objection, "whose ignorance?", is the essay's open question: if there is only the whole, who is fooled? Same shape as the decombination problem (G2-2, not in this dossier). Advaita replies that avidya is beginningless and the question arises inside it. One honest sentence will do.
9. Gaudapada's caveat on the third clause [M; numbers U]. From the highest view there is no origination and no dissolution, no one bound, no seeker, no one released (Karika 2.32; 3.48). Bondage and liberation are within maya. So "the separation ends" holds for the individual's experience (jivanmukti); strict Advaita adds that at the highest level it never began. Say "ends, in experience" or flag the other reading. Sirhindi's unity of witnessing supports the same reading.
10. Yogacara: the three natures (trisvabhava) [M; verse numbers U]. Imagined (parikalpita), dependent (paratantra), perfected (parinishpanna). Vasubandhu's Trisvabhavanirdesa says: what appears is the dependent, since it arises from conditions; how it appears is the imagined, since it is only imagined; and the perfected is the constant non-existence of that "how" in the "what". Madhyantavibhaga 1.1: the imagination of what is unreal exists; duality is not found in it; emptiness is found in it, and it in emptiness. The magician example: a magician charms a piece of wood so it looks like an elephant; the appearance of the elephant is the dependent, the elephant is the imagined, the absence of any elephant in that appearance is the perfected; the wood itself is not one of the three natures [wording U]. The seventh consciousness, manas, constantly takes the base consciousness for a self and carries four afflictions (self-view, self-delusion, self-conceit, self-love; Trimsika 5 to 7). Translations: Anacker, Seven Works of Vasubandhu (1984); Kochumuttom (1982); Hopkins (1999). The best structural match to "wrong reading of something real": a real flow, a misreading laid on it, and freedom as the misreading's absence; the perfected nature is not added, the imagined is subtracted, which fits letting go. Lusthaus (2002), Kochumuttom and Gold hold Yogacara is not simple idealism.
11. Madhyamaka: two truths and samvrti as concealing [M; verse numbers beyond 24 U]. Nagarjuna, Mulamadhyamakakarika 24.8 to 10: the Buddha's teaching rests on two truths, the worldly conventional and the ultimate; those who do not see the difference do not see the Buddha's teaching; without the conventional the ultimate cannot be taught and without the ultimate nirvana is not reached. 24.18 equates dependent arising, emptiness and designation. Candrakirti, commenting on 24.8, derives samvrti three ways: as covering (ignorance covers the real nature of things), as mutual dependence, and as worldly usage. In the Madhyamakavatara (ch. 6, around verses 23 to 28 [U]) he distinguishes two kinds of conventional: the sound kind (what a healthy faculty perceives and the world can act on) and the false kind (the hairs a cataract shows, mirage, double moon). "Real inside the system" is Candrakirti's sound conventional: not a mirage, still empty of inherent nature. G2-3 prefers this to vyavaharika for its contrast with the false conventional. The caveat is large: Madhyamaka has no substrate, and what is misread is a dependent thing taken to have its own nature. Never use it to say Madhyamaka teaches oneness. Garfield (1995), Siderits and Katsura (2013), Huntington (1989). MMK 25.19 (no difference between samsara and nirvana). Gelug scholars hold Madhyamaka rejects paratantra as ultimately real [M].
12. Innate against conceptual: the rubber hand in three Buddhist schools and in Advaita [M; Cheng Weishi details U]. Four traditions separate a belief that argument can remove from a habit that only practice wears down. Pali: the stream-enterer drops identity view, but the conceit "I am" remains until arahatship; in the Khemaka Sutta (SN 22.89) Khemaka says he regards nothing among the five aggregates as self or as belonging to self, yet the conceit "I am" has not been uprooted in him; the elders' answer is a simile of a cloth washed with salt earth, lye or cow dung that keeps a residual smell until it is put in a sweet-scented casket; the sutta does not name his attainment, and the label "non-returner" is the commentary's [M; wording unchecked]. Yogacara: the Cheng Weishi Lun (Xuanzang, 659, on Vasubandhu's Trimsika) divides self-clinging into co-born (sahaja) and discriminated (parikalpita); the second goes at the path of seeing, the first only by long cultivation. Gelug: Tsongkhapa (Lamrim Chenmo, 1402) distinguishes innate (lhan skyes) from acquired (kun btags) self-grasping; the innate is shared with animals and does not leave when the argument is understood; after insight the appearance of inherent existence continues but assent is withdrawn, like an illusion. Advaita: badhitanuvritti, the continuing of a cancelled appearance, as the double moon does after one knows; the jivanmukta continues like a potter's wheel after the push (Samkhya Karika 67; Brahma Sutra Bhashya 4.1.15 [U]). The clean answer to the rubber hand: the feeling of a wall does not respond to being told it is a model. The Western parallel is the Muller-Lyer illusion. One difference: the traditions claim practice can wear the habit down, which cognitive science does not show.
13. The Buddhist wave and ocean [M]. The Awakening of Faith in the Mahayana (Dasheng qixin lun, Chinese, 6th century, traditionally credited to Asvaghosha; tr. Hakeda, 1967): wind moves ocean water into waves not apart from the water; when the wind stops the waves stop and the water stays; ignorance is the wind, the mind's nature the water. The Lankavatara (tr. Suzuki, 1932) has the base consciousness as an ocean stirred by the wind of objects. A Chinese synthesis (Tathagatagarbha with Yogacara); Theravada and Madhyamaka do not accept a One Mind.
14. Ibn Arabi: He and not He, imagination, barzakh [M]. See 10.2. Chittick and Schuon set "He/not He" beside maya as neither real nor unreal; Izutsu sets Ibn Arabi beside Laozi and Zhuangzi. The best Islamic witness for "real, but not what it looks like", since it keeps both statements true.
15. Plato and Plotinus [M; Plotinus sub-chapters U]. Republic 509d to 511e (the divided line: the visible splits into images and things, the intelligible into reasoning and direct insight, in the same proportion); the Cave, 514a to 517a for the image, its reading running on to about 521b; Timaeus 27d to 29c (what always is, and what only becomes); Sophist 240a to b (a likeness is not the real thing it likens, but it really is a likeness): the nearest Western wording of "real, in its own order". Plotinus: Intellect, Soul and the sensible world descend as images of images, matter last and barely real (Enneads I.8, III.6), yet the cosmos stays a good image, against the Gnostics who called it a prison (II.9). One sentence.
16. Bradley, Kant, Schopenhauer: the Western path of the word [M]. F. H. Bradley, Appearance and Reality (1893; 2nd ed. 1897): appearance is not nothing, because it appears; it belongs to reality, which is one; things come in degrees of truth and reality; each finite centre of experience is closed to the others. A passage that T. S. Eliot cites in his note to The Waste Land (line 412, Bradley p. 346) says each person's experience falls in a circle closed on the outside, every sphere opaque to the others [wording U, do not print until checked]; a public-domain sentence for the memory wall. Kant (Critique of Pure Reason, B69 to 71) separates appearance (Erscheinung) from illusion (Schein), which defends the essay's care with "illusion". Schopenhauer brought the Upanishads' veil of Maya into European philosophy (World as Will and Representation, vol. 1, sections 3 and 63 [U]) through Anquetil-Duperron's Oupnek'hat (1801 to 1802). Paul Deussen, The System of the Vedanta (1883), read Shankara's two standpoints as Kant's empirical and transcendental. Halbfass and Hacker say Schopenhauer and Deussen shaped the English sense of maya as "illusion".
17. The perennial authors on maya [M; works U]. All three Traditionalists reject "mere illusion". Guenon: maya is the relative nature of manifestation, real in its own degree (Introduction, 1921; L'Homme et son devenir, 1925; a chapter "Maya" in Etudes sur l'hindouisme [C per P02]). Schuon: maya is the possibility of the relative inside the Infinite, neither being nor non-being, tied to the Sufi "He and not He". Coomaraswamy: the divine power that measures the one out into forms (root ma), the artist's skill, and the error is taking the made thing as self-existent. Textual root: Rigveda 6.47.18, used in Brihadaranyaka 2.5.19, and Svetasvatara 4.9 to 10 (maya is nature, the Lord the maya-wielder); Gita 18.61 has the Lord turning all beings by maya "as if mounted on a machine", which suits a machine metaphor. The Vedic sense (power, craft) beside Shankara's (superimposition) shows "illusion" as a late narrowing. Radhakrishnan (Indian Philosophy vol. 2, 1927; The Hindu View of Life, 1927) argues Shankara does not teach that the world is simply unreal: it is real as the manifestation of the Absolute, without independent reality, and maya is a principle of relativity. Vivekananda (Jnana Yoga, "Maya and Illusion", 1896) calls maya a statement of the facts of experience and not a theory [recalled sentence U, do not print]. Halbfass, King and Rambachan class both as Neo-Vedanta, partly faithful and partly a reply to missionary charges of world-denial. Shankara does use maya (Brahma Sutra Bhashya 2.1.14 calls name and form the maya-power of the Lord, as recalled [U]); Hacker's point (a 1950 article in the ZDMG on avidya, namarupa, maya and isvara [M title, U pages]) is that avidya, adhyasa and namarupa are his working terms and the maya doctrine as a system is later. Keep Shankara's mithya in view: he did rank the world lower. Specialists reject equating maya with the Yogacara imagined nature or the Madhyamaka conventional.
18. The Theosophical Glossary, Blavatsky and the sheaths [M structure; wording U]. The Theosophical Glossary (1892, ed. G. R. S. Mead, assembled from her notes and published after her death in 1891) defines Maya in outline as illusion, the cosmic power that makes phenomenal existence possible, with only the changeless counted real (opening wording U, do not print). The Secret Doctrine, vol. 1, has a sentence that maya is an element in all finite things, since everything has only relative reality depending on the observer's power of cognition (wording and page U, around p. 39 of the 1888 edition [U]). The Theosophists read the Vedic sheaths (annamaya, pranamaya, manomaya, vijnanamaya, anandamaya) and the three bodies as steps in a human constitution of seven principles with after-death stages; T. Subba Row proposed a Vedantic classification (three upadhis plus Atma) and resisted Sinnett's Esoteric Buddhism. In Advaita the sheaths are layers of misidentification to be discriminated away (neti neti). Mulaprakriti as the veil over Parabrahman is, to an Indologist, a mix of Samkhya prakriti and Advaita maya. Quote the Glossary as one voice only; Guenon (Le Theosophisme, 1921) and Indologists object to the septenary count and the after-death map.

A device Alex can borrow (G2-3 §4): Indian khyativada put one example (shell-silver, rope-snake) to every school and asked what each calls real in it; Alex can line up the traditions on one case, such as a dream, a mirage or a program's memory wall. The equation of maya with parikalpita or samvrti with vyavaharika is quick and is where specialists object most. Check first (G2-3 lead 9): the Adhyasa Bhashya wording; Madhyamakavatara 6.23 to 28; Khemaka (SN 22.89, simile and the commentary's label); Trisvabhava verses 2 and 3 and the elephant verses; Karika 3.3 to 8 (which number is the dust verse); 2.32; Bradley p. 346; the Glossary entry; the Secret Doctrine sentence; Hacker's date for "inclusivism".

#### The way out (P15)

- The anthologists and the Traditionalists. Huxley: the obstacle to knowing the Ground is the personal ego, called self-will, and the cure is subtraction; mortification is dying to self-will and not hurting the body; non-attachment is the root of freedom (chapters on mortification and non-attachment, self-knowing, grace and free will); he leans on Law, Eckhart, John of the Cross and the Gita. Perry's contents unknown. Smith: the four yogas of knowledge, devotion, work and meditation as four temperaments, the cleanest "ways" list. Guenon: the separate "ego" is one limited state of a larger being, the error is identifying with the part, the cure is metaphysical realization, which needs initiation (Man and His Becoming, 1925; Perspectives on Initiation, 1946). Schuon: discernment of Real from unreal, concentration on the Real through a Name (japa, dhikr, the Jesus Prayer, the nembutsu), and virtue, humility first; the best compact method list in the movement; call it his scheme. He treats maya as relativity with a veiling and a projecting aspect, echoing the Advaita avarana and vikshepa [doctrine M, Schuon's use U]. Coomaraswamy: every tradition says to deny, forget or sacrifice the small self so the Self may be known ("Self-Naughting", "Atmayajna": Mark 8:34, John 12:25, the Vedic sacrifice, Eckhart). Huxley and Perry show the theme exists in every tradition and not why.
- Wilber, Watts, James. Wilber: suffering comes from drawing a boundary between self and not-self, and the Atman project is the drive to regain wholeness through substitutes because the real goal is unseen; this maps closely onto Alex's Default, Driven and Drifting rooms. Watts (The Book, 1966): the "skin-encapsulated ego" is a social agreement taught to each child. James (Varieties, lectures 8 to 10, the divided self and conversion): the crisis often resolves when effort gives out and the person surrenders, a respectable Western witness that release follows the failure of grip, which is the shrinking swing.
- The Theosophists. Blavatsky, The Voice of the Silence (1889: the Voice of the Silence, the Two Paths, the Seven Portals) says the mind slays the real and the disciple must slay the slayer [recalled near-verbatim, not for quotation] and contrasts private liberation with a path of renunciation taken for the world's sake; the Two Paths parallels the gate in Part one, a reason bigger than yourself. The Voice's portals are dana, shila, kshanti, viraga, virya, dhyana, prajna: the six Mahayana perfections with viraga (indifference) inserted [M] (P18).
- Hindu texts [M unless marked; numbers moderate]. The Upanishads tie separateness to fear and desire: Brihadaranyaka 1.4.2 and 1.4.3 (the One alone is afraid, reasons that there is nothing but itself so what to fear, then wants a second and divides in two); 2.4.14 (where there is duality, one sees another); Taittiriya 2.7 (fear arises when a person makes the slightest gap); Mundaka 2.2.8 and Katha 2.3.15 (the knot of the heart is cut); Katha 2.3.14 (desires shed, the mortal becomes immortal here); Chandogya 8.7 to 8.12 (Virochana is content with the body's reflection as the Self, Indra keeps asking). The Gita and Yoga Sutras tie the way to dropping the "I-doer" and the "mine": Gita 3.27 (the one deluded by ahamkara thinks he is the doer), 2.71 and 12.13 (free of "mine" and "I"), 18.66. Yoga Sutras 1.12 (practice and dispassion together, the oldest compact statement of work and stillness and Part three in one sentence), 2.3 to 2.6 (the five afflictions, among them avidya and asmita), 2.45 (surrender to the Lord), 4.3 (the cause does not push nature, it removes the barrier, as a farmer opens a channel to water already flowing). Shankara: rope and snake.
- Buddhist texts including Zen [M]. Pali: Samyutta 22.59 (of each aggregate: this is not mine, this I am not, this is not my self); the ten fetters, where self-view goes at stream entry and the conceit "I am" only at the end; Samyutta 22.89 (Khemaka: no self seen in the aggregates, yet the "I am" lingers like a faint scent in washed cloth); Majjhima 22 (the raft, the teaching itself set down after use); Samyutta 1.23 (the tangle inside and outside, number moderate); Digha 27, Agganna Sutta (luminous beings come down by tasting the earth, grow heavy, then divide the land and call it theirs: a Buddhist fall story). Mahayana: the Awakening of Faith. Zen: Dogen, Genjokoan (c. 1233), to study the Way is to study the self, to study the self is to forget the self; the ten ox-herding pictures (the eighth an empty circle, the tenth a return to the marketplace); Mumonkan 2 (Hyakujo's fox: an old teacher said the enlightened do not fall into cause and effect and was a fox for five hundred lives; the right answer was that they do not obscure it); the mirror verses of Shenxiu and Huineng (Huineng's "originally there is not a single thing" is from later editions of the Platform Sutra, the Yuan edition of 1291, and not the Dunhuang manuscript, so quote from a named edition). Khemaka says the wall is soft yet a trace stays; the fox is the tradition's own warning against bypassing.
- Taoism [M]. Tao Te Ching 48 (learning adds daily, the Way subtracts daily until non-action); 16 (reach utter emptiness, hold stillness, things return to their root); 19 (see the plain, hold the uncarved, lessen the private and the wanting); 13 (great trouble comes from having a "self", where the Chinese can mean body). Zhuangzi 2 opens with Ziqi saying he has lost his me (wu sang wo); chapter 4, the fasting of the mind; chapter 6, sitting and forgetting. Slingerland, Effortless Action (2003): wu-wei is both an ideal and a paradox, since effort cannot reach effortlessness; the answer in Confucian and Taoist writers is long cultivation until it turns spontaneous (Analects 2.4). The paradox of trying not to try is the exact snag in "letting go as a skill" and suits the hand-off to Part three.
- Jainism [M]. Tattvartha Sutra 1.1 (right view, knowledge and conduct together are the path), with influx, stopping and shedding of karma as fine matter; the four passions (anger, pride, deceit, greed) as the glue; the five great vows ending in non-possession; samayika (a fixed period of equanimity, traditionally 48 minutes [U]); kayotsarga. The knowledge-obscuring karma is the veil. Jain "maya" means deceit, not cosmic illusion; Jain souls are many and liberation is each soul's isolation. Jainism agrees on the practice and objects to the One; say that plainly instead of claiming Jainism as a witness to the One.
- Christian scripture [M]. Paul: Romans 6:6 (old self crucified), 7:15 to 24 (the divided self), Galatians 2:20, Ephesians 4:22 to 24 and Colossians 3:9 to 10 (put off the old man), 1 Corinthians 15:31 (I die daily), Philippians 2:7 (kenosis). Jesus: Mark 8:34 to 35, John 12:24 to 25, John 3:30, Matthew 6:25 to 34. Return: Luke 15:11 to 32, where verse 17 says the prodigal "came to himself" (Greek eis heauton elthon) and the father runs to meet him, a gentle sentence for waking from forgetfulness. Babel (Genesis 11) and Pentecost (Acts 2) are read together in church tradition as scattering and gathering. Paul's old man is not the modern ego; it is a life under sin and flesh.
- Christian mystics and the Eastern Church [M unless marked]. Theologia Germanica (anonymous, 14th century; Luther edited it in 1516 and 1518): the fall of the devil and of Adam was claiming "I", "me", "mine"; sin is self-will. Boehme, The Way to Christ (1622): "Of True Resignation", "Of the Supersensual Life". William Law (1686 to 1761), A Serious Call (1729): fallen nature as self-will and self-love [titles U]. Eckhart: On Detachment (authorship usually accepted, not certain [U]); Sermon 52. The Cloud of Unknowing (14th century): a cloud of forgetting under all creatures [chapters U]. John of the Cross, Ascent of Mount Carmel: attachment is a thread that holds a bird as well as a cord (1.11); desire nothing to have all (1.13) [numbers moderate]. Ignatius, Spiritual Exercises 23: holy indifference. Marguerite Porete was burned in Paris in 1310 for The Mirror of Simple Souls (the annihilated soul); Eckhart's trial ended in the bull of 1329. Moderns: Merton, New Seeds of Contemplation (1961), false and true self; Keating, Open Mind, Open Heart (1986), the false self built on programs for happiness (security and survival, affection and esteem, power and control), which maps onto Alex's rooms. Eastern Church: Evagrius on apatheia (dispassion, not apathy) as the fruit of practice and parent of love; the Jesus Prayer.
- Sufism [M]. The veil is forgetting, the self has stages, and the way ends in passing away and subsisting. Quran 59:19 (do not be like those who forgot God, so God made them forget their own selves); 12:53 (the self that commands evil), 75:2 (the blaming self), 89:27 to 30 (O self at peace, return to your Lord, well pleased and well pleasing, the Quran's own return sentence); 83:14 (what they earned has rusted on their hearts, the base of the heart-as-mirror image). Qushayri, Risala (1046): stations and states, fana and baqa; Hujwiri, Kashf al-Mahjub (the unveiling of the veiled, 11th century); Ghazali, Mishkat al-Anwar, on veils of darkness, of mixed light and darkness, of pure light [moderate]; Junayd read fana as a return to the state before existence, tied to the covenant of 7:172 [wording U]. Bistami's "Glory be to me" and Hallaj's "I am the Real" (executed 922) are the ecstatic cases orthodoxy handled with care. "Die before you die" is cited by Sufis as a saying of the Prophet, which hadith critics do not accept [verdict U].
- Jewish thought [M]. Bittul (nullification) and ayin (nothing), taught by the Maggid of Mezritch (d. 1772) as making oneself nothing so the divine can flow through; Exodus 16:7 ("what are we", mah) and Job 28:12 read that way in Hasidic sources. R. Nachman, Likutei Moharan II:48: the whole world is a very narrow bridge and the main thing is to have no fear at all (the best one-sentence Jewish statement of the BE axis) [M strongly, paraphrase]. Talmud, Tamid 32a: what shall a man do to live? let him put himself to death [page moderate].
- Greek and Roman voices [M]. Plato, Republic 7 (514a to 520a): the cure for the cave is a turning round of the whole soul (518c to d), and the freed prisoner must go back down (519c to 520e). Phaedo 64a, 67d to e: philosophy is practice for dying. Symposium 189c to 193d (Aristophanes): once whole, split, and love is the pursuit of wholeness. Plotinus, Enneads 1.6.9 (if you do not yet see yourself beautiful, be a sculptor and cut away until the statue shines; the clearest picture of letting go as subtraction) and 6.9.11. Plutarch (a fragment in Stobaeus): to die and to be initiated are alike, in the Greek verbs teleutan and teleisthai [locator unknown]. Marcus Aurelius 2.1 (made for cooperation like feet, hands, eyelids), 8.34 (the severed hand or foot is what a man makes of himself when he separates, yet he can grow back into the whole; a Stoic fingers image from a man Part one already names, with the return built in), 11.8 (a branch cut from its neighbour is cut from the tree) [numbers moderate].
- Dying before dying, the comparers' list [M; each line the comparer's equation]. Phaedo; Paul (I die daily; baptism as burial, Romans 6:3 to 4); Jesus (lose life to find it); Sufi fana; Zen's Great Death; Hindu jivanmukti (Katha 2.3.14; Brihadaranyaka 4.4.7, the snake's shed skin) and the sannyasa initiation with its own funeral rite; Tamid 32a; Hasidic bittul; Zhuangzi's forgetting; initiations that act out death and rebirth (Eliade, Birth and Rebirth, 1958; Guenon). The modern case is Ramana Maharshi's 1896 account, at sixteen, of lying as if dead and finding the I remained.
- Grace and effort [M]. The split runs inside every tradition. Katha 1.2.23 and Mundaka 3.2.3 (the Self is not gained by teaching or intellect but by the one it chooses, and it uncovers its own form); Gita 10.10 to 11 and 18.66; Yoga Sutras 1.12 and 2.45; Sri Vaishnavism's monkey school against its cat school; Pure Land, Shinran's other-power (tariki) against self-power (jiriki); Paul and Augustine against Pelagius, with the Orthodox synergy of divine and human work; Sikh nadar, the glance of grace; Huxley gives it a chapter. The traditions that teach consequence also teach a way out that is not payment: Japji 4, Pure Land other-power, Paul's grace (Ephesians 2:8), the Islamic mercy that outruns wrath, the Jain verse of forgiveness; Alex says repair undoes karma, so forgiveness is the partner of repair (P16 §24).
- Shared images [M]. Veil (avarana; hijab); cloud (Cloud of Unknowing; Jain karma on the sun); knot (Mundaka 2.2.8) and tangle (Samyutta 1.23); rust (Quran 83:14) and dust (Platform Sutra) on a mirror; a thread on a bird (John of the Cross); a rope taken for a snake (Shankara); sleep (the Buddha is "awakened") and intoxication (Sufi ghafla); amnesia (Plato's recollection; the river of forgetting in the Myth of Er; the Hymn of the Pearl; Quran 7:172, 59:19); exile and the way home (galut; the reed's lament that opens Rumi's Mathnawi; the prodigal); the cave; the fall (Genesis 3, Phaedrus, Agganna); sea and waves (Awakening of Faith, Rumi). A caution the comparers rarely make: some are fall stories (a lapse) and others are play or overflow stories (lila; Plotinus; the hidden treasure; the Kabbalists' contraction). Alex's axiom belongs to the second family, so Genesis 3 is a weak witness unless he says how he reads it; say which family he means.
- Why the same practice appears everywhere: the comparers' stated reasons [P15 §17]. (a) Huxley: one Ground, one obstacle (self-will), so one cure by subtraction, and knowing depends on the knower's being. (b) Guenon and Schuon: one Principle and one human constitution, so the practice is a method derived from what the Real is and what man is [wording U]. (c) Coomaraswamy: the same truth held everywhere and always, and Eckhart reached it without India. (d) James: a shared psychology, the subconscious region where crisis and release happen. (e) Stace: one core experience, many interpretations. (f) Wilber: shared deep structure, different cultural surfaces. (g) Campbell: one pattern of separation, initiation and return in myths worldwide (English "atonement" was built from "at one"; coiner U). (h) A neuroscientist's reason would be a shared brain: differences in the default mode network in long-term meditators (Brewer and colleagues, 2011) and measured "ego dissolution" under psychedelics (Nour and colleagues, 2016) [small and contested]. Alex's method (derive from one axiom, then show fit) is closest to (b) and not to Huxley's subtraction; say so.
- Scholars on the way out [P15 §5, M]. Katz: no experience is free of the tradition that shapes it, so Buddhist and Christian ends differ. Zaehner: Sufi fana and Vedantic identity differ. Eckhart specialists (McGinn, Turner, Flasch) read him inside Dominican and Neoplatonic theology; Turner treats apophatic language as a Christian discipline and not a generic mystical experience. Chittick: "unity of being" is mostly not Ibn Arabi's own phrase. On not-self, Rahula reads the Buddha as denying any self, the Rhys Davids and Coomaraswamy as denying only the small one, and Thanissaro calls it a strategy for letting go and not a metaphysical claim. Critical Buddhism calls Buddha-nature thought a substance-monism that is not Buddhist (interdependence is not a One). Hindu schools read the Upanishads three ways (Advaita, qualified non-dual, dual), so even tat tvam asi is disputed. King, Hanegraaff and Ferrer call the perennial frame Western and monistic; Sharf calls meditative "experience" talk modern rhetoric. Weber's typology (inner-worldly ascetic against world-fleeing mystic) places Alex's work-with-stillness path as the inner-worldly kind.

### 11.3 Lives, the soul and karma

Source: P16 (checked copy, no search), with P02, P03, P04, P05, P06, P08, P10, G1-3. Nothing from this file goes into the essay as a quotation, verse number, chapter number or date until someone opens the work. [M] and [U] as marked.

The picture in brief (P16 §1). On lives and karma the perennial camp is split, and the split is the useful fact. There are five camps.
1. The strict Traditionalists (Guenon, and after him Schuon, Nasr, Lings, Burckhardt) deny reincarnation in the Western sense, the return of the same individual to an earthly life. Guenon calls it a nineteenth century error born in Spiritism and Theosophy [M]; the others follow him because they hold the same Islamic or Traditionalist position [U]. They teach instead that a being has many states, that no state is repeated, and that Hindu and Buddhist talk of rebirth is a lower or popular way of speaking about this. Mainstream Islam and Christianity teach one earthly life and then an afterlife (barzakh or purgatory, resurrection, judgement); Guenon and Schuon read that afterlife as passage to other states; the religions themselves do not say it in those terms [M].
2. Coomaraswamy keeps transmigration but changes the question: the one who goes from life to life is not the individual but the one Self, the only transmigrant. The nearest thing in the literature to Alex's axiom.
3. The Theosophists (Blavatsky, Olcott, Sinnett, Besant, Judge) put reincarnation and karma at the centre, with a soul that carries the harvest of each life into the next.
4. Huxley, Smith and Wilber do not make rebirth part of the common core. Jung translates it into psychology.
5. The evolutionary reincarnationists (Aurobindo, Steiner).
They disagree for five reasons: a metaphysical argument (nothing is repeated); loyalty to an Abrahamic home religion; a polemic against modern ideas of progress; a quarrel over which layer of a tradition counts as the real teaching; and a quarrel over who or what is the subject that continues. They agree on more than they admit: the ego is not the final self, the being has more room than one short life, action has consequences that outlast the act, and the aim is the same return. Alex's axiom need not take a side, because on his axiom the one who lives every life is the whole.

The map of positions (P16 finding 1), to state in one plain paragraph: (a) the same individual returns (popular Theosophy, Hindu and Buddhist folk belief); (b) one earthly life, then other states or worlds (Islam and Christianity in their own terms; Guenon, Schuon and the Muslim Traditionalists read this as passage through other states); (c) no return to the same state but an indefinite passage through many states (Guenon's own doctrine); (d) one subject lives all lives (Coomaraswamy, Advaita, the axiom). An earlier version folded (b) into (c); that was wrong.

Voices.
- Guenon: see 3.2. The strongest voice against "I come back". He does not deny continuation, only return to the same state, and the axiom needs neither. He traced Western reincarnationism to Allan Kardec and the Theosophists and tied it to the modern faith in progress [M]. A matching idea in Ibn Arabi (no repetition in the divine self-disclosure) [M]. Whether he went as far as saying the Hindu and Buddhist traditions themselves do not teach reincarnation is [U]. Say that he does not count a Spiritist or Theosophist as a tradition's voice.
- Coomaraswamy: see 3.3. P16 adds: the verses the tradition uses and that fit his argument (the Self as the thread on which all beings are strung, Brihadaranyaka 3.7.1 to 2 and Gita 7.7, the sutratman; the Knower of the field in all fields, Gita 13.2; the two birds; Plato's recollection; Eckhart's ground; Matthew 16:24) are real, but that he cites each in this particular paper is [U]. The thread in Alex's "lives and the thread" picture is the sutratman. The Buddhist test of his reading: the monk Sati who says one consciousness runs on through rebirths and is rebuked (Majjhima 38); the Buddha's silence when asked if there is a self (Samyutta 44.10); his refusal of the question "who feels?" (Samyutta 12.12) [M]. The mainstream reading (Rahula 1959, Collins 1982, Gombrich) is that early Buddhism denies any abiding self, so his reading is a minority one, though a few modern scholars and Thanissaro Bhikkhu argue the silence is strategic [U]. The essay's line "no fixed soul, yet something continues" is the safe one; say the comparer reads the silence as agreement with the Upanishads and specialists keep the two apart. Karma and second birth: karma as the Sanskrit word for action and a law of consequence, not fate or reward and punishment; action done without grasping at results frees (Gita 3.9, 4.18) [M]; rebirth as a second birth inside this life [U as a sustained thesis]; check Hinduism and Buddhism (1943).
- Schuon: forms differ, the truth does not. The Eastern long cycle and the Western single decisive life can both be read as perspectives, one stressing the gravity of this life, the other the length of the road [M principle, U for the rebirth application]; he agreed with Guenon that modern reincarnationism is wrong [U]. On law and grace he was a close student of Pure Land Buddhism. The best source for how a comparer explains why traditions differ on rebirth. Open the Transcendent Unity before citing.
- Nasr, Lings, Burckhardt, Pallis, Perry. Nasr (Knowledge and the Sacred, 1981), Lings and Burckhardt are Muslims in the Guenon and Schuon line, so they hold the Islamic position: one earthly life, barzakh, resurrection, rejection of tanasukh. Pallis, a Traditionalist and a Buddhist, treats rebirth as part of his tradition. Perry's Treasury has no known rebirth entry [U]. The Traditionalist stance on rebirth follows the author's own religion, which is itself a fair point to make [M; what each says on rebirth U].
- Huxley (one popular anthology among many). Rebirth is not in his three-part definition. His chapter "Immortality and Survival" (chapter 14 by recall [U]) separates survival from immortality and sets posthumous states beside one another (the Tibetan bardo, Christian purgatory, Hindu and Buddhist rebirth) as depending on the state of the soul; that he quotes the Gita clothes verse (2.22) there is U; as recalled he treats mere survival as no liberation. His chapter "Time and Eternity" is a different thing from Coomaraswamy's 1947 book of that name. He shows that one popular statement of the common core can omit rebirth, which supports saying the traditions are not unanimous; a door and not an authority. The load-bearing perennial doctrine on lives and karma sits in Guenon, Schuon, Coomaraswamy and Smith (P16, P08).
- Smith and Wilber: Smith's four levels give a vertical map of what continues; Wilber's Atman project (1980), Up from Eden (1981) and Sex, Ecology, Spirituality (1995) describe development as union, separation, then return, with involution and evolution owing much to Aurobindo and Hegel; each man's own stand on reincarnation is U (both seem cautious).
- Aurobindo, The Life Divine and The Problem of Rebirth (1952): a psychic being, the evolving soul, takes on a new mind, life and body each time; the personality does not return; no fall back into animals; karma is a working of nature that the soul can outgrow [M]. Rudolf Steiner, Theosophy (1904) and later karma lectures, teaches rebirth and a Christ-centred karma; he left the Theosophical Society and founded Anthroposophy. Aurobindo is the best Indian model for a staged ascent across lives, a parallel for the arrow.
- Theosophy [M substance, U wording]. In Isis Unveiled (1877) Blavatsky rejected Kardec's reincarnation and allowed return only in exceptional cases; from the later 1870s and 1880s she and her circle taught reincarnation as the rule. The reincarnating Ego (the individuality) returns, the personality does not; between lives come a purgatory-like kamaloka and a long heaven-state, devachan; karma is the law of cause and effect and of restored harmony, with no judge. Sinnett, Esoteric Buddhism (1883); Besant, Reincarnation (1892), Karma (1895), The Ancient Wisdom (1897); William Quan Judge, The Ocean of Theosophy (1893). They also teach that the unity of humanity is the basis of ethics and of karma; "separateness" is called the great heresy [U on the exact phrase and place]. Like Guenon, they hold the popular belief in rebirth as animals is a distortion; unlike him, they hold the true teaching is reincarnation. For lives and karma in plain English use Judge and Besant; for the cosmic frame the Proem; say which is quoted. Critics: Muller said in 1893 there is no "esoteric Buddhism" (the Buddha's closed fist of a teacher, Mahaparinibbana Sutta, Digha 16, numbering differs by translator); Coleman (1895, an appendix to Solovyov's A Modern Priestess of Isis) traced much of the Secret Doctrine to nineteenth century books; the Hodgson Report (1885) judged her phenomena fraudulent, a verdict Vernon Harrison's "J'Accuse: An Examination of the Hodgson Report of 1885" (Journal of the SPR, 1986) reopened; Guenon called it a pseudo-religion; histories: Campbell, Ancient Wisdom Revived (1980); Godwin, The Theosophical Enlightenment (1994). Alex can cite Theosophy for the comparative project and admit that specialists dispute its sources.
- Jung: see 8.3. Gives Alex a clean way to separate the senses of "rebirth" before claiming one.
- The Greek record [M]. Pythagoras and the soul of the beaten dog (Xenophanes, Diels-Kranz 21 B7); Empedocles (DK 31 B117); Herodotus 2.123 on Egyptian teaching (Egyptologists do not accept it); Pindar, Olympian 2; Orphic gold tablets (the waters of Memory against Lethe); Plato in Meno 81, Phaedo, Phaedrus 246 to 249, Republic 10, Timaeus 41 to 42, Laws 10 (903 to 905); Caesar, Gallic War 6.14 on the Druids. Whether Greek rebirth came from India is disputed; many scholars say it arose independently. The Myth of Er is the best Western statement that responsibility and not punishment is the point: the blame lies with the chooser and God is blameless (Republic 617e).
- Plotinus [M, exact passages U]. Enneads 4.9 asks whether all souls are one and answers that they are one and many; 3.2 compares souls to actors who change roles; 3.4 the allotted spirit; 6.4 and 6.5 the one Being present everywhere whole. He accepted transmigration, including punitive falls; Porphyry and Iamblichus rejected rebirth into animals (secondhand). The oneness of souls and many lives are already together in Plotinus, the axiom's pairing.
- Christianity [M]. Origen (De Principiis; Commentary on Matthew 13.1) taught pre-existence and final restoration and called transmigration foreign to the church; the claim that the Council of 553 banned reincarnation is not supported, and Theosophical and anthology writers (Head and Cranston, 1961 and 1977) repeat it; do not repeat the myth. John 9:2 is quoted by rebirth advocates and read by scholars as a reflection of Jewish views on prenatal sin. Geddes MacGregor, Reincarnation in Christianity (1978), argues a Christian case; John Hick (Death and Eternal Life, 1976; Evil and the God of Love, 1966) argues that if the world is a place of soul-making then the task is longer than one life and so continues in other lives or worlds: Alex's "otherwise the whole is a bad designer" argument in academic form.
- Judaism, gilgul [M]. In the Bahir (12th century), the Zohar, and above all Isaac Luria (1534 to 1572) and Hayyim Vital (Sha'ar ha-Gilgulim): the soul returns to repair a flaw (tikkun), souls have roots in the limbs of the first Adam, and there are special forms (ibbur, dibbuk). Saadia Gaon (Emunot ve-De'ot, 933) argued against transmigration; Menasseh ben Israel wrote on it (1651). Scholem, "Gilgul" in the Encyclopaedia Judaica and in On the Mystical Shape of the Godhead (1991); he treated gilgul as a late medieval Kabbalistic doctrine with disputed origins. The picture of souls as organs of Adam and repair as the purpose of return is the fingers and the thread in Jewish form.
- Islam [M]. Mainstream theology rejects tanasukh (Avicenna's argument that each soul arises with a fitted body; Quran 23:99 to 100); the Druze (taqammus: death is followed directly by rebirth as a human), Nusayris, Yarsan and some extremist Shia groups teach it; Stevenson drew many cases from Druze Lebanon. Barzakh (Quran 23:100) is the in-between, often set beside the Tibetan bardo, though one ends in resurrection and the other in rebirth. Ibn Arabi: creation is renewed at every moment and nothing repeats. Mulla Sadra: the soul's substantial movement and development after death without rebirth in this world. Rumi's lines on dying as mineral, plant, animal and man (Masnavi 3, around 3901 to 3906 in Nicholson's numbering) describe the soul's rise through forms and are read by Chittick and others as the arc of ascent and not reincarnation; use a named translator for any English "Rumi" line. A strong case of "growth continues, the return is dropped".
- Hindu texts that answer "not enough time" [M]. Gita 6.45 (the yogi perfected through many births reaches the goal), 7.19 (the wise one comes to Me at the end of many births), 4.5 (Krishna remembers many births), 8.6 (the last thought at death directs the passage). Brihadaranyaka 4.4.3 to 5 (the caterpillar and the goldsmith: the Self fashions a newer form; as one acts so one becomes), Chandogya 5.10.7 (conduct decides the womb), Yoga Sutra 2.13 (the store of karma bears fruit as birth, lifespan and experience). Gita 6.45 and 7.19 are direct witnesses for "the run is longer than one life".
- Hindu texts for the axiom [M except Ramana]. Gita 13.16 (13.17 in some editions) and 18.20: the Reality is undivided in beings and yet appears divided; the nearest line in any scripture to "one whole experiencing separation". Gita 15.7: the living soul is an eternal part of Me. Brahma Sutra 2.3.43: the individual is a part (Ramanuja: a real part; Shankara: "as if" a part). Brahma Sutra 2.1.34 to 36 faces the objection that a creator who makes unequal lives is cruel and answers with karma and a beginningless cycle. Gaudapada, Mandukya Karika 2.32 is the honest answer to the essay's "who carries over?": at the end, no one was bound. Ramana Maharshi's habit, as recalled, was to turn the question "who is reborn?" back to "who are you now?" [U].
- Buddhist teaching on karma [M]. Intention is karma (Anguttara 6.63). Beings are heirs of their actions (Anguttara 5.57). Short and long analyses of action (Majjhima 135, 136). The Buddha rejects "everything I feel comes from past karma" and lists other causes (Samyutta 36.21), and later tradition gives five orders of natural law, karma being one; Samyutta 36.21 supports the essay's warning that blame is the cheap karma. At the awakening he gains three knowledges, recall of past lives, the passing of beings by their deeds, and the end of the taints (Majjhima 4, 36). Dhammapada 1 and 2 (the wheel follows the ox), 127, 165. Yogacara adds the store consciousness carrying seeds; Tibetan tradition has recognised lines of rebirth (tulku) from the 13th century. Rebirth without a fixed soul: the Milindapanha lamp flame.
- Jain and Sikh [M]. Jain: karma is fine matter that binds the soul through passion; eight kinds; souls are many, eternal and uncreated; liberation is the shedding of karma (Tattvartha Sutra chapter 8, 8.1 on the causes of bondage; 5.21 on souls helping one another). Sikh: rebirth through a long cycle of births, the human birth a rare chance, liberation while living; Japji pauri 4, closing line (Guru Granth Sahib page 2): the body comes by karma and liberation by grace. Strong on rebirth and karma but not on one whole (Jain souls are many), so cite them for the lives and not the oneness.
- Karma in the West and the fingers [M]. Hosea 8:7 (sow the wind, reap the whirlwind), Psalm 7:15 to 16, Matthew 7:2 (measure for measure), Galatians 6:7, Aeschylus (the doer must suffer, Choephori 313), Anaximander (things pay penalty to one another, DK 12 B1), Heraclitus B94, Emerson's "Compensation" (1841). The closest match to the fingers is Schopenhauer, The World as Will and Representation vol. 1, section 63 ("eternal justice"): since one will lives in all, tormentor and tormented are the same, and the tormentor suffers what he inflicts [M; the Indian myth of the wrongdoer born as his victim pictures it, M-]. He drew on the Upanishads through the Oupnek'hat and put rebirth as palingenesis (the will returns, not the memory) against metempsychosis (vol. 2, chapter 41). Public domain in old translations and the best precedent for the fingers. Luria's souls as limbs of Adam are the Jewish form; Matthew 25:40 and 1 Corinthians 12:26 and the hadith of believers as one body ground unity in Christ, the Church or a community and not a metaphysical One (P06, P17).
- Survey figures, as recalled and unchecked [U]: about a quarter of US adults believed in reincarnation in Pew 2009 and about a third in Pew 2018.

Scholars (P16 §5, M). Sedgwick notes the school's Muslim and anti-modern commitments, the Evola strand with fascist politics, and the later controversies around Schuon. Guenon read Sanskrit sources through informants and never went to India. Indologists reject any claim that Hinduism and Buddhism do not teach rebirth. Katz, Proudfoot and Sharf reject strong perennialism; Forman and Hick answer. Coomaraswamy's reading of anatta is a minority view. Donald Lopez (The Tibetan Book of the Dead: A Biography, 2011) shows how the title and framing came from Evans-Wentz. Paul Edwards (1996) is the main critic of reincarnation; Stevenson's cases did not convince the scientific community. Chittick reads Rumi and Ibn Arabi as ascent and renewal, not reincarnation. Historians of the Origen controversy reject the claim of a council ban.

Leads from P16 (unchecked): state the split and place the axiom above it (the whole lives all lives, so the quarrel between Guenon and Blavatsky is not one the essay has to settle); use Gita 13.16 and 18.20 for one whole experiencing separation and Gita 6.45 and 7.19 for the long run; Secret Doctrine third proposition as Theosophy's version of the axiom, after checking the text; Schopenhauer section 63 and Luria's souls for the fingers; Samyutta 36.21 and Myth of Er 617e for responsibility and against blame; Gaudapada 2.32 and the Buddhist silence for "who carries over"; a sentence on grace next to repair; Guenon's states are not stages in time, so keep the arrow as the school's scale and say so; prefer "transmigration" and "rebirth" to "reincarnation", which carries Spiritist freight; where the essay needs "the perennial philosophy says", prefer the primary Traditionalist and scholar-perennialist authors and name Huxley at most as the book many readers will have met, saying that some perennial authors (Evola) have a political shadow so cite them for doctrine; for "you are more than one short life" use Schuon, Guenon, Coomaraswamy and Smith, each in a different metaphysical dialect, and say honestly that they split on whether the same person returns.

### 11.4 The ethic of unity and the golden rule

Source: P17 (never verification-checked; first pass without search), with P01, P05, P06, P07, P08, G1-4. [M] and [U] as marked.

Two ways of arguing for an ethic (P17 §1). The first is reciprocity: treat others as you want to be treated (Hillel; Confucius, Analects 15.24; Matthew 7:12; the Mahabharata; the Udanavarga; the hadith; the Jain verse). It needs no metaphysics, which is why it appears almost everywhere, and why Hans Kueng's 1993 Declaration and Karen Armstrong's 2009 Charter can use it as a common floor. The second is identity: the other is you, so harming them harms you (Isha Upanishad 6 and 7, Gita 6.29 and 13.28, Paul's members of one another, Matthew 25:40, Shantideva's limbs, the Neo-Confucian one body). This is what the perennialists argue for. Schopenhauer made it the foundation of morals; Vivekananda and Deussen follow from the Indian side; Schuon and probably Coomaraswamy from the Traditionalist side; Huxley's Charity chapter makes the same move as the third leg of his definition. In this reading the golden rule is the visible shadow of the metaphysics. Who draws the line between the two: Kant (rejects the rule as trivial; Groundwork 4:430 footnote), Schopenhauer (reciprocity is egoism in better clothes), Ricoeur (the rule is a hinge between exchange and gift), the rabbis Akiva and Ben Azzai (love your neighbour versus common descent from Adam), Shankara on Gita 6.32, Wattles (unverified), Vivekananda (only oneness explains why to be unselfish; source U); Kueng and Armstrong do not, because they want a floor everyone can stand on. Critics of the identity route: Levinas, Buber, Zaehner, Nietzsche. For Alex: the fingers sit on the identity rung; P17 suggests turning the draft's sentence that most rest on reciprocity or empathy and a few go further into a ladder of three rungs (reciprocity, analogy, identity), each with citations: reciprocity (Hillel, Confucius, Matthew 7:12, the hadith), analogy (Sutta Nipata 705, Gita 6.32, Sirach 31:15, Mencius's child at the well), identity (Isha 6 and 7, Gita 6.29 and 13.28, Paul, Matthew 25:40, Ben Azzai with Tanhuma, Shantideva's limbs).

- Schopenhauer: compassion (Mitleid) as the one moral motive, grounded in tat tvam asi [M; section number U]. Morality is compassion, not duty or reciprocity; individuality is appearance (the principium individuationis, which he equates with maya), and the compassionate person has in practice seen through it. His content formula: harm no one and help everyone as far as you can (neminem laede, imo omnes, quantum potes, juva); two cardinal virtues, justice and loving-kindness. He holds that the golden rule and Kant's imperative smuggle in self-interest, since I agree to them by picturing myself on the receiving end. Ueber die Grundlage der Moral was written 1839 to 1840 for a prize competition of the Danish Royal Society, which gave no prize, and published in 1841 in Die beiden Grundprobleme der Ethik (the first file said 1840; the correction is P14's) [M]. Translations: Payne, On the Basis of Morality (Berghahn, 1995, Cartwright introduction); Bullock (1903, publisher U). The tat tvam asi passage is in section 22 [U]; the fuller scene in WWR vol. 1, section 66 [M]. Public domain in older translations, the oldest Western statement of Alex's step. Add his own ending: his ethics leads to denial of the will, which is not return.
- Neo-Vedanta: Paul Deussen (1845 to 1919), Schopenhauer's disciple, took tat tvam asi as the Upanishadic basis of morals (Das System des Vedanta, 1883; Die Philosophie der Upanishad's, 1899); founder of the Schopenhauer Society in 1911, translator of sixty Upanishads (1897) [M]. Vivekananda argued that only Vedanta answers why to be unselfish, because the Self is one (Complete Works, Karma-Yoga and Practical Vedanta lectures; titles U). Radhakrishnan, An Idealist View of Life (1932), argues similarly. Anantanand Rambachan, The Advaita Worldview (SUNY, 2006), argues from inside Advaita that non-duality grounds ethics and social concern, against the charge that a world called illusion cannot motivate action; Advaita specialists have long asked the question, and Shankara treats ethics as purification before knowledge, with the knower freed from injunctions. Vivekananda is the best Indian voice for the step; Rambachan answers the honest objection.
- Huxley, chapter "Charity" (chapter 5 by recall [U]): love of neighbour as the practical face of knowing the Ground in all; sets Christian sources (Paul, Law, the mystics) beside the Gita (12.13 is the likely passage) and the Mahayana bodhisattva. The Foreword's ethic clause is about knowing the Ground, and the draft essay's description of reciprocity as "the third part of Huxley's definition" needs repair: say his ethic puts the end in knowing the Ground and his chapter on charity says the knowing does not come without love, or credit Schopenhauer or Vivekananda for the identity argument (G1-4). Cite him as one anthologist of the ethic and use primary texts. Huxley's line of reasoning (the ethic follows from the metaphysic) is the same shape as Alex's.
- Schuon: charity and humility are ontological and not only moral; pride is the ego's false absoluteness; to love the neighbour is to recognise the Self in him; knowledge without virtue sours into spiritual pride; the golden rule is the moral corollary of "thou art that" [M; locators unknown; his triad of fundamental virtues U]. A Traditionalist witness for the warning that unity without humility turns into pride.
- Coomaraswamy and the neighbour [U for Coomaraswamy]. The reading "love thy neighbour as thyself" as "the neighbour is thyself", tied to Atman in all, is recalled but unplaced. The Hebrew kamocha (Leviticus 19:18, ve'ahavta lere'acha kamocha) can be translated "love your neighbour, who is like you", which is how Buber and Rosenzweig (Die Schrift, 1920s) and Hermann Cohen before them took it [M]. Eckhart is cited for the same reading [U where]. Alex can use the kamocha reading without Coomaraswamy; check with a Hebrew scholar.
- Smith: no page where he links the golden rule to unity was found [U]; do not cite him on this until the passage is found.
- Hans Kueng and the 1993 Declaration toward a Global Ethic [M]. Drafted by Kueng (1928 to 2021, Catholic theologian, Tuebingen) for the Parliament of the World's Religions, Chicago, 28 August to 4 September 1993; signed 4 September by well over a hundred leaders (143 to 200 [U]). Two principles: every human being must be treated humanely, and the golden rule in negative and positive form, the irrevocable, unconditional norm for all areas of life. Four directives: non-violence and respect for life, solidarity and a fair economy, tolerance and truthfulness, equal partnership of women and men. An endnote lists the rule across traditions: Analects 15.23, Hillel (Shabbat 31a), Matthew 7:12 and Luke 6:31, Nawawi's Forty Hadith 13, Sutrakritanga I.11.33, Samyutta Nikaya S V 353, Mahabharata XIII 114.8 [numbers medium]. Kueng and Kuschel (eds.), Erklaerung zum Weltethos (Piper, 1993); A Global Ethic (SCM, 1993); Projekt Weltethos (1990). Kueng seeks a minimal consensus on practice and avoids a shared metaphysics on purpose, which makes it the best foil: the rule is shared without the axiom, and the axiom is a candidate for why. Its ground is interdependence, weaker than oneness. The 1893 Parliament's stated aim included making the golden rule the basis of union (P01 [C]).
- Karen Armstrong [M; the Charter's wording from memory]. She won the 2008 TED Prize and used the wish to build the Charter for Compassion, launched 12 November 2009: compassion lies at the heart of all religious, ethical and spiritual traditions; treat all others as we wish to be treated; dethrone ourselves from the centre of our world and put another there. The Great Transformation (Knopf, 2006): the Axial Age sages (Confucius, Buddha, Upanishads, Hebrew prophets, Greeks) made compassion and the golden rule the test of religion and practised kenosis, self-emptying (Jaspers coined Axial Age, 1949); Twelve Steps to a Compassionate Life (2010). Her dethrone-the-self is Alex's letting-go axis written as ethics; she keeps to practice and history and does not argue for the One. A popular historian and not a specialist in each tradition.
- The golden rule's scholars [M; Wattles U]. Jeffrey Wattles, The Golden Rule (Oxford, 1996) and "Levels of Meaning in the Golden Rule" (Journal of Religious Ethics, 1987): the rule has depth levels, from fair exchange upward toward sharing the other's view and love; I recall a Kent State philosopher whose sources include the Urantia Book, which a sceptic will ask about. Paul Ricoeur, "The Golden Rule: Exegetical and Theological Perplexities" (New Testament Studies 36, 1990): a hinge between a logic of equivalence and a logic of superabundance (love your enemies, Matthew 5:44), the cleanest map of the rule's limits. Harry Gensler, Ethics and the Golden Rule (2013): a formal consistency test. Neusner and Chilton (eds.), The Golden Rule: The Ethics of Reciprocity in World Religions (2008). Hans Dieter Betz, The Sermon on the Mount (1995), and Albrecht Dihle, Die Goldene Regel (1962): the Greek and Jewish history, the negative form older; Tobit 4:15 (negative) and Sirach 31:15 (judge your neighbour by yourself) are early Jewish forms. Historians (Dihle, Betz) show formulas differ in sign, scope (kin, co-believers, all) and motive (prudence, fairness, compassion), and that inside a hierarchy the rule can keep the hierarchy. Kant called it trivial; Shaw's joke is the popular form of the point.
- Judaism [M; Sifra and Genesis Rabbah locators medium]. Shabbat 31a: a would-be convert asks for the whole Torah on one foot; Hillel says what is hateful to you do not do to your fellow, that is the whole Torah, the rest is commentary: the negative form and a reciprocity rule. The better find is the rabbinic argument about the same verse. Sifra, Kedoshim 4:12: Akiva calls love your neighbour as yourself the great principle of the Torah; Ben Azzai answers that "this is the book of the generations of Adam" (Genesis 5:1) is greater, since all descend from one man. Genesis Rabbah 24:7: Rabbi Tanhuma, if you shame your neighbour, know whom you shame, since he was made in God's likeness. Tanya ch. 32 grounds love of the fellow Jew in the common soul root. Mishnah Sanhedrin 4:5: whoever destroys one life destroys a world, paralleled in Quran 5:32. Akiva against Ben Azzai is an ancient version of the reciprocity versus unity split inside one tradition; Tanhuma gives a one-line identity argument.
- Confucius and the Confucians [M]. Analects 15.24 (15.23 in Legge): Zigong asks for one word to act on for life; the Master says shu (reciprocity) and gives the negative rule; 12.2 repeats it; 6.30 gives the positive form and calls taking what is near as an analogy the method of ren; 4.15: the Master's way is one thread, which Zengzi calls loyalty (zhong) and shu. All reciprocity or analogy. The identity rung comes later: Cheng Hao (11th century), the person of ren forms one body with heaven, earth and the ten thousand things; Zhang Zai's Western Inscription; Wang Yangming, Inquiry on the Great Learning (1527), the proof being Mencius 2A6, the child about to fall into a well, felt before any calculation. Shu for the first rung; Wang Yangming and the child at the well for the last, from a tradition outside the Indian and Abrahamic ones. Specialists note that the Confucian one body is graded love and not numerical identity.
- Hindu texts: reciprocity, analogy and identity in one book [M; alternative Mahabharata numbers and Shankara's wording U]. Mahabharata, Anushasana Parva 13.113.8 in the critical numbering (13.114.8 in the Vulgate numbering the Declaration uses): do not do to others what would harm you; this is the whole of dharma, the rest is desire. Isha 6 and 7 (Vajasaneyi Samhita 40.6 and 7): the one who sees all beings in the Self and the Self in all beings does not shrink or feel revulsion (na vijugupsate; translators differ); for the one who sees oneness (ekatvam), what delusion, what sorrow? Gita 6.29 (sees the Self in all beings and all in the Self), 6.30, 6.31, 6.32 (sees sameness everywhere by analogy to the self, atmaupamyena, in pleasure and in pain), 13.28 (seeing the Lord equally present everywhere, he does not harm the self by the self and reaches the highest goal), 5.25 (intent on the welfare of all beings), 12.13 (hates no being). Shankara's commentary on 6.32 reads atmaupamya as empathy by analogy [gist M]. Gita 6.32 sits between rungs; 13.28 is the closest classical text to the fingers. Chandogya 6.8.7 (first tat tvam asi) is metaphysics with no ethic attached; the ethical reading is later.
- Buddhism: parity, no owner, exchange [M; chapter 8 verse numbers U]. Dhammapada 129 and 130 (all tremble at violence; put yourself in the other's place and do not kill); Sutta Nipata 705 (as I am so are they); Samyutta Nikaya 55.7 (S V 353); Samyutta 3.8 and Udana 5.1 (nothing is dearer than oneself, others hold themselves as dear); Udanavarga 5.18 (Rockhill). All are parity: from my feeling to theirs. Shantideva, Bodhicaryavatara 8 goes further: the limbs are many yet one body, so beings are one in wanting happiness (8.91 in Padmakara; numbering differs by edition); suffering has no owner, so remove it because it is suffering; then the practice of exchanging self and other. Not identity; Paul Williams (Altruism and Reality, 1998) and Charles Goodman (Consequences of Compassion, 2009) keep that clear. A comparer who calls it One is the comparer. Keep the draft's note that Shantideva reaches compassion by another route (no owner of suffering).
- Jainism [M]. Sutrakritanga 1.11.33 (Jacobi, SBE 45): treat all creatures as you yourself would be treated; Acharanga 1.4.1 (no living being may be killed, hurt or driven away); Acharanga 1.5.5 (the one you want to strike is like you; Jacobi is more careful than the oneness reading); Tattvartha Sutra 5.21 (souls serve one another). Jain doctrine holds souls to be infinitely many: a strong ethic on plural metaphysics, showing unity is one possible ground and not the only one.
- Christian texts, and Merton [M]. Matthew 7:12 and Luke 6:31 (Didache 1.2 has both forms); Leviticus 19:18 taken up in Matthew 22:39, Galatians 5:14, Romans 13:9; Matthew 25:40 and Acts 9:4 (Christ identified with the least); Romans 12:5 and Ephesians 4:25 (members one of another); 1 Corinthians 12:26 (if one suffers all suffer); 1 John 4:20. Thomas Merton's Fourth and Walnut epiphany (18 March 1958), Conjectures of a Guilty Bystander (1966): he realises he loves the passing strangers, that they cannot be alien to him, and that people walk around shining like the sun [gist; locator unknown]. Paul is the one Western scripture with the body argument; Merton reached the identity rung by experience.
- Islam, Sikhism, Baha'i [M hadith and Quran; Sikh pages U]. Bukhari 13 and Muslim 45 (Anas): none of you believes until he loves for his brother what he loves for himself (Nawawi 13). Bukhari 6011 and Muslim 2586: believers are like one body, and when one limb suffers the rest answer with sleeplessness and fever. Muslim 2569 (hadith qudsi): God says he was sick and was not visited, the same identification as Matthew 25. Quran 4:1 (created from one soul), 5:32 (killing one soul equals killing all mankind), 49:13. Sikh: Kabir in the Guru Granth Sahib (p. 1349) and Guru Arjan (p. 1299). Baha'u'llah: you are fruits of one tree and leaves of one branch.
- Stoics and Plotinus [M]. Marcus Aurelius, Meditations 2.1, 7.13 (limb versus part), 8.34; Hierocles (2nd century CE) drew concentric circles from the self to all humanity and said to draw the outer ones inward; Plotinus, Ennead IV.9 (Are all souls one?) explains sympathy by the unity of soul [title M, application U]. Part one already uses Marcus; 8.34 is the nearest Western Stoic to the fingers.
- Theosophy: brotherhood as a fact in nature [M; Key and Voice U]. The Society's first object (1891 form): to form a nucleus of the Universal Brotherhood of Humanity without distinction of race, creed, sex, caste or colour; the second is comparative religion, philosophy and science. The Secret Doctrine's Proem, third proposition, joins ethics to identity directly. The Key to Theosophy (1889) calls brotherhood a fact in nature and not a sentiment [locator U]; the Voice of the Silence, Fragment 3, calls compassion the law of laws [wording U].
- Experiment. Cialdini, Brown, Lewis, Luce and Neuberg (Journal of Personality and Social Psychology 73(3), 481 to 494, 1997) found that felt oneness predicted helping better than empathic concern; Batson and colleagues replied in the same issue (about pp. 495 to 509 [U]), arguing empathy motivates helping without merging. Add Batson's reply to the draft sentence that says others dispute it [M].
- The honest objection. Levinas held that ethics begins from the otherness of the other and that unity absorbs the other into the same (Totality and Infinity, 1961); Buber's I and Thou needs two; Zaehner held the monistic mystic and the theistic lover are different cases; Nietzsche attacked pity, which hits Schopenhauer. One honest sentence helps: a merged self has no one left to be responsible to. Kueng and Armstrong get respect and the usual criticisms: a thin floor, drafted largely by Western or liberal hands, and broad generalisation [soft].
- Methods in this area (P17 §4): thematic anthology (the golden rule is the easiest theme because the surface wording is so close); derivation from metaphysics (Schopenhauer, Deussen, Vivekananda, the Traditionalists: reciprocity as visible effect and identity as cause; differences explained by how much of the identity each tradition says aloud: exoteric forms keep the rule, esoteric ones keep the reason); consensus on practice (Kueng); historical and axial (Armstrong, after Jaspers); philosophical typology (Wattles, Ricoeur, Gensler); internal exegesis (the rabbis, the oldest comparison of the two grounds and the cleanest).

### 11.5 Stages, and what lies after the last rung

Source: P18 (verification pass, no search), with P11, P14, P16, P12, G1-4, S3. [M], [M-] (less confident) and [U] as marked. The brief asked for names and counts "verified"; none is; check each count once before printing.

The picture (P18 §1). Almost every tradition that describes a path describes it in steps, and the steps carry names that sound like distance: mansions, valleys, heavens, spheres, grounds, gates, rungs, worlds, planes. The comparers use these maps in four ways: Underhill lays Christian maps side by side and claims one human pattern; the Traditionalists treat the ladder of the soul and the ladder of the cosmos as one ladder seen from two sides; Wilber and Brown test it on meditation stages; the Theosophists build one numbered map from many sources. Three things recur. The number of rungs varies (four, five, seven, nine, ten, fourteen, thirty, one hundred); seven is frequent, which in Mediterranean and Near Eastern cases may come from the seven classical planets and not from a shared inner fact (P18's reasoning; it fits Hermes, Mithras, Hagigah and Dante and does not fit Teresa, the Jain fourteen or the Buddhist ten). Most maps allow falls. And the end of the ladder is handled in four ways, each an interpretive grouping and not a doctrine of the traditions: rest (Jain siddha, Theravada parinibbana as usually glossed), no end (Gregory of Nyssa, Ibn Arabi on one reading), return into action (bodhisattva, Teresa's seventh mansion, the tenth ox-herding picture, baqa on one reading, Theosophy's Nirmanakaya) and denial that there was ever a ladder (Gaudapada, sudden Zen, Wilber's "one taste"). Alex's arrow, with a stage called birth after plus four, fits the return family and the second-birth family, and he can say honestly that the traditions disagree about what comes after the last rung.

Caution on a sentence. Do not say that no other scale is nine steps centred on a door. Nine-step schemes exist (the nine abidings of MN 111, the nine stages of calm abiding in the Tibetan manuals, the nine vehicles of the Nyingma school, Dante's nine moving heavens, ten with the Empyrean, and Dionysius's nine angelic orders), and scales centred on a threshold exist (Gurdjieff's seven categories of man are centred on Man No. 4, the first man who is made and not born; the five Buddhist paths are centred on the path of seeing, which is stream entry). What the files know of none is a scale numbered symmetrically about zero [M, not searched]. The draft's line that other traditions draw staged maps too is safe.

The maps.
- Underhill's five stages: see 9.2.
- Teresa of Avila, The Interior Castle (written 1577, first printed 1588; "mansions" echoes John 14:2 in the King James version, the Spanish is moradas, dwelling places) [M].
