A castle of one diamond or clear crystal with seven groups of mansions: one, self-knowledge; two, perseverance; three, an orderly life that can go dry and proud; four, the prayer of quiet, where she says the supernatural begins; five, the prayer of union (the silkworm becomes a butterfly); six, the betrothal, with raptures and trials; seven, the spiritual marriage. She says the purpose of the marriage is the birth of good works (Seventh Mansions, chapter 4; the Spanish wording not cleared), and that Martha and Mary go together. A Christian text where arrival produces action and, in her wording, birth. The essay already cites Teresa without a date; if one is added, say "written 1577, printed 1588".
- John of the Cross (the poem about 1578; the prose commentaries between about 1579 and 1585 [M-]): two nights, of the senses and of the spirit, each with an active and a passive side; beginners, proficients, the perfect; and in Dark Night Book II, chapters 19 and 20 (18 introduces the ladder) [M-], ten steps of the ladder of love, which John says he takes from Bernard and Aquinas.
- The ladder family [M]. Genesis 28:12 and 17; Plato, Symposium 210a to 212a (Diotima's ladder from one beautiful body to Beauty itself); John Climacus, Ladder of Divine Ascent (about 600), thirty steps, the last four on stillness, prayer, dispassion, and faith, hope and love (the icon shows monks dragged off the ladder by demons); Rule of Benedict ch. 7, twelve degrees of humility, introduced with Jacob's ladder; Bonaventure, Itinerarium mentis in Deum (1259), six stages after the six wings of the seraph and a seventh of rest; Origen, Contra Celsum VI.22 (about 248), reporting Celsus on the Mithraic mysteries: a ladder with seven gates of seven metals tied to the planets, and an eighth gate above. The seven Mithraic grades (Raven, Bridegroom, Soldier, Lion, Persian, Sun-runner, Father) are not in Origen; they come from Jerome (Letter 107) and from the Santa Prisca and Felicissimus Mithraea, and whether gates and grades were one scheme is debated [M-]. Corpus Hermeticum I (Poimandres): the soul climbs through the seven planetary zones, giving back one vice at each, reaches the eighth and becomes divine; Paul's third heaven (2 Corinthians 12:2); Hagigah 12b names seven heavens (Vilon, Raqia, Shehaqim, Zevul, Maon, Makhon, Aravot). The order of the vices is U.
- Dante and the Mi'raj [M, lines U]. Divina Commedia (about 1308 to 1320): Paradiso has ten heavens (Moon, Mercury, Venus, Sun, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn, the fixed stars, the Primum Mobile, the Empyrean); Purgatorio seven terraces (pride, envy, wrath, sloth, avarice, gluttony, lust); Inferno nine circles. The Empyrean is outside space and time and has no "where" except the divine mind (around Par. XXVII, XXX). In Paradiso I Beatrice says his rising should surprise him no more than a stream running down a mountain, and that the marvel would be if, free of hindrance, he stayed below, as a living flame would be if it stayed still on the ground (I.136 to 141); in XXI, at Saturn, he sees a golden ladder going up past sight (XXI.25 to 30); in XXVIII a point of light ringed by nine circles of angels, the circles nearest the point turning fastest, so speed rises with nearness to God (XXVIII.16 to 39); in the last canto the scattered leaves of the universe bound by love in one volume (XXXIII.85 to 87). The nine angelic orders are Pseudo-Dionysius's, in three triads (Seraphim, Cherubim, Thrones; Dominions, Virtues, Powers; Principalities, Archangels, Angels). The Islamic Mi'raj: Muhammad ascends through seven heavens; the Qur'an gives the night journey (17:1) and the Lote tree vision (53:1 to 18); the seven heavens, the prophets in them and Gabriel stopping at the Lote tree are from hadith and commentary; the sequence as recalled is Adam; John and Jesus (two in the second heaven); Joseph; Idris; Aaron; Moses; Abraham [hadith numbers U]. Asin Palacios (1919) argued that Dante drew on the Mi'raj and Ibn Arabi; Cerulli (1949) added the Latin and French "Book of the Ladder"; most scholars call a direct source possible and unproven. P18: the best cultural precedent for Alex's picture. The old maps were already scale maps with the planets as steps; his solar system, galaxy and universe are the modern version. Dante's natural rising and speed rising with nearness fit his speed and gravity picture. Check cantos before use.
- Sufi stations and states [M; lists U]. Stations (maqamat), won by effort and stable, against states (ahwal), gifts that pass. Sarraj (d. 988), Kitab al-Luma: seven stations (repentance, scrupulousness, renunciation, poverty, patience, trust in God, contentment) and ten states (list U). Ansari of Herat (d. 1088 or 1089), Manazil al-Sa'irin: one hundred stations in ten sections of ten (section names U), ending in three grades of tawhid (of the common, the elect, the elect of the elect). Attar, The Conference of the Birds (about 1177): seven valleys (Quest, Love, Insight, Detachment, Unity, Bewilderment, Poverty and Annihilation); thirty birds arrive and find that they are the one they sought; the pun (si murgh, thirty birds, in the king's name Simurgh) is Attar's wordplay and not the name's etymology; subsistence (baqa) follows annihilation. Survey: Schimmel, Mystical Dimensions of Islam (1975). Attar carries Alex's axiom in a story: many, a journey, a mirror, one; valleys are a distance word. Baha'u'llah's Seven Valleys (about 1860, Baghdad; Search, Love, Knowledge, Unity, Contentment, Wonderment, True Poverty and Absolute Nothingness) answers Attar.
- Ibn Arabi and Mulla Sadra [M]. Ibn Arabi (1165 to 1240), al-Futuhat al-Makkiyya, 560 chapters in six parts (parts U). The highest rank is the station of no station (maqam la maqam), a person not fixed in any one station (Chittick 1989). The Real never shows itself twice in the same form, so no disclosure is last (the second half is an inference). Mulla Sadra (died 1635 or 1640), The Four Journeys: from creation to the Real; in the Real with the Real; from the Real to creation with the Real; in creation with the Real; the scheme is older than Sadra [U]. The third and fourth journeys are the cleanest short formula for return as action; baqa is usually read as return to life from the other side, one reading among others.
- Kabbalah [M]. Four worlds: Atzilut (emanation), Beriah (creation), Yetzirah (formation), Asiyah (action); ten sefirot (Keter, Chokhmah, Binah, Chesed, Gevurah, Tiferet, Netzach, Hod, Yesod, Malkhut, with Da'at sometimes counted for Keter); beyond them Ein Sof; in Safed Kabbalah and Chabad the morning prayer is a climb through the four worlds; Luria's tzimtzum, breaking of the vessels, tikkun; the Pardes story (Hagigah 14b): four entered the orchard, one died, one was harmed, one lost his faith, and Akiva entered and left in peace, a warning that ascent can hurt. Survey: Scholem, Major Trends in Jewish Mysticism (1941).
- Buddhist stages [M]. Four stages, eight kinds of noble person: stream-enterer (sotapanna), once-returner (sakadagami), non-returner (anagami), arahant, defined by the ten fetters. Stream entry cuts the first three (self-view, doubt, clinging to rites) and carries at most seven more lives; the once-returner weakens desire and ill will; the non-returner cuts them and is reborn in the Pure Abodes (five, the highest Akanittha) to finish there, though a non-returner may also finish in this life; arahantship cuts desire for form, desire for the formless, conceit, restlessness and ignorance. The jhanas: four form jhanas, four formless attainments (infinite space, infinite consciousness, nothingness, neither perception nor non-perception) and the cessation of perception and feeling, nine abidings in all (Anupada Sutta, MN 111, locator U); the first formless rung is literally called infinite space. The cosmology has 31 planes (11 sense-sphere, 16 form, 4 formless). Sutras speak of thousandfold, thousand-thousandfold and thousand-thousand-thousandfold world systems, that is 1,000, 1,000,000 and 1,000,000,000 worlds (Anguttara Nikaya 3.80 [U]); Alex's 1,000 by 1,000 is exactly the middle tier as a count of worlds, a numerical echo and not a source (the sutras count worlds; he counts squares).
- The ten bhumis and the bodhisattva who returns [M]. Pramudita (Joyful), Vimala (Stainless), Prabhakari (Luminous), Arcismati (Radiant), Sudurjaya (Hard to Conquer), Abhimukhi (Facing), Duramgama (Far-going), Acala (Immovable), Sadhumati (Good Intelligence), Dharmamegha (Cloud of Dharma); source the Dasabhumika, part of the Avatamsaka Sutra and also circulating alone; Chandrakirti's Madhyamakavatara (7th century) is built on it; they sit inside five paths (accumulation, preparation, seeing, meditation, no more learning). Chinese schemes run to fifty-two stages; Tibetan lamrim (Atisha, Tsongkhapa) to three scopes of person. Beyond the last: the vow to stay until all are free; non-abiding nirvana, where wisdom keeps one out of samsara and compassion out of nirvana; the Heart Sutra mantra (Sanskrit gate gate paragate parasamgate bodhi svaha; the earliest dated version is Xuanzang's Chinese of 649; Jan Nattier argued in 1992 that the Sanskrit is a back-translation from the Chinese, so make a claim about the "Sanskrit original" with that in view); the ten ox-herding pictures (Kuoan, 12th century): seeking, tracks, first glimpse, catching, taming, riding home, ox forgotten, both forgotten (an empty circle), return to the source, entering the marketplace with open hands; earlier sets stopped at the empty circle and the last pictures are the return. Shantideva: "born into the family of the Buddhas" is Bodhicaryavatara 3.25 [U numbering], the line the essay already uses; the vow to remain as long as space endures and beings remain to dispel the world's suffering is 10.55 in the numbering recalled [U]. The Dasabhumika and the tenth ox picture are the sharpest Buddhist answers to "what is after the last rung"; the seventh bhumi is "far-going", a distance word. Dharma-megha is the cleanest verifiable term equation: the tenth bhumi and the last samadhi in Yoga Sutra IV.29 (S3), to confirm.
- Jain fourteen stages [M; the English glosses are the file's]. Gunasthanas: 1 mithyatva (deluded view); 2 sasvadana (a taste of right view while falling); 3 misra (mixed); 4 avirata samyagdrishti (right view, no vows); 5 desavirata (partial vows, the householder); 6 pramatta-samyata (ascetic, lapsing); 7 apramatta-samyata (ascetic, vigilant); 8 apurvakarana; 9 anivrtti-karana; 10 suksma-samparaya (only subtle passions); 11 upasanta-kasaya (passions suppressed); 12 ksina-kasaya (passions destroyed); 13 sayogi-kevalin (omniscient, embodied, active); 14 ayogi-kevalin (activity stilled), then siddha. A soul at stage 11 falls back because the passions were quieted and not destroyed; from 12 there is no return. Sources: Shatkhandagama, Gommatasara, the Karmagranthas; Jaini, The Jaina Path of Purification (1979). Where the freed soul goes (Tattvartha Sutra ch. 10, sutras 5 to 8 [U]; numbering differs between Digambara and Svetambara recensions): it rises straight to the end of the universe by its own nature, like a flame or a gourd freed of its clay (four similes: the potter's wheel, the gourd, the castor seed, the flame), and stops because beyond is aloka, empty space with no medium of motion. The cosmos is a person fourteen rajju tall. The clearest ancient match to gravity and escape velocity: karma as weight, release as natural ascent, a hard edge of the cosmos. The Jain ladder ends in stillness at the boundary, not return; Jain souls stay many, so this is a witness to staged growth and not to the one whole.
- Yoga Vasistha, Patanjali, the jivanmukta [M]. Yoga Vasistha (Kashmir, built over centuries, core perhaps 10th century [M-]): seven stages of knowledge (subheccha good wish, vicarana inquiry, tanumanasa thinning of mind, sattvapatti attaining clarity, asamsakti non-attachment, padarthabhavana non-perception of objects, turyaga going to the fourth), the first three still waking and the last four liberated (mapping to waking, dream, deep sleep and turiya varies [U]); and seven stages of ignorance, fourteen in two ladders [sargas around 117 and 118, U]. Patanjali: eight limbs (II.29); samadhi with seed and without; a sevenfold last wisdom (II.27); the highest samadhi called dharma-megha (IV.29); kaivalya as the return of the gunas to their source (IV.34). Patanjali's Yoga is dualist, so equating kaivalya with Advaita liberation is the comparers' move and not his. The jivanmukta who stays: Advaita defends it (Vidyaranya, Jivanmuktiviveka, 14th century); Shankara allows begun karma to run on until the body falls (candidate locator Brahma Sutra Bhashya 4.1.15 [U]); the Gita says the realized person acts to hold the world together (III.20 to 25, Janaka as example). Gaudapada, Karika II.32, fits "treat the labels as labels".
- Plotinus and Plato [M]. Plotinus (Enneads, six groups of nine, edited by Porphyry about 301): three hypostases (the One, Intellect, Soul), matter as the lowest limit, procession out, conversion back; the way up runs through the grades of virtue (civic, purificatory, then contemplative and exemplary in Porphyry, Sentences 32) and ends in the flight of the alone to the alone (VI.9.11). Porphyry says Plotinus reached union four times in his presence and Porphyry once, at sixty-eight (Life 23). Plato: the divided line (Republic VI, 509d to 511e); the cave with the compelled return of the philosopher (VII, 514a to 520e); the place above the heavens (Phaedrus 247c). Republic VII is the oldest "must go back down" text, a good partner to the bodhisattva. Scholars debate Indian influence on Plotinus.
- Gurdjieff [M; numbers U]. Seven categories of man: one, two and three are men of the moving-instinctive, emotional and intellectual centres, living by habit; four is balanced through work; five has a unified permanent I; six has objective consciousness, not yet permanent; seven has all a person can have (Ouspensky, In Search of the Miraculous, 1949). The ray of creation runs from the Absolute through all worlds, all suns, the Sun, all planets and the Earth to the Moon, with a number of laws at each step; the law of octaves says a seven-step process stalls at two intervals unless a shock is added. Distance from the Absolute counts the steps, and a soul is made and not given, which differs from Alex's soul that continues; flag it.
- Theosophy: portals, two paths, initiations, planes [M; later initiations and Logoi U]. Blavatsky, The Voice of the Silence (1889): three fragments, the Voice of the Silence, the Two Paths, the Seven Portals (dana, shila, kshanti, viraga, virya, dhyana, prajna: the six Mahayana perfections with viraga inserted). The Two Paths set personal liberation against renunciation, whose master is a Nirmanakaya who stays to help. The Secret Doctrine's third proposition (2.4). Seven principles of the human being (sthula sharira, prana, linga sharira, kama, manas, buddhi, atma), seven planes, seven rounds. Besant and Leadbeater, then Alice Bailey, add initiations tied to the Christian life (Birth, Baptism, Transfiguration, Crucifixion or Renunciation, Resurrection; Besant's Esoteric Christianity 1901 and Leadbeater's The Masters and the Path 1925 [M-]) and, in Leadbeater, to the four Buddhist stages plus the Master; Bailey runs past five. In the Pali texts an arahant already is an asekha, so their split of the two is an addition. Their "Birth" is the first initiation and not Alex's stage after plus four. Buddhist scholars say the Theosophical Nirmanakaya and Pratyekabuddha are not the Buddhist ones. The only existing map that puts human steps inside a cosmic hierarchy reaching the solar system and beyond, which is Alex's plus two to plus four. Krishnamurti, raised as World Teacher, dissolved the Order at Ommen on 3 August 1929 and said truth is a pathless land (date M; whether "stage" is in the address U). Part one bans school vocabulary.
- Birth, and what lies after it [M]. Eckhart preaches the birth of the Son in the ground of the soul (main text the sermon on Wisdom 18:14, German Works no. 101 [U]), then a breakthrough (Durchbruch) into the Godhead beyond God, so the birth is not last (Sermon 52, where he prays God to rid him of God); scholars read the birth and the breakthrough as two moments (McGinn, as recalled [U]). John 3:3 and 3:7 use the Greek anothen, again or from above. A dvija, twice-born, is one who has received the upanayana initiation. Teresa's seventh mansion ends in the birth of works. Gregory of Nyssa, Life of Moses (390s), builds on Philippians 3:13 the idea of epektasis, a never-ending stretch toward God, so union does not stop the ascent; theosis (2 Peter 1:4) is the Orthodox word for the end. Alex's paragraph says the traditions use the image differently; add that Eckhart himself puts something after the birth. Epektasis is the best Christian authority for "even plus four may be a stage".
- Aurobindo, Teilhard, Bucke [M]. Aurobindo, The Life Divine: matter, life, mind, then above mind Higher Mind, Illumined Mind, Intuition, Overmind, Supermind; Overmind still divides the aspects of the One into separate powers and Supermind sees them as one [M-]; the point is a descent that transforms life, not escape. Teilhard, The Phenomenon of Man (French 1955; English 1959): matter, life, mind, Omega, and union differentiates, so the end keeps persons; it answers the worry that return means loss of self. Bucke (9.3). The nearest precedents for a "new stage of evolution"; the Traditionalists reject all three.
- The perennialist comparers on levels [M]. Smith, Forgotten Truth: four levels of reality matched to four of selfhood, the higher including the lower. Schuon: degrees of Reality (Beyond-Being, Being, Existence), and for the Muslim path sharia, tariqa, haqiqa. Nasr, An Introduction to Islamic Cosmological Doctrines (1964) and Knowledge and the Sacred: the cosmic hierarchy and the five Divine Presences. Guenon, The Symbolism of the Cross and The Multiple States of the Being: a vertical axis of degrees and a horizontal axis of range, crossing at the Universal Man (a coincidence with Alex's drawing of two axes meeting at a centre, not a source). Coomaraswamy: the sun as the single exit of the cosmos. Burckhardt: planetary heavens in Ibn Arabi, the nearest Traditionalist treatment of "heavens as stages". Perry's stage chapters are unknown. Huxley has no stage map of his own that the files recall, and for stages he is the weakest of the sources to cite [U]. Method: cosmos and person are one ladder, so distance words stand for degrees of reality and not kilometres; Traditionalist readers will say "up" is symbolic. The strict school (Guenon, Coomaraswamy, Schuon, in good part Nasr) rejects evolution and reads history as decline, so "new stage of evolution" runs against it; Smith is softer. Wilber and Brown: 8.1 and 8.6. Hawkins, Power vs. Force (1995), a popular scale of levels with a threshold at 200 whose muscle-testing method scientists do not accept, is mentioned only because a reader may bring it up; not a tradition and not a witness.
- Other maps [M]. The Jewish ladder of rungs: Moshe Chaim Luzzatto, Mesillat Yesharim (1740), built on the baraita of Rabbi Pinchas ben Yair (Avodah Zarah 20b; a shorter form in Mishnah Sotah 9:15 [U]): Torah leads to watchfulness, zeal, cleanliness, separation, purity, piety, humility, fear of sin, holiness, the holy spirit and the revival of the dead [order U], a ladder whose last rung is resurrection, a return; Maimonides, Guide for the Perplexed III.51, the parable of the king's palace, people standing at different distances from the king. The five khands of the Japji (pauris 34 to 37): Dharam (duty), Gian (knowledge), Saram (effort), Karam (grace), Sach (truth) [English glosses vary]. The five sheaths (Taittiriya Upanishad II): food, breath, mind, understanding, bliss, a ladder of selves and the closest Indian match to Smith's levels of selfhood. The seven upper lokas (Bhu, Bhuvar, Svar, Mahar, Jana, Tapas, Satya) and seven lower. Bernard of Clairvaux, On Loving God: four degrees of love, from loving oneself for oneself to loving oneself for God's sake, the last rarely reached in this life. Rumi, Masnavi III about lines 3901 to 3906 [U]: dying as mineral, becoming plant, animal, human, then angel, then passing beyond the angel to what no mind has conceived; a stage map with something after the top rung; often read as Darwinian evolution, where the older reading is the arc of descent and ascent of the Islamic Neoplatonic cosmos; quote only from a named translation. Kukai's ten stages (10.3); the Daoist inner alchemy in four steps (essence into qi, qi into spirit, spirit into emptiness, emptiness into union with the Dao) and Zhuangzi 1's bird Peng rising ninety thousand li on a whirlwind while a cicada and a dove laugh, an old speed picture useful beside run, bike, car, fly, orbit [M].

Beyond the last rung: four answers (P18 §2.20). Rest: Jain siddha; Theravada parinibbana as usually glossed; Yoga's kaivalya. No end: Nyssa's epektasis; Ibn Arabi's no repetition (inference); Mulla Sadra. Return: bodhisattva; tenth ox picture; jivanmukta and the Gita; Teresa's seventh mansion; baqa on one reading; Plato's cave; Theosophy's Nirmanakaya; Campbell's monomyth, seventeen stages in departure, initiation and return (his, 1949; Vogler's twelve is a screenwriting adaptation), with the refusal of the return as a stage. No ladder: Gaudapada; sudden Zen (the Shenxiu and Huineng verses; quote from a named edition); Wilber's one taste. All four exist, and the teacher's "birth" belongs with return and new beginning. The "rest, return and no end" placements are interpretations: parinibbana as "rest" is a common gloss and not a Pali word; baqa as "return to the world" is one reading; Ibn Arabi's no repetition does not itself say "no last rung" (P18 C17).

Scholars (P18 §5, M). Katz (1978): tradition shapes the experience from the start, so cross-tradition stage maps may match the writers' vocabulary and not events; Forman answers with contentless events; Zaehner says there are at least three kinds; Ferrer says Wilber-style hierarchies assume one summit and argues for plural ends. Repeated sevens are weak evidence of a shared inner structure, since planets and calendar explain them. Theosophy: Hanegraaff and Godwin read it as part of the history of Western esotericism, a 19th-century synthesis; Lopez, Prisoners of Shangri-La (1998), is critical of how it reshapes Tibetan Buddhist terms; K. Paul Johnson, The Masters Revealed (1994), argues the Masters were identifiable historical persons [M-]. Traditionalism: Sedgwick places it historically. Dante and the Mi'raj: possible, unproven.

What the draft already says in this area (P18 §12, read-only check): Teresa's seven mansions (add dates); early Buddhism's four stages; Shantideva's born-into-the-family line (3.25, numbering U); "in Eckhart a lasting state" (Eckhart also puts a breakthrough after the birth, Sermon 52, so one clause may help since the essay uses "birth" for the stage after plus four); "other traditions draw staged maps too" (safe); the Hindu twice-born as second birth (matches). The draft's perennial paragraph names Huxley as the one who made the phrase famous; 5.7 and 5.8 give the movement's other voices if Alex wants the paragraph less Huxley-centred. Suggestion (P18's reading, not a fact): if Part two's skeleton is meant to lean on the movement and not on one anthologist, Smith's four levels (for the arrow) and Schuon's exoteric against esoteric (for "same things in different words") are the movement's own frames and could stand beside or in place of Huxley's three-part definition.

Traps in this area (P18 §11, M or U): "Mysticism is the art of union with Reality" is Practical Mysticism (1914); Underhill's Part Two is "The Mystic Way" and her 1913 book of that name is different; "Christ has no body now but yours", credited to Teresa, has no source known to the files [U]; the Mithraic grades are Jerome's and not Origen's; the Mi'raj heavens are hadith and not Qur'an 17:1, and the second heaven has two prophets; the Eckhart "thank you" line is unsourced [U] while "the eye with which I see God" is genuine; Eckhart asking God to rid him of God is Sermon 52, wording differs by translation (Walshe: "free of God"); the Simurgh pun is Attar's, not the name's etymology; Huineng's verse is from later editions; the Heart Sutra's Sanskrit may be a back-translation; Campbell's monomyth has seventeen stages and the twelve-stage version is Vogler's; Rumi's mineral, plant, animal poem is not Darwinian; "seven is the number of rungs everywhere" is false.

## 12. How the movement compares religions

Source: S3 (catalogue of methods) and S5 (why the religions differ), which synthesise P01 to P18; every wording is recalled or carried and needs the book open. Scope: the Traditionalists and the Theosophists attacked each other, so neither family is quoted as a team and the methods do not sit in one box.

### 12.1 Catalogue of methods

1. The thematic anthology (Huxley, Perry, Stace, Wilson, Novak, Needleman, Eliade; on the Theosophical side Walker, 1888, and Head and Cranston on rebirth) [M]. See section 6 for the worked row ("the other is oneself") and what it hides. Strength: fast and readable; shows that wording can be set side by side across many traditions. Weakness: the headings belong to the anthologist, each excerpt was picked for fit, context is cut, and the translation often carries the equation already; Huxley read the translations of his day, and for Eckhart the usual source is Pfeiffer's 1857 edition, which mixes authentic and doubtful sermons. Schuon rejects the arithmetic of a common factor; Huxley's other move, that knowledge is a function of the knower's being, makes the claim hard to refute (the objection is the files' own). For Alex: he differs from Huxley in that Huxley subtracts to a common factor and Alex derives from an axiom and then shows examples. Take each passage from a named primary translation and not through an anthology; state a selection rule.
2. The parallel-passage method (Coomaraswamy, Otto, Shah-Kazemi; Guenon states the doctrine first in Sanskrit terms and reads the rest as saying it in other words) [M]. Coomaraswamy's unit is one sentence or symbol followed through Sanskrit, Greek, Latin and German with a short comment; Otto sets Eckhart beside Shankara term by term. Strength: a reader can open the line and check it; Otto is the model of a comparer who lists the differences. Weakness: one voice for three thousand years, tilted to Advaita (the standard complaint, recalled); his reading of Buddhism moves between 1916 and 1943; Rahula's objection; Theravada reads brahma-bhuta as "become holy"; his school refuses syncretism and would not accept Alex's witness method. Borrow: quote one line at a time from a named translation; put the comparer in the sentence ("Otto sets Eckhart's Godhead beside Shankara's nirguna Brahman", not "they are the same"); compare inner voice with inner voice, never a catechism beside the Gita; if Coomaraswamy answers "who carries over", state the price.
3. The glossary equation of terms (Dara Shikoh; Blavatsky's Key, 1889, and the Theosophical Glossary, 1892, posthumous, ed. Mead; Purucker's Occult Glossary, 1933; Schuon's grid; Guenon on Universal Man; Subba Row's "Aryan-Arhat" essay; Faivre names this practice "concordance" and calls it a frequent but secondary mark of Western esotericism) [C, M]. Working example: Dara (2.2). Strained example: Blavatsky read the Tibetan bde-ba-can as Sanskrit deva plus chan, "dwelling of the gods", and equated devachan with Sukhavati; the Tibetan renders Sukhavati, a place of practice reached by Amitabha's vow and not a rest between lives [M for direction, spellings U]; alaya as Universal Soul, Anima Mundi and Over-Soul is the same move, though alaya-vijnana is an individual stream carrying seeds [M]. Strength: shows which words do the same job; Subba Row gave it an honest form (the count of principles differs between schemes because each cuts the same thing at different joints). Weakness: the comparer picks the match, and the equation is made before the reader sees a quotation; Dara's Vedanta came out in Sufi words; specialists keep apart Ein Sof and Brahman (Scholem, Idel), emptiness and Brahman (Loy), buddhi and "spiritual soul" (Subba Row himself); the glossaries lean on nineteenth-century Orientalists and many etymologies are fanciful [U]. Borrow: keep the table, label each row as a comparer's equation; Panikkar's term for the honest version is homeomorphic equivalents; Loy's rule is to give each sense of a shared word its own name; write "Schuon equates nirvana with the Absolute", keeping the essay's own line that emptiness means no fixed independent nature.
4. Symbols and myths (Blavatsky: egg, serpent, lotus, tree, cross, keys; Guenon, Symbolism of the Cross; Coomaraswamy, the Sun door and Figures of Speech or Figures of Thought; Campbell; Jung's amplification; Eliade's morphology; Mead on the Poimandres) [M]. Example: the veil of Isis. Blavatsky's title plays on the inscription at Sais. Plutarch (On Isis and Osiris, about section 9) puts it on a statue of Athena whom the Egyptians identify with Isis, and writes "robe" (peplos) and not "veil". "Veil" comes later: Kant quotes it in a footnote in section 49 of the Critique of Judgment, and Proclus gives a longer form [locators U]. A symbol is edited as it travels. A second case: Coomaraswamy's Sun door (Mundaka 1.2.11, John 10:9, the clashing rocks). Strength: a symbol carries an idea without a doctrine; Alex's machine is of this kind. Weakness: nearly any story can be read through a key, so the method cannot be tested; J. Z. Smith: similarity is made by the comparer. Borrow: use images as pictures and say whose image each is; give the origin and check the wording; keep Campbell's two scales (return to the world, return to the One) apart.
5. The exoteric and esoteric split (Guenon, Introduction, 1921; Schuon, French 1948; Blavatsky: an outer form for the many and an inner meaning for the few, the "Eye Doctrine" and "Heart Doctrine"; Besant, Esoteric Christianity, 1901; Smith's two books) [C, M]. Example: Schuon on exclusivism (the outer layer must present itself as the one truth, because that makes a form work for a whole society, and the inner layer sees the outer as a symbol) and his pair salvation and deliverance. The cost: the Church condemned the Eckhart proposition about something uncreated in the soul (27 March 1329, after his death). Strength: it gives a reason why popular teaching may not carry the idea; Alex's own rule of 2026-10-07 in source form. Weakness: it cannot fail, since a text that disagrees is assigned to the outer layer and the esoteric is defined as whatever agrees with the metaphysics; the Theosophical version adds priestly corruption and deliberate blinds; Schuon's grid is Advaita and Sufi theory laid over other traditions and Scholem and Idel do not use it. Borrow: credit Schuon and the Traditionalists for the rule and not only Huxley, and say it is a theory some scholars reject; use it as a reading rule with a stop: for each quote name the layer (scripture, mystic, popular teaching) and say where the popular layer says something else; do not rescue a disagreeing text by moving it outward; write a specialist's objection as what the specialist adds and not as a verdict that the tradition fits badly.
6. The primordial tradition, or comparison by descent (Plethon, Ficino's prisca theologia, Steuco; Guenon's primordial Tradition; Blavatsky's Wisdom-Religion; Smith, whose title phrase is Guenon's) [C, M]. Example: Blavatsky's Introductory calls the Secret Doctrine the universally diffused religion of the ancient and prehistoric world, with proofs in the "secret crypts of libraries" kept by the Occult Brotherhood [about 80 percent on the wording, not for printing]; Steuco's one principle and one knowledge among all peoples. Both explain agreement as inheritance and difference as loss of keys, literal reading or priestcraft. Strength: it supplies a mechanism, an old one. Weakness: the historical claim failed (Casaubon, 1614); no one has found the Book of Dzyan or any Senzar; Coleman traced much of her text to printed books (1895); most historical comparers had a home team. Borrow: do not. Say in one sentence that Alex claims no original revelation and no lost source; his explanation of agreement is a fourth kind (common logic) beside revelation, transmission and common experience. Add that he has no team.
7. Stage maps (Underhill, Teresa, Climacus, the Jain fourteen, Attar, Kukai, Besant and Leadbeater, Smith, Wilber) [M]. See 11.5. Working example of a term equation: dharma-megha, the tenth bhumi in Buddhism and the last samadhi in Yoga Sutra IV.29. Strained example: Besant and Leadbeater map the four Buddhist stages onto the first four of five initiations, with Christian labels. Strength: a shared shape of distance words. Weakness: rungs run from four to a hundred; sevens may come from the planets; Katz would say maps may match the writers' vocabulary and Ferrer that one ladder assumes one summit; the strict Traditionalists read history as decline, and Smith's levels are worlds and not stages. Borrow: say the number of rungs varies and map by function and not by count; claim no one-for-one match with Underhill or Teresa; say the arrow is for a person and makes no claim that the world improves.
8. Each religion as a perspective on one Reality (Schuon: Absolute and Infinite, Beyond-Being, Being, Existence; Guenon: Universal Possibility; Smith: four levels; Hick: the Real in itself, met through personal and impersonal faces; Schmidt-Leukel) [M]. Schuon fixes the level before equating: Nirguna Brahman, Dhat and Eckhart's Gottheit sit at Beyond-Being; Ishvara, Allah as Lord and God the Father at Being. The Shahada read as discernment (nothing real but the Real) then concentration on the Real is set beside neti neti then identity with Atma. Why the forms differ: the Infinite includes every possibility, receptivity differs, and a form that stressed its own relativity would no longer save anyone [a reconstruction, not Schuon's numbered list]. Strength: it explains "sometimes different gods" without calling the gods the same, and offers an argument for why a whole would separate. Weakness: the grid is circular and tilted to Advaita and Sufism; Buddhist specialists keep nirvana and the Absolute apart; Scholem, Idel and Orthodox and Catholic theologians keep a line between Source and many that the comparers erase; Plantinga and D'Costa say Hick's pluralism is itself a doctrine; the Traditionalists rest the claim on doctrine and the Intellect and not experience, so Katz's attack mostly misses them. Borrow: one sentence such as "one comparer reads the personal God and the impersonal Absolute as two degrees of one thing, and Buddhists and many theologians would not accept the equation"; offer "the Infinite includes the finite" as an argument credited to the movement and labelled an argument and not a proof.
9. The claimed esoteric revelation (the Theosophical Society, 1875: the Masters; the Mahatma Letters, about 1880 to 1884, published 1923, Barker, and Hao Chin 1993; the Book of Dzyan and its Stanzas; Leadbeater's clairvoyant reports; Bailey writing for "the Tibetan") [M]. Example: the three propositions of the Secret Doctrine Proem are given as what she was taught and not as logic. "Over-Soul" is Emerson's word, and in her third proposition it is "an aspect of the Unknown Root", a softer step than Alex's axiom [structure M, wording U]. The authority rule: where a text and a Master disagree, the Master wins. Her Preface calls the book a nosegay with only the string her own, but that line is Montaigne's (Essais III.12), which she quotes. Strength: it answers why the traditions agree; an early, bold act of respect for Asian texts; Gandhi's Autobiography says the Key sent him back to his own scriptures [gist, chapter U]. Weakness: it cannot be checked; the SPR's Hodgson report (1885) judged her phenomena fraudulent and Harrison, "J'Accuse", Journal of the SPR 53 (1986), pp. 286 to 310, challenged it in the SPR's own journal (whether the SPR ever withdrew the report is unchecked [U]); Muller wrote against "Esoteric Buddhism" in The Nineteenth Century (1893); the stock reply is the Buddha's "closed fist of a teacher" (acariyamutthi, Digha 16); Isis Unveiled did not teach reincarnation as the rule (that is in Sinnett's Esoteric Buddhism, the Key and the Secret Doctrine). Borrow: not the method. Borrow the stance of gathering what lies scattered and saying what is one's own. Olcott's Fourteen Fundamental Buddhist Beliefs (accepted 1891 by authorities of several schools, signatories U, drafted earlier, 1884 U) is the one Theosophical comparison shown to the people compared. The contrast sentence is ready: her reason for agreement is a lost original and hidden teachers, and his is an axiom and the reader's own check. Hammer names three strategies in this literature (appeal to tradition, to science, to experience); Alex's method is none of them.
10. Comparison by experience (James, Underhill, Bucke, Otto, Stace; the foils Zaehner, Smart, Katz, Forman) [M]. See section 9. James's second conclusion is the best authority for "not a proof", and his test by fruits supports "what you can check". Borrow: say the claim is about texts and ideas, with "reports" and not "the same experience"; a family of ideas survives Katz at the level of structure, which is all Alex claims; use James's limit on outsiders as his permission.
11. More methods the files show. Dialogue and negotiation: Cusa's De pace fidei (1453), Akbar's Ibadat Khana (1575), the Parliament of 1893; the yield was peace and policy, rarely a finding of sameness. Practice: Ramakrishna's lake, Gandhi's common prayer; Schuon sets the Name side by side (japa, dhikr, the Jesus Prayer, the nembutsu); Yoga Sutra 1.12 joins practice and letting go in one line; sameness is lived before it is argued, which suits Part three. Structure without identity: Izutsu, Sufism and Taoism (1966); Sells (1994); Loy, Nonduality (1988): they set shapes side by side and say where they diverge. Ranking: Zongmi, Kukai, Vivekananda and the Baha'i scheme put their own tradition on top; Hacker called Hindu tolerance inclusivism; Gita 4.11, 7.21 and 9.23 are inclusivist. Every comparer has a top, and Alex's is his axiom.

### 12.2 Explanations of why the religions differ

Every comparer answers "why do the religions differ if they say the same thing", and every answer is the comparer's claim and not the tradition's own (P01, P13). The files sort them into ten families (S5 §1) [C, M]:
1. One teaching, then decay: Plethon, Steuco, Blavatsky, Guenon in part.
2. Same idea, other words: Pico, Dara Shikoh, Rumi's grapes, Subba Row.
3. One faith, many rites: Cusa, Vivekananda.
4. Stages of education, or a teacher adapted to an age: Lessing, Leibniz, Besant, Baha'i.
5. Outer form and inner teaching, with the diversity willed: Schuon, Guenon, Besant.
6. Levels of Reality: Schuon, Smith.
7. Temperament and path: Huxley, Ramakrishna, the Gita, Hodson.
8. Partial standpoints: the Udana, Jain many-sidedness, Rumi's elephant.
9. One Real through different filters: Hick.
10. The differences are real and should be argued and not explained away: Griffiths, Zaehner, Katz, Prothero, Ferrer.

Alex has a different kind of answer to a different question. P01 lists three old explanations for the agreement itself (common revelation, common transmission, common experience) and says his is a fourth, common logic. That explains why the same ideas keep turning up. It says nothing about why the words differ [M].

The individual answers and what scholars say:
- Schuon, the difference is willed: 3.4. Scholars (standard criticism, who said what U): the grid decides what counts as inner; it is Vedanta and Sufi theory laid over the rest; history is set aside; Theravada scholars deny that nirvana is the Absolute; Scholem and Idel read Kabbalah without the grid; Katz aimed at the common-core claim about experience, and whether he touches Schuon is U. Serious allegations have been reported about Schuon's circle, so cite the books and not the man.
- Guenon, exoteric and esoteric, with decline and orthodoxy: 3.2. The same move gave two verdicts on Christianity inside one school. Besant's Esoteric Christianity reads the creed as the outer face of the lesser mysteries (T08, not in this dossier). Scholars: the rule cannot lose; Sedgwick calls Guenon's approach theological and not historical; Legenhausen finds the comparisons tenuous; the strict school would refuse Alex's method.
- Huxley: temperament (the chapter "Religion and Temperament", the Gita's three yogas, Sheldon's body types), idolatry, and the condition in the knower; and a highest common factor (5.4). Scholars: Zaehner, Stace, Katz.
- Smith: levels and the two books (7). Scholars: the levels can reassign any disagreement upward; the grid puts both Brahman and emptiness at the Infinite.
- Blavatsky and her circle [M; locators unknown]. Her explanation of agreement is descent from one archaic Wisdom-Religion kept by initiates, with proofs said to lie in the crypts of libraries of the Occult Brotherhood. Her explanation of difference is loss, in five parts: an outer form for the many and an inner teaching for the few, so disagreement sits in the outer form; symbols read literally, so allegory became history; keys lost, the Mysteries closed, priestcraft kept the husk; adaptation, each age getting a teacher who restates the one teaching (Buddha, Krishna, Zoroaster, Jesus); cycles of decline, the Kali Yuga. The "blinds" (deliberate veils set by the initiates) appear in one line in one file (T07) and nowhere else; treat it as a pointer; find her own use in the Secret Doctrine first; meaning and place U. Her circle adds more: Besant's Four Great Religions (1897) has each Great Teacher give the same truth in the form suited to a people and an age [chapter content U]; Hodson ties differences in religious feeling to seven types [title M, content U]; Subba Row argues that counts of a person's principles differ because each scheme cuts the same thing at different joints, and he still disagreed with Blavatsky and Sinnett on the count [grouping and specific points U]; Olcott's fourteen propositions of 1891 are the one case where leaders of different Buddhist schools were asked to accept a short common list. Scholars: the method cannot fail, since agreement confirms it and disagreement counts as corruption; it reads Indian and Buddhist terms through a Western occult frame; Coleman; Muller's 1893 article and the closed-fist reply; historians reject claims that a Church council banned rebirth; and her treatment of Jehovah as a lower power breaks Alex's rule never to say the Abrahamic religions fit badly (T02).
- Scripture, parables and saints: 10.1 and 10.2. The Rigveda, the Gita and the Udana were not written for pluralism; Quran 5:48 says each community has its own law and way; Rumi's grapes is the cleanest parable about words; Ramakrishna's lake and Vivekananda's streams; Coomaraswamy's mountain and "one of His".
- Hick and the realists: Hick (An Interpretation of Religion, 1989) says the Real an sich lies beyond every human concept and the traditions are different human responses, filtered in a Kantian way and met as persons or as impersonal absolutes, and the test is whether a tradition moves people from self-centredness to Reality-centredness; he accepts that concepts shape experience and defends "one Real, many responses" and not a common core of experience. Critics: Plantinga, D'Costa, Buddhists, and the point that if nothing can be said of the Real then "it is one" is out of bounds; the price is calling the traditions partly mistaken about themselves. The realists: Griffiths (contradictory doctrines should be argued, not explained away), Zaehner (loving union with a personal God differs from union by identity), Katz, Prothero, Loy ("not two" does not mean "one"), J. Z. Smith (the comparer makes the similarity and must state the respect and include difference). Alex's claim concerns texts and ideas, and Katz's concerns experience.
- The "different words, same thing" move is old: Pico, Dara, Cusa, Lessing's ring, Leibniz's gold (2.2). The risk is that the comparer picks the match, and the old comparers mostly had a home team. Translators carry equations too (P07). The careful defenders share rules (13.5).

### 12.3 What all the comparers share, and where they fight

(S5 §3, P01, P13.) Every answer is the comparer's. Nearly every one keeps the difference on the surface and the agreement underneath; the realists in 12.2 are the exception. Every one has a top: Dara reads Vedanta in Sufi words, Vivekananda ranks Advaita highest, Kukai puts Shingon highest, the Baha'i scheme puts the latest revelation last, and Schuon's grid is Vedanta and Sufi theory. Every one deals with a counterexample by reassigning it: to the outer layer, a lower level, corruption, an earlier stage or a filter.

Where they fight [C, M]. Guenon attacked Theosophy (1921, 1923) while sharing the idea of a primordial teaching, and split with Schuon over Christianity. Subba Row disagreed with Blavatsky on the count [U]. Zaehner argued against Huxley, and Katz against Stace. On lives the movement splits: Guenon rejects Western reincarnation, Coomaraswamy has one transmigrant, Blavatsky taught rebirth by the 1880s. P01's fair summary: the agreement is partly real, partly made, and the makers had a stake.

What each costs. Descent from one source failed as history (Casaubon redated the Hermetica in 1614; Yates and Walker called the lineage mistaken history). Outer and inner cannot lose. Levels are one school's grid. Temperament and stages carry the Sheldon and progress problems. Standpoint parables need a teller who sees the whole. Hick's filter is the cleanest philosophically and is attacked from both sides.

Rigour and permission trade [S5's reading]. The strict authors (Guenon, Schuon, Coomaraswamy) demand one form and a lineage and refuse mixing. The loose ones (Huxley, Perry, Wilson) mix freely and explain less. Alex's witness method is nearer the loose camp and wants the strict camp's care. On Race's map of exclusivist, inclusivist and pluralist, an axiom on top with the traditions as witnesses sits with inclusivism, and Hick's pluralism is the one that puts no frame on top. Say so before a reader does.

Four ways to say it (S5 §4; the drafts are in S5 for Alex to put in his own voice). A, name the match and keep it small (anchors J. Z. Smith, Panikkar, Loy, and Dara's lesson that the comparer picks the match; each pair gets a named "both say X about Y", for example both say the sense of a separate self is made and can end). B, outer face and inner face, credited and limited (Schuon, Guenon, the Theosophist version; credit it, say what it costs, tell the reader which layer each quote comes from and invite them to catch him filing awkward texts under the outer face; it would be honest to add that the strict school would not accept the method since he has no lineage). C, one thing seen from different places, with the teller problem (Rumi's grapes fits best because it is about words; the link to the computer picture is S5's and no author's; it meets Newbigin's point if Alex says he is not the teller who sees the whole). D, decline to say why, and show your top (the ten families are each the comparer's claim; the axiom explains recurrence and not difference, so leave that open; every comparer ends up with a top; Dara read the Upanishads in Sufi words and Vivekananda put Advaita highest; mine is the axiom). S5: A and D fit together best; C is the warmest and the most quotable; B is in the draft already and should be named.

### 12.4 Wording equations that stay true to each tradition

(S3 practical guide, all [M] unless marked.)
1. Name the respect. Write "both say the sense of a separate self is built and can end", never "they teach the same thing".
2. Claim texts and ideas, not feelings. Say "reports", "a family of ideas", "keep turning up". The draft's own wording is right.
3. Give the tradition's own term first, then the gloss, then its job. Maya means dependent, neither fully real nor simply unreal, and "illusion" translates it badly. Blavatsky's own gloss makes maya what changes and ends, close to mithya.
4. Attribute every equation to its maker: "Schuon sets nirvana beside the Absolute and many Buddhist scholars keep them apart"; "Blavatsky equated devachan with Sukhavati, and the Tibetan name renders Sukhavati". Say "the teaching later called wahdat al-wujud", since the phrase is not in Ibn Arabi's own works as far as the files know.
5. Match inner with inner at the same level, and say which layer each quote comes from. Never rescue a disagreeing text by moving it outward.
6. Say where the grounds differ under similar words. For the golden rule: reciprocity (Hillel, Analects 15.24, Matthew 7:12), analogy (Gita 6.32, Sutta Nipata 705), identity (Isha 6 and 7, Gita 13.28). Shantideva reaches compassion by the ownerlessness of suffering, and the Acharanga line reads as oneness only in one modern commentary, while Jacobi's translation is more careful.
7. Keep each tradition's own account of what continues. Buddhism: no fixed self and yet something continues (the lamp-flame in the Milindapanha). Advaita: the one Self is the only transmigrant, and the individual does not carry over. Judaism: gilgul, which Saadia rejected. Islam: growth after death without return (Mulla Sadra) and tanasukh mostly rejected. Christianity: one life, purification, then epektasis. Say "rebirth" or "transmigration", since "reincarnation" carries Spiritist freight.
8. Use each tradition's own relation word. Christianity keeps participation and not identity. Chabad is "close to acosmism in the Tanya's reading", which is not "the world does not exist" [U]. In Judaism separation is a rupture to repair. Jainism agrees on the practice and objects to the One, since souls are many. The Traditionalists reject Theosophical return, though Schuon's own word on Theosophy is unchecked [U].
9. Quote only from a named translation; do not take lines from memory, an anthology or a quote site. Traps: "Truth is one, the wise call it by many names" (Rigveda 1.164.46 is about names of gods; Gandhi and Vivekananda quoted it); loose "Rumi" lines (use Nicholson or a named translator); Eckhart on "thank you" (unsourced [U]); "He who knows himself knows his Lord" and "I was a hidden treasure" (Sufi sayings, not accepted as hadith); "There is no religion higher than truth" (the Benares family motto, adopted by the Society around 1880, not Blavatsky's); "heresy of separateness" (unplaced [U]); "Over-Soul" (Emerson's); "doors of perception" (Blake's).
10. Write an objection as what a specialist adds, and never as "this tradition fits badly". Never claim a tradition says what it does not. The draft's habit is the model.
11. Say once that every comparer had a home team and a top, and that Alex has no team and his top is his axiom.
12. Say once that the movement is plural: the perennial philosophy is a family of books and schools and not one doctrine (Guenon from 1921, Coomaraswamy, Schuon 1948, Perry's anthology 1971, Smith 1976); Huxley (1945) made the phrase familiar to English readers; the authors disagree with each other on rebirth, method and Theosophy; Alex uses them as witnesses and not as a team.

### 12.5 Equation tables

Each line is a comparer's equation, not a claim each tradition makes. Compiled from P04, P05, P06, P09, P14, P15, S3. [M] unless marked.

| Idea | Terms set side by side | Whose equation | Who objects or what to note |
|---|---|---|---|
| The Absolute without qualities | Nirguna Brahman and Atma; Dhat; Eckhart's Gottheit; Ein Sof [U as Schuon's pairing]; the nameless Tao [U as his pairing]; the Good beyond being (Republic 509b) and the One; nirvana, sunyata, Dharmakaya | Schuon, Smith, Huxley, Perry | Buddhist specialists keep nirvana and the Absolute apart; Scholem and Idel keep Ein Sof apart from Brahman; Orthodox and Catholic theologians reject the mapping of Godhead and Trinity |
| The Absolute as Person | Saguna Brahman, Ishvara; Allah as Lord; God the Father or Logos; the Sefirot | Schuon, Smith | The Logos is strictly the Son, so the row is loose; "Allah" is also used for the Essence |
| The world as veil | Maya; "He and not He" and hijab; samsara; the cave | Schuon, Chittick, Izutsu | Madhyamaka has no substrate; maya is not the Yogacara imagined nature |
| The deepest subject | Atman; nous; Eckhart's spark; Spirit; ruh; buddhi | Coomaraswamy, Schuon, Smith | Theravada reads anatta as no self; the Pali atta is not the Upanishadic Atman |
| The faculty that knows | nous, intellectus, qalb and ruh, buddhi and hridaya, the mind in the heart | Schuon, Smith | Eckhart's uncreated spark was condemned in 1329 |
| The end | moksha, fana and baqa, theosis, nirvana, jivan-mukti, Eckhart's birth | Schuon, Guenon | Buddhists; Zaehner says Sufi fana is not Vedantic identity [U on Buddhist and Taoist pairs] |
| Remembrance as return | japa, dhikr, the Jesus Prayer, the nembutsu | Schuon | Each has primary sources that can be checked without Schuon |
| Forgetting as fall | ghafla and Quran 7:172; anamnesis; avidya | Coomaraswamy, Schuon [U as his] | Fall stories against overflow stories (11.2) |
| The perfect man | al-Insan al-Kamil, Purusha, Adam Kadmon, Wang, Christ | Guenon, Schaya | Adam Kadmon is not Adam ha-Rishon |
| Levels of presence | Hahut to Nasut; the Mandukya's four states; the four worlds | Nasr, Smith [U as Schuon's] | A ladder of worlds and not of personal stages |
| Letting go | Gelassenheit, bittul, kenosis, fana, wu wei, islam as surrender | Schuon, Jung | Each differs (Pali sankhitta is a dull mind) |
| The neighbour as self | tat tvam asi, Isha 6, Matthew 22:39, karuna and metta, the hadith of the brother | Schuon, Schopenhauer | Leviticus and Matthew ground love in shared status under God; Shantideva in the lack of a fixed self |
| Transmigration | samsara; change of state | Guenon | Indologists read the texts as ordinary rebirth |
| Many names | Rigveda 1.164.46; Kabir's Ram and Rahim; Rumi's grapes; Ramakrishna's jal, pani, water | many | The Rigveda verse is about the names of gods |
| Why the One becomes many | lila (Brahma Sutra 2.1.33); sunder warumbe; Heraclitus 52; Laws 803; the hidden-treasure saying; the broken vessels | Coomaraswamy and others | The hidden-treasure saying is not an accepted hadith; tzimtzum is a 16th-century doctrine |
| The door of the sun | Mundaka 1.2.11, John 10:9, the Symplegades | Coomaraswamy | Alex's door is in the middle of the chart; his is the exit from the cosmos |
| A term equation | dharma-megha as the tenth bhumi and Yoga Sutra IV.29 | the comparers | Yoga is dualist, so kaivalya is not Advaita liberation |

## 13. The scholarly debate (for the author, not the essay)

Source: P12 (main), with P08, P10, P11, P13, S5. Everything is [M] or [U]; no quotation is cleared. P12's purpose: for Alex's own understanding. This section marks what a knowledgeable reader will bring.

### 13.1 The shape of the debate

"Perennialism" in the scholarly argument means one of three claims (2.1): experience, doctrine or origin. Since 1978 the academic study of religion has leaned against the first claim. Katz argued that no experience is unmediated. Proudfoot and Sharf questioned "experience" as a category. Forman answered that an awareness with no content cannot be shaped by concepts it does not contain. The second claim was argued mostly in theology and philosophy: Hick said the traditions respond to one Real; Griffiths, Plantinga and D'Costa said that this is itself a doctrine few of the people it describes would accept. The third claim became intellectual history: Hanegraaff and Sedgwick study Theosophy and Traditionalism as modern movements with founders, quarrels and politics. Above all of these sits a question of method: how to compare at all (13.5).

The Traditionalist form of the second claim does not rest on mystical experience at all. Guenon set metaphysics (a knowledge reached by intellectual intuition and transmitted by initiation) against mysticism (which he put in the religious, emotional, individual domain), and Schuon speaks of intellection and not of experience [M; Schuon's term U]. So the experience debate (Katz, Forman, Proudfoot, Sharf) is mostly about Stace, James and the experiential camp and only indirectly about the Traditionalists; their critics are the historians (Sedgwick, Hanegraaff) and the philosophers of religion (Griffiths, Plantinga, D'Costa).

For Alex: his own claim (examples that fit, witnesses and not authorities, the same ideas in different words) is a modest form of the second claim and can be held as a family resemblance. The strong forms (same experience, one source, all traditions say the same) are the ones scholars contest hardest. And "the two movements he leans on" is looser than it sounds: the perennial philosophy is an umbrella (Traditionalists, Huxley, Smith, Wilber) whose members differ on rebirth, method and Theosophy, and Theosophy can be counted in the same family but the most rigorous branch of the umbrella fought it by name.

### 13.2 The experience debate

- Stace (Mysticism and Philosophy, 1960): a universal core; Zaehner (Mysticism Sacred and Profane, 1957): the opposite, with three types; Smart ("Interpretation and Mystical Experience", Religious Studies 1, 1965): doctrine feeds into how a state is experienced and described. The vocabulary a knowledgeable reader brings: core against interpretation. Alex's "same things in different words" is Stace's move if applied to experience, and safer applied to ideas. Title traps: Zaehner's Mysticism Sacred and Profane is not Eliade's The Sacred and the Profane (German 1957, English 1959); keep apart Stace's Mysticism and Philosophy (1960), Katz's Mysticism and Philosophical Analysis (1978) and Forman's The Problem of Pure Consciousness: Mysticism and Philosophy (1990).
- Katz (9.7). The premise (all experience is conceptually mediated) is a philosophical assumption, stated more than shown, and untestable, since every report arrives already described. Katz is the name an informed critic will cite against any "mystics all agree" sentence. Write about texts and ideas; say "reports", not "the same experience".
- Peter Moore, "Mystical Experience, Mystical Doctrine, Mystical Technique" in Katz (1978), and "Recent Studies of Mysticism: A Critical Survey", Religion 3 (1973) [volume and year U]: separates experience, doctrine and technique and holds that interpretation runs through the experience at several levels, so a clean core cannot be pulled out of a wrapper; different techniques tend to produce different results, so some differences between traditions may trace to practice and not only belief; he does not conclude that nothing is shared. A strict contextualist says even technique is cultural; a perennialist says he concedes too much. Source for the sentence "experience and interpretation are tangled".
- Wayne Proudfoot, Religious Experience (University of California Press, 1985): the subject identifies an experience through concepts and beliefs, so it cannot be a free-standing, self-authenticating datum. Schleiermacher, James, Otto and Eliade treated religious experience as autonomous and beyond explanation; Proudfoot calls this a protective strategy. He separates a descriptive reduction (redescribing an experience in terms the subject would reject, a mistake) from an explanatory reduction (explaining it by causes, legitimate). Against: William Alston, Perceiving God (Cornell, 1991): a person can be justified in taking a religious experience as direct perception of God. A reminder that "experience proves it" is not available to Alex; his essay already says it is not a proof.
- Robert Sharf: "The Zen of Japanese Nationalism", History of Religions 33 (1993); "Buddhist Modernism and the Rhetoric of Meditative Experience", Numen 42 (1995); "Experience" in Mark C. Taylor (ed.), Critical Terms for Religious Studies (Chicago, 1998). The idea that Buddhist, especially Zen, practice centres on a private inner experience was built in the late nineteenth and twentieth centuries by Japanese modernizers (D. T. Suzuki, the Kyoto School) answering Western criticism, and by Western readers shaped by James. Premodern monastic life ran on ritual, doctrine and lineage; reports of meditative states are checked by the tradition's authorities. Rejoinder, reconstructed by the file [U]: the Pali discourses and later manuals (Buddhaghosa) describe meditative states in detail. Agreeing in part: David McMahan, The Making of Buddhist Modernism (2008); Evan Thompson, Why I Am Not a Buddhist (2020). If Alex cites Zen or Buddhist meditation, quote what the texts say and not what monks "experience".
- Forman and Griffiths: 9.7. Forman is the best scholarly ally for a limited cross-cultural claim: one state, interpretation differs.

### 13.3 Pluralism and the question of the Real

- John Hick, An Interpretation of Religion: Human Responses to the Transcendent (Yale, 1989; Gifford Lectures 1986 to 87); God and the Universe of Faiths (1973); Death and Eternal Life (1976, which I believe also proposed a series of lives in other worlds, a Western philosopher's version of rebirth [U]). The great post-axial traditions are different human responses to one ultimate Reality, the Real, which in itself lies beyond every human concept. Borrowing Kant's split between thing-in-itself and appearance, the Real is experienced through cultural filters as persons (Yahweh, Allah, Vishnu, the Trinity) or as impersonal absolutes (Brahman, the Dharmakaya, the Tao). The test is soteriological. Hick gives the philosophical form of what Alex does (one thing, many words), and his critics name the price: you must say the traditions are partly mistaken about their own claims. Against: Plantinga, "Pluralism: A Defense of Religious Exclusivism" (in Senor (ed.), The Rationality of Belief and the Plurality of Faith, Cornell, 1995; reprinted in Quinn and Meeker (eds.), The Philosophical Challenge of Religious Diversity, Oxford, 2000): pluralism is itself an exclusive claim; D'Costa, "The Impossibility of a Pluralist View of Religions", Religious Studies (1996): the theory is not neutral and does not match what the traditions say; a Buddhist version: nirvana is not a manifestation of a ground, so the Real is an import; and if nothing can be said of the Real, "it is one" is out of bounds.
- Paul Griffiths, An Apology for Apologetics (Orbis, 1991); Problems of Religious Diversity (Blackwell, 2001). Religious communities hold doctrines they treat as non-negotiable and some contradict each other (a permanent self versus none; a creator versus none); the respectful response is to say so and argue, not to stack the traditions into a common core; Hick-style pluralism is one more doctrinal stance. Hick and Schmidt-Leukel reply that the disputes concern models and metaphors and the shared fact is the transformation produced. A ready counter to "you make traditions agree when they do not"; Alex's essay already says where a specialist objects; keep that habit.
- Perry Schmidt-Leukel, Transformation by Integration (SCM, 2009); Religious Pluralism and Interreligious Theology: The Gifford Lectures (Orbis, 2017): the patterns of difference between religions recur inside each of them, a fractal structure; the contrast of a personal God and an impersonal absolute appears inside Christianity, Hinduism and Buddhism (for example Pure Land and Zen); he argues inductively with less reliance on Kant [examples U]. Fits Alex's practice of quoting inner layers.
- Ferrer: 8.2. David Loy, Nonduality: A Study in Comparative Philosophy (Yale, 1988): Advaita Vedanta, Madhyamaka and Yogacara, Zen and Daoism all deny dualisms but in several distinct senses; "not two" does not mean "one"; Buddhist emptiness is not a Brahman; how many senses he lists is U (four in one file, five as recalled, the fifth unnamed; do not state a number). Classical Advaitins and Madhyamikas argued against each other for centuries (Shankara against the Buddhists; Candrakirti against a self), so a comparer who joins them takes a side. A model of comparing that names the differences; it backs Alex's caveat that Buddhist interdependence is not a One.

### 13.4 History, esotericism, Traditionalism

- Wouter Hanegraaff, New Age Religion and Western Culture (Brill, 1996); Esotericism and the Academy (Cambridge, 2012); Western Esotericism: A Guide for the Perplexed (2013). Esotericism as a scholarly category for currents (Hermetism, Neoplatonism, Christian Kabbalah, alchemy, Rosicrucianism, Theosophy, occultism) that churches and the Enlightenment pushed out as rejected knowledge. He criticises "religionism", scholarship that assumes a transhistorical inner truth (with the Eranos circle in view [U]); he sees Theosophy as turning Western esotericism toward Asia. Antoine Faivre, Access to Western Esotericism (SUNY, 1994): four intrinsic marks (correspondences, living nature, imagination and mediations, transmutation) plus two relative ones, transmission and the practice of concordance, the search for what traditions share; concordance is thus on his list of what esotericists do. Olav Hammer, Claiming Knowledge (Brill, 2001): the rhetoric of appeals to ancient tradition, science and experience, the footnote for "the ancient traditions agree". Faivre's definition is called too narrow; Kocku von Stuckrad treats the field as a discourse about higher knowledge.
- Mark Sedgwick, Against the Modern World (2004) and Western Sufism (Oxford, 2016). Traditionalism is a small, well-networked current from Guenon through Schuon, Coomaraswamy, Evola and a wider circle (including Nasr), with traces in comparative religion, art, architecture, Sufism and, via Evola, the radical right. Its core is a primordial tradition, decline through ages, rejection of modernity and the need for initiation inside one real tradition. A historian: he tracks people, orders and publications and does not rule on the metaphysics. Traditionalist authors object that politics and Evola get too much space; Sedgwick's reply is that influence is a historical fact. Naming Schuon borrows a figure of a movement hostile to modernity and to mix-and-match religion. The book covers controversies around Schuon's later community, which the files could not check. Read Sedgwick before naming Schuon, Guenon or Evola in print.
- The two movements disagree (Guenon against Theosophy, and rebirth): 2.4, 3.2. Isis Unveiled (1877) argued against the spiritists' return of the individual to earth; the Theosophical doctrine of repeated earthly lives belongs to the 1880s onward. So "Guenon against Theosophy on rebirth" is also "the early Blavatsky against the later Theosophy". The draft's sentence calling Isis Unveiled (1877) and The Secret Doctrine (1888) one of the main routes by which English readers met karma and reincarnation is weak in its 1877 half; check the books before changing the draft.
- The orientalism and inclusivism critiques [P12 f15]. Richard King, Orientalism and Religion (Routledge, 1999): the Mystic East and the idea of a universal mysticism were shaped by colonial-era Western writers and Indian reformers answering them (Schopenhauer, Muller, Theosophy and Vivekananda are in the story). Paul Hacker named the Hindu habit of absorbing other religions into one's own scheme inclusivism; Halbfass shows Neo-Vedanta speaking in this key; Edward Said, Orientalism (1978); Wilfred Cantwell Smith, The Meaning and End of Religion (1962), "religion" as a countable thing is a recent concept; Alan Race, Christians and Religious Pluralism (1983), the standard map (exclusivist, inclusivist, pluralist). "All religions are one" is an old Hindu-modernist line with a name and a history; Alex's "witnesses, not authorities" avoids it. A late history of a concept does not show its contents false. If a reader asks "isn't this orientalism?", the essay already answers by quoting Western, Jewish and Muslim sources too.
- Jeffrey Kripal, Roads of Excess, Palaces of Wisdom (Chicago, 2001); Authors of the Impossible (2010); Comparing Religions (Wiley-Blackwell, 2014); The Flip (2019): comparison with reflexivity, admitting the scholar's own position and taking the odd, erotic and paranormal seriously; not a classical perennialist, since he sees cultures coding experience differently, but he treats the humanities as able to speak of a deeper consciousness (a filter or transmission model of mind in the line of Myers, James, Bergson, Huxley); in The Flip he argues consciousness is basic and materialism fails on its own terms. The contemporary scholar closest to the first view in Alex's opening; most of the field does not share his stance. Against: Russell McCutcheon, Critics Not Caretakers (SUNY, 2001); Kali's Child (1995) drew sharp criticism from Hindu writers.

### 13.5 How to compare

- Jonathan Z. Smith: Imagining Religion (Chicago, 1982), especially "In Comparison a Magic Dwells" (a play on a line of Hesse's); Drudgery Divine (1990); Map Is Not Territory (1978); Relating Religion (2004). Similarity is made by the comparer. A comparison has three parts (two things and a respect) and is never a plain "A is like B"; done honestly it states the respect, includes difference and treats resemblance as a puzzle to explain. The "never dyadic, always triadic" passage is, as recalled, in Drudgery Divine around p. 51 [gist M; page and wording U]; "disciplined exaggeration" wording U; four modes of comparison (ethnographic, encyclopedic, morphological, evolutionary) U. He criticised morphological comparison by essence (Eliade, Frazer). Against: Patton and Ray (eds.), A Magic Still Dwells (California, 2000); Robert Segal. One cheap discipline: say "both say X about Y" and name X; for example both say the sense of a separate self is made and can end.
- Family resemblance and polythetic classes. Wittgenstein, Philosophical Investigations, sections 65 to 67; applied to religion by William Alston (a multiple-criteria account of "religion", 1967) and Benson Saler (Conceptualizing Religion, 1993); Rodney Needham, "Polythetic Classification: Convergence and Consequences", Man 10 (1975). Ninian Smart is related but not the usual citation here. A class defined this way needs no essence and no member needs every feature, so "these traditions share a cluster of themes" is allowed without a shared core. Against: it can be stretched to cover almost anything. For "same things in different words", "a family of ideas that recur" is the careful form; it fits the essay's own statement that rebirth is not unanimous.
- Defenders who compare carefully [M]. Raimon Panikkar, The Intrareligious Dialogue (1978): diatopical hermeneutics and homeomorphic equivalents. Francis X. Clooney, Comparative Theology (2010): read a few texts of another tradition slowly and let them change your own reading. Ann Taves, Religious Experience Reconsidered (Princeton, 2009): break "religious experience" into building blocks and attributions. Oliver Freiberger, Considering Comparison (Oxford, 2019): make the comparative category explicit [detail U]. Ninian Smart: compare along dimensions. Bernard McGinn (a model of care for Christian mysticism). Huston Smith, "Is There a Perennial Philosophy?" (JAAR 55, 1987): the claim concerns doctrines about levels of reality, not experiences [U]. Wilber: shared deep structures, different surface structures.
- Rules most responsible defenders share (P12 §4, a summary of the field and not a count): state the respect in which you compare; use the tradition's own term; report difference as well as match; quote inner or scholarly sources and not the popular version; do not let a similar word hide a different meaning (Smith, Loy, Griffiths).

### 13.6 A fair summary, and what a reader will ask

There is no consensus, and a reader should not be told there is. P12's impression of the field since about 1980: strong experiential perennialism is a minority position and the contextualist view that tradition shapes experience is the baseline, although strict contextualism has critics (Forman) and a quieter middle (Moore, McGinn, Smart). "Religion" and "mysticism" are treated as modern categories every comparer must handle. Theosophy and Traditionalism are studied as modern movements with histories and politics and not as bearers of ancient wisdom. A doctrinal perennialism (the inner teachings overlap) is argued by theologians and philosophers (Hick, Schmidt-Leukel, Huston Smith) and attacked by others (Griffiths, Plantinga, D'Costa). Comparison is accepted if openly stated, as discovery and not as proof [U as an impression].

Likely reader questions (P12 lead 9), with the essay's available answers: isn't this cherry-picking (say how you chose: inner layers, close scripture); isn't it circular (the essay says it tests coherence, not truth); do the traditions mean the same by illusion (the essay warns the words differ); isn't Theosophy discredited (the essay borrows the comparison, not the Masters). A line P12 suggests in his own voice: scholars still argue whether mystics share one experience, and he is not claiming that, only that the ideas, as written, overlap. Also from P12 (lead 5): James, Underhill and Muller are cited by the experiential perennialists (Stace, Huxley) and by the contextualists who criticise them (Katz, Proudfoot); the Traditionalists rejected psychologising approaches like James's. So do not say they are "cited by both camps".

## 14. Quotation and attribution traps

Merged from the trap lists in P01, P02, P03, P04, P05, P06, P07, P08, P10, P11, P12, P13, P14, P15, P16, P18, G1-3, G1-4, G2-3 and S5. Where several files raise the same trap it appears once. Everything here is from memory unless a tier says otherwise, and none of it was searched. The traps are grouped by the kind of mistake, because the same mistake tends to recur. Where a line is in quotation marks in this section, it is the popular wording under discussion, given so that it can be recognised. It is not a checked quotation.

### 14.1 The name of the movement and who said it first

- "Huxley coined the phrase perennial philosophy" is false. Steuco published De perenni philosophia in 1540, and Leibniz used the phrase in a letter to Nicolas Remond in 1714 (some give 1715; the date is [U]). [C, P01 and P08]
- "Leibniz coined it" is Huxley's own error in his Foreword. It is in the book. Do not repeat it. [M]
- The phrase has two meanings: the Catholic neo-scholastic one (Thomism) and the common-core one. Say which is meant. [M, G1-4]
- Schuon preferred "sophia perennis" to "philosophia perennis". Guenon's word is "Tradition", primordial and one. Smith's own preference for "primordial tradition" is [U]. [P02, P09, P16]
- Do not write "Huxley made it famous" as the first line about the movement. Write the founders first, then say Huxley made the phrase known to English readers. [P06]
- The Theosophical doctrine of repeated earthly lives belongs to the 1880s onward. Isis Unveiled (1877) argued against the spiritists' return of the individual. So "Guenon against Theosophy on rebirth" is also "the early Blavatsky against the later Theosophy". A sentence calling Isis Unveiled one of the main English routes to karma and reincarnation is weak in its 1877 half. [M, P12]
- Eliade's "Some Notes on Theosophia Perennis" (1979) uses theosophia in the Corbin and Boehme sense and is not a bridge to the Theosophical Society. [M, P03]

### 14.2 Lines that circulate under the wrong name, or with no source

Use none of these until the page is open.

Huxley's circle and the doors line
- "If the doors of perception were cleansed every thing would appear to man as it is, infinite" is Blake (The Marriage of Heaven and Hell). Huxley took his title from it. It is not Huxley's sentence and not Jim Morrison's. [M]
- The line "there are things known and things unknown, and in between are the doors" circulates under Huxley's name. As recalled it is a Jim Morrison line built on Huxley's title. Do not use it as Huxley's. [M for the Morrison attribution, M for the Blake source, P08 and G1-4]
- Huxley's book is an anthology. Its quotations come from the authors he chose. A line that circulates as "Huxley said" may be someone else's. [M]
- A Huxley-via-Pfeiffer Eckhart line can be spurious. Check the critical edition (Quint). [M, P07]
- "The reducing valve was written under mescaline" is wrong as put. [M, G1-4]

The one truth and the many names
- "Truth is one, the wise call it by many names" is the stock English slogan for Rigveda 1.164.46. Gandhi and Vivekananda quoted it. The verse itself is about a single being called by the names of gods. Do not quote the slogan as scripture. [M, P07 and P13]
- "He who knows one religion knows none" is Max Muller (Introduction to the Science of Religion, 1873; date [U]), his rewording of Goethe's remark about languages. [C and M, P01 and P07]
- "Plato is Moses speaking Attic Greek" belongs to Numenius, via Clement of Alexandria (Stromata I.22.150.4). One search result attached it to Pico; this is doubted and unconfirmed. [C and U, P01]
- William Jones's "common source" sentence (Third Anniversary Discourse, 1786) is about the languages Sanskrit, Greek and Latin, not about religions. [M, P01]
- James: "the mystical classics have neither birthday nor native land" is a saying James passes on ("as has been said"), not his coinage. His screen is "the filmiest of screens", often misquoted online as "the thinnest" or "the flimsiest". Which lecture holds the unanimity passage is open (XVI or XVII). [M, P11 and G1-4]
- Underhill: "Mysticism is the art of union with Reality" is, as recalled, from Practical Mysticism (1914), not the 1911 Mysticism. [M, P11 and P18]
- "Cosmic consciousness" is Bucke's term (1901). Ouspensky, Maslow and others reuse it. It is not a Sanskrit or Theosophical term. [M, P11]
- The "Over-Soul" is Emerson's term, not Blavatsky's. [M, P14]
- The Theosophical Society's motto "There is no religion higher than truth" is, as recalled, taken from the arms of the Maharajas of Benares and is not a sentence Blavatsky composed. Do not present it as her teaching. [U, P13]
- The cards image (all religions as different hands) credited to Radhakrishnan or Nehru is unsourced under both names. [U, G1-4]

Eckhart and the Christian mystics
- "The rose is without why" is Angelus Silesius (Cherubinischer Wandersmann, 1657), not Eckhart. Eckhart's own "without why" (sunder warumbe) is in the German sermon on life living from its own ground. [M, P03 and P14]
- "If the only prayer you ever say in your entire life is thank you, it will be enough" is widely credited to Eckhart. No source is known in his works. [U, P11, P18, G1-4]
- "The eye with which I see God is the eye with which God sees me" is genuine Eckhart (Sermon 12, "Qui audit me", as recalled; the number is [U]). [M, P11]
- Eckhart "asked God to rid him of God" is Sermon 52, "Beati pauperes spiritu". The wording differs by translation (Walshe has "free of God"). [M, P18]
- Check any Eckhart line against Quint, Walshe, or Colledge and McGinn, whether it came via Huxley or a quotation site. [M]
- "Christ has no body now but yours" is widely credited to Teresa of Avila. No source is known in her works. [U, P18]
